🟢 Part 1: Linux Fundamentals

Chapter 1: What is Linux? — History, Distributions & Why It Matters

Before you type a single command, let's understand what Linux is, why it was created, and how it came to power 96% of the world's servers. We'll explore the fascinating history, compare Linux against Windows and macOS, and understand why every cloud server — including anihpj/jobpost — runs on Linux. This chapter sets the foundation for everything that follows.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📖 Concepts 🎯 Foundation

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The fascinating story of how a Finnish student created Linux because he couldn't afford UNIX
  • ✅ How the GNU Project + Linux kernel combined to create a free, complete operating system
  • ✅ Key differences between Linux, Windows, and macOS — and why Linux dominates servers
  • ✅ The "Everything is a File" philosophy — the most important Linux concept
  • ✅ How to choose the right Linux distribution for your needs (Ubuntu 22.04 LTS vs Rocky Linux 9)
  • ✅ What the Linux kernel does and how user space vs kernel space works
  • ✅ Step-by-step VM setup for LFCS practice (Ubuntu + Rocky Linux)
  • ✅ The LFCS exam environment — exactly what to expect on exam day
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Operations Deployment 25% Foundation ALL DOMAINS
Understanding Linux History Helps on the LFCS

The LFCS is a performance-based exam (2 hours, ~24 tasks in a live terminal). While history questions aren't directly tested, understanding why Linux works the way it does — the philosophy behind "everything is a file," the separation of kernel and user space, and the distro ecosystem — helps you reason through unfamiliar scenarios and troubleshoot faster. Key concepts to internalize:

  • Everything is a File — Devices, processes, and network sockets are all accessible as files. This explains why cat, grep, and echo work on everything.
  • Kernel vs User Space — The kernel manages hardware; user programs run in protected space. This separation is why Linux is so stable and secure.
  • Distribution Flexibility — LFCS tests BOTH Ubuntu (apt, AppArmor, ufw) and Rocky Linux (dnf, SELinux, firewalld). You must know both!

1.1 The Problem That Created Linux

To understand Linux, you need to understand why it was created. The story begins in 1991 at the University of Helsinki, where a 21-year-old Finnish student named Linus Torvalds faced a problem: he wanted to learn about operating systems, but UNIX — the dominant OS of the time — was expensive and proprietary. Students simply couldn't afford it.

📖 Key Term: Kernel

The kernel is the core program of an operating system. It talks directly to hardware (CPU, RAM, disk, network) and manages resources. Linux is technically "just" a kernel — the GNU Project provided all the tools (shell, compiler, text editors) that made it a complete OS. This is why some people insist on calling it GNU/Linux.

At the same time, Richard Stallman had been working on the GNU Project since 1984 — creating free, open-source replacements for UNIX tools (compilers, editors, shell utilities). By 1991, GNU had everything... except a working kernel. Linus's kernel filled that exact gap. The combination — Linux kernel + GNU tools = a complete, free, open-source operating system — changed computing forever.

Linux History Timeline — From UNIX to Today
  1969 ── AT&T Bell Labs creates UNIX (Thompson & Ritchie)
  1983 ── GNU Project started (Richard Stallman, MIT)
  1991 ── Linux kernel v0.01 by Linus Torvalds (10,239 lines!)
  1992 ── Linux + GNU tools = first complete free OS
  1994 ── Linux kernel 1.0 officially released
  1996 ── Tux the penguin becomes the Linux mascot
  2004 ── Ubuntu 4.10 released (first Ubuntu LTS)
  2007 ── Android launched — Linux kernel inside every phone
  2014 ── Kubernetes open-sourced by Google (runs on Linux)
  2022 ── Ubuntu 22.04 LTS "Jammy Jellyfish" ← THIS GUIDE
  Today─ 96% of top web servers + 100% of supercomputers = Linux 🐧

Linux Dominance — By the Numbers

Web Servers 96%
Top 500 Supercomputers 100%
Cloud (AWS/GCP/Azure) ~90%
Android Phones ~70%

Why Linux wins on servers: Free + Stable + SSH-able + Scriptable + No license fees

The Linux Family Tree — UNIX Descendants
  🧬 UNIX (1969)
      │
      ├──── BSD (Berkeley) ──── macOS (Darwin) ──── iOS
      │
      └──── GNU/Linux (1991) ──── DISTRIBUTIONS:
                │
                ├── 🔴 Red Hat ── CentOS, Rocky, Fedora, RHEL
                │
                ├── 🟠 Debian ─── Ubuntu 22.04 ← THIS GUIDE
                │                  ├── Linux Mint
                │                  └── Raspberry Pi OS
                │
                └── 🟢 Arch ──── Arch Linux, Manjaro
💡 Your anihpj Server Runs on Linux

Every server that runs the anihpj/jobpost Django application — whether it's on AWS EC2, Google Cloud, Azure, or a bare-metal server in a data center — runs Linux. Understanding Linux isn't optional for a sysadmin or DevOps engineer; it is the foundation of everything. The LFCS certification proves you can manage that foundation.

🌍 The Open Source Philosophy — Why It Matters for Your Career

Linux isn't just an operating system — it's the most successful example of the open source development model. Understanding this philosophy is essential for any IT professional because it shapes how modern infrastructure is built, maintained, and secured.

👁️
Transparency = Security

With proprietary software, you trust the vendor. With open source, millions of eyes review the code. Security vulnerabilities are found and fixed faster. For anihpj in production, this means the entire stack — from the kernel to Nginx to PostgreSQL — has been audited by thousands of experts worldwide.

🔧
You Can Fix It Yourself

When something breaks in proprietary software, you wait for the vendor. In Linux, you can read the source code, understand the bug, apply a patch, and recompile — all without waiting. This is why companies like Google, Facebook, and Amazon run Linux: they need that level of control at scale.

💰
No License Costs, No Vendor Lock-In

Deploying anihpj on Linux means zero per-server licensing. Need to scale from 1 to 100 servers? Just spin them up — no CALs, no per-core fees, no license audits. This is why startups and enterprises alike build on Linux.

🌐
The Standard Platform

Linux is the lingua franca of cloud computing. Docker containers run Linux. Kubernetes orchestrates Linux. AWS, GCP, and Azure all run Linux. Master Linux and you've mastered the platform that powers the entire internet.

📖 How the Linux Ecosystem is Organized

The Linux Foundation (founded 2000) employs Linus Torvalds and other key developers. It oversees Linux kernel development, provides the LFCS/CKA/CKAD certifications, and hosts hundreds of open source projects including Kubernetes, Node.js, and Hyperledger. The kernel.org website is where the official Linux kernel source code lives. Thousands of contributors from competing companies (Intel, Red Hat, Google, Samsung, Oracle) collaborate openly — this is what makes Linux unstoppable.

📜 The GPL License — Why Linux is Forever Free

Linux is released under the GNU General Public License v2 (GPLv2). This license guarantees four essential freedoms: (1) run the program for any purpose, (2) study and modify the source code, (3) redistribute copies, (4) distribute modified versions. The key provision — copyleft — means anyone who distributes modified Linux must also release their modifications under GPL. This prevents companies from taking Linux, improving it, and keeping those improvements proprietary. It's the legal mechanism that has kept Linux free and open for 30+ years.

1.2 Linux vs Windows vs macOS — What Makes Linux Different

If you're coming from Windows or macOS, Linux has fundamental differences that shape how you work. Understanding these differences is essential before you start typing commands.

📖 The 8 Key Differences

These aren't just trivia — they're the design principles that make Linux the dominant server operating system:

Feature🐧 Linux🪟 Windows🍎 macOS
CostFree (mostly)$99-200/serverFree with Mac
Source CodeOpen SourceProprietaryPartial open
Remote AdminSSH (native)RDP / WinRMSSH (native)
Case SensitiveYES ✅NO ❌Usually NO
Filesystem Root/ (unified tree)C:\, D:\ (drive letters)/ (unified tree)
Package Managerapt, dnf, etc.MS StoreHomebrew
Server Market96% of webIIS-basedRarely servers
CustomizableCompletelyLimitedModerate
anihpj runs onNative ✅WSL2 workaroundYes

The "Everything is a File" Philosophy

This is the single most important concept in Linux. Everything — hardware devices, running processes, network connections, kernel settings — is represented as a file. This means you can use the same tools (cat, grep, echo) to interact with ANYTHING.

Everything is a File — Real Examples
  /dev/sda          → your hard drive (block device file)
  /dev/null         → a black hole (discard output forever)
  /dev/random       → random number generator device
  /proc/cpuinfo     → your CPU info (virtual kernel file)
  /proc/1234/       → all info about process with PID 1234
  /proc/net/tcp     → active TCP connections (virtual file)
  /sys/class/net/   → your network interfaces as files

  This unification means: cat, grep, echo, awk work on ALL of
  these. Same tool, hardware OR process OR network OR config.
⚠️ Case Sensitivity — A Common Pitfall

In Linux, anihpj, Anihpj, and ANIHPJ are three completely different files or directories. Windows treats them as the same. This is the #1 mistake newcomers make — always double-check your case!

🔓
Open Source

Anyone can read, modify, and distribute the code. No vendor lock-in, no per-server licensing fees.

👥
Multi-User

Designed from the ground up for many users simultaneously. Each user has isolated files and processes.

📦
Package Managers

Install any software with one command: apt install nginx. No websites, no installers, no wizards.

🔌
SSH-Native

Designed for remote administration from Day 1. No GUI needed — the terminal IS the interface.

1.3 Linux Distributions — Choosing the Right One

A Linux distribution (or "distro") is a complete operating system built from: the Linux kernel + GNU tools + a package manager + default software. Think of the kernel as the engine and the distribution as the entire car — different cars (distros) can use the same engine (kernel) but offer completely different experiences.

📖 Key Term: Distribution (Distro)

A distribution = Linux kernel + software selection + package manager + configuration defaults. Ubuntu, Rocky Linux, Debian, and Arch are all distributions. They all use the Linux kernel but differ in package managers, default software, release cycles, and target audiences.

Package Manager Quick Reference

Distro FamilyPackage ManagerInstall CommandLFCS Tested?
Ubuntu / DebianAPT (dpkg)apt install nginx✅ Ubuntu 22.04
RHEL / Rocky / CentOSDNF / YUM (rpm)dnf install nginx✅ Rocky Linux 9
Arch Linuxpacmanpacman -S nginx❌
SUSE / openSUSEzypper (rpm)zypper install nginx❌
⚠️ LFCS Tests TWO Distributions!

The LFCS exam provides both Ubuntu 22.04 LTS AND Rocky Linux 9 VMs. You MUST know both package managers (apt and dnf), both firewalls (ufw and firewalld), and both MAC systems (AppArmor and SELinux). Practice on both!

Which Distribution Should You Use? — Decision Tree

🎓 I'm studying for the LFCS exam
✅ Primary VM: Ubuntu 22.04 LTS (apt)
Most LFCS tasks tested here
Huge community, 5-year support
✅ Second VM: Rocky Linux 9 (dnf)
Practice firewalld, SELinux
Enterprise RHEL compatibility
🏢 Production servers (enterprise)
Need RHEL support → RHEL 9 (paid)
Free RHEL-compatible → Rocky Linux 9
🏠 Personal laptop/desktop?
First-time Linux → Ubuntu Desktop / Linux Mint
Power user → Arch Linux / Fedora
💡 For anihpj: Ubuntu 22.04 LTS is Our Choice

We'll deploy the anihpj/jobpost Django application on Ubuntu 22.04 LTS because it's the most widely used server distro, has 5 years of free security updates, and is the primary distro tested on the LFCS exam. You'll also set up a second VM with Rocky Linux 9 for dual-distro practice.

1.4 The Linux Kernel — The Heart of the OS 🎯

The kernel is the core program that talks directly to hardware. It manages CPU time scheduling (which process runs when), RAM allocation (who gets memory), I/O (disk reads/writes), filesystem access, and networking. Without the kernel, nothing works — it's the bridge between your programs and the physical machine.

Kernel Layers — User Space vs Kernel Space
  USER SPACE (programs you run)
  ┌──────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ bash │ │  Python  │ │ nginx  │ │  anihpj (Django app) │
  └──────┘ └──────────┘ └────────┘ └──────────────────────┘
       │         │           │                │
       └─────────┴───────────┴────────────────┘
                           │
                     System Calls
                    (read, write, open, fork)
                           │
  KERNEL SPACE (protected core — Ring 0)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Process Mgmt │ Memory Mgmt │ Filesystem │  Networking  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
  HARDWARE
  ┌──────┐  ┌─────┐  ┌──────┐  ┌────────┐  ┌─────────────┐
  │ CPU  │  │ RAM │  │ Disk │  │ NIC    │  │ USB/PCI Dev │
  └──────┘  └─────┘  └──────┘  └────────┘  └─────────────┘

Essential Kernel Commands

CommandWhat It Shows / DoesLFCS Use
uname -rKernel version: 6.5.0-35-genericQuick version check
uname -aAll: version, hostname, arch, dateFull system info
uname -mArchitecture: x86_64 / aarch6432-bit vs 64-bit check
cat /proc/versionKernel version + GCC build infoDetailed version
lsmodAll loaded kernel modulesDriver inspection
modinfo <name>Module description, author, parametersModule documentation
modprobe <name>Load a kernel module (with deps)Load drivers on demand
rmmod <name>Unload a kernel moduleRemove unused drivers
sysctl -aALL kernel runtime parametersView tunable settings
sysctl -w vm.swappiness=10Set parameter (temporary)Tune without reboot
dmesg \| tail -20Last 20 kernel boot messagesHardware/driver debug
📖 Kernel Modules — No Reboot Needed

Linux can load and unload kernel modules (device drivers, filesystem support, etc.) at runtime without rebooting. This is critical for production servers — you can add hardware support or filesystem capabilities while the server keeps running. Use lsmod to see what's loaded, modprobe to load, and rmmod to unload.

🔬 Beyond Basics: Kernel Tuning for Production

As a professional sysadmin, you'll need to tune kernel parameters for specific workloads. The anihpj Django application, for example, benefits from these optimizations:

Production Kernel Tuning for anihpj (sysctl)
  # Network tuning — handle more concurrent connections
  net.core.somaxconn = 1024          # Max queued connections
  net.ipv4.tcp_tw_reuse = 1          # Reuse TIME_WAIT sockets
  net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range = 1024 65000  # More ephemeral ports

  # Memory tuning — prefer RAM over swap for database performance
  vm.swappiness = 10                  # Only swap when really needed
  vm.vfs_cache_pressure = 50         # Keep directory entries cached longer
  vm.dirty_ratio = 15                 # Start writing dirty pages at 15% RAM

  # File handles — prevent "Too many open files" errors
  fs.file-max = 200000                # System-wide max file descriptors
  fs.inotify.max_user_watches = 524288  # For file watchers (Django dev server)

  # Apply: sysctl -p /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf
  # These are PROFESSIONAL-LEVEL optimizations you'll use in production!

To practice for the LFCS, you need a real Linux environment — not a web simulator, not WSL, but an actual virtual machine running Ubuntu Server. Here's exactly how to set it up:

The LFCS exam uses a command-line-only environment. There is no GUI, no mouse, no desktop. Install Ubuntu Server (not Desktop) so you practice in the exact same environment you'll face on exam day.

🐧
Ubuntu 22.04 LTS VM

🪨

Second practice VM. Same specs as Ubuntu. Rocky uses dnf (not apt), firewalld (not ufw), and SELinux (not AppArmor). LFCS tests BOTH — you must practice on both!

  • ✅ Create VM: 2 vCPU, 4-8 GB RAM, 50 GB primary disk
  • ✅ Add a second 10 GB disk (for LVM/partition labs)

1.6 The LFCS Exam Environment — What to Expect 🎯

The LFCS is a performance-based exam — you perform real tasks in a live Linux terminal. There are no multiple-choice questions. You either make the system do what the task asks, or you don't get the points. This is both challenging and fair: real skills are what matter.

How Exam Tasks Are Graded — The Key Insight

Tasks Describe OUTCOMES, Not Commands. Grading Checks STATE.
  Task: "Create user 'alice' with UID 2001, add to group 'devs'"
  Graded by: grep alice /etc/passwd | grep 2001 → pass/fail

  Task: "Mount /dev/sdb1 at /data persistently"
  Graded by: reboot → is /data mounted? /etc/fstab entry exists?

  KEY INSIGHT: The SYSTEM STATE is what matters.
  → Any valid command that reaches the right result = credit
  → "Persistent" = must survive a reboot (check /etc/fstab)
  → Test your work: verify BEFORE moving on
💡 "Persistent" is the Most Important Word in the LFCS Exam

If a task says "persistently" or "make permanent," your change MUST survive a reboot. This usually means editing files in /etc/ — like /etc/fstab for mounts, /etc/sysctl.conf for kernel parameters, or creating systemd service files. Temporary commands (like mount without an fstab entry) will NOT earn full credit.

Chapter 1 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
What command displays the current kernel version? Run it and write down the output. Then display ALL system information including hostname, kernel version, and architecture in a single command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
uname -r          # Shows just kernel version: 6.5.0-35-generic
uname -a          # Shows ALL info: Linux ubuntu-server 6.5.0-35-generic #36~22.04.1-Ubuntu SMP x86_64 x86_64 x86_64 GNU/Linux
📖 Explanation

uname (Unix Name) prints system information. -r shows only the kernel release. -a shows everything: kernel name, hostname, kernel version, build date, architecture, and OS name. The kernel version format is major.minor.patch-build (e.g., 6.5.0-35).

Q2
You need to check which kernel modules are currently loaded on your anihpj production server. What command do you use? How would you load the ext4 module if it wasn't loaded?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
lsmod                    # List all loaded kernel modules
modprobe ext4            # Load ext4 module (with dependencies)
modinfo ext4             # Show module description and parameters
📖 Explanation

lsmod reads /proc/modules and displays all currently loaded kernel modules with their sizes and dependencies. modprobe is smarter than insmod because it automatically loads any dependent modules first. modinfo shows the module's description, author, license, and accepted parameters.

Q3
What is the difference between the Linux kernel and a Linux distribution? Give an example of each and explain why this distinction matters for the LFCS exam.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

The Linux kernel is the core program that manages hardware (CPU, RAM, disk, network). Linux distributions (Ubuntu, Rocky Linux, Debian) bundle the kernel with GNU tools, a package manager, and default software. The LFCS tests two distributions (Ubuntu 22.04 + Rocky Linux 9) because they use different package managers (apt vs dnf), firewalls (ufw vs firewalld), and security systems (AppArmor vs SELinux).

Q4
Explain the "Everything is a File" concept in Linux. Give three concrete examples of non-file entities that are accessed as files, and explain what command you'd use to read from each.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
cat /proc/cpuinfo       # CPU information as a virtual file
cat /dev/urandom        # Random number generator as a device file
cat /proc/net/tcp       # Active TCP connections as a virtual file
ls -la /dev/sda         # Hard drive as a block device file
📖 Explanation

The "Everything is a File" philosophy means Linux represents hardware devices, kernel data, process information, and network connections as files in the filesystem. This allows standard tools like cat, grep, echo, and awk to interact with anything — from reading CPU temperature to monitoring network connections — without needing special-purpose tools.

Q5
You're setting up a practice VM for LFCS. List the exact specifications and steps needed. What's the one critical choice during installation that most beginners get wrong?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

VM Specs: 2 vCPU, 4-8 GB RAM, 50 GB primary disk + 10 GB secondary disk, NAT + Host-Only network.

Critical Choice: Install Ubuntu Server (NOT Ubuntu Desktop). The LFCS exam has NO GUI — only a terminal. Practicing with a GUI creates bad habits. Also choose LVM partitioning during install and enable OpenSSH server.

Q6
On the LFCS exam, a task says "make the change persistent." What exactly does this mean, and what files/directories must you typically modify to make changes survive a reboot?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

"Persistent" means the change must survive a reboot. You must edit configuration files in /etc/:

/etc/fstab for mount points, /etc/sysctl.conf (or /etc/sysctl.d/) for kernel parameters, /etc/systemd/system/ for services, /etc/hostname for hostname, /etc/netplan/ for network config.

Q7
How does the LFCS exam grade your work? If a task says "Create user alice with UID 2001," what exactly does the grading system check?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

The grading system checks the SYSTEM STATE, not the commands you typed. For user creation: grep alice /etc/passwd | grep 2001 — if the user exists with the correct UID, you get credit. It doesn't matter whether you used useradd, adduser, or edited /etc/passwd directly — the result is what counts.

Q8
Which TWO Linux distributions are tested on the LFCS exam, and what are the key tool differences between them that you must know?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ToolUbuntu 22.04Rocky Linux 9
Package Manageraptdnf
Firewallufwfirewalld
MAC SystemAppArmorSELinux
Network Confignetplannmcli / nmtui
Q9
What is the difference between uname -r and cat /proc/version? Which gives more detailed information about how the kernel was built?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
uname -r          # 6.5.0-35-generic (just the version string)
cat /proc/version # Linux version 6.5.0-35-generic (buildd@lcy02-amd64-076)
                  # (gcc (Ubuntu 11.4.0-1ubuntu1~22.04) 11.4.0, GNU ld 2.38)
                  # ↑ Shows GCC version, build host, build date
📖 Explanation

/proc/version is a virtual file that contains the complete kernel version string including the GCC compiler version used to build it, the build hostname, and the GNU linker version. This is useful when debugging kernel module compatibility issues — modules must be compiled with the same GCC version as the kernel.

Q10
Your anihpj server's kernel parameter vm.swappiness needs to be set to 10 temporarily for testing, then made permanent. Show both the temporary and persistent commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# TEMPORARY (lost on reboot):
sysctl -w vm.swappiness=10
cat /proc/sys/vm/swappiness   # verify: should show 10

# PERSISTENT (survives reboot):
echo "vm.swappiness=10" >> /etc/sysctl.conf
# OR create a drop-in file:
echo "vm.swappiness=10" > /etc/sysctl.d/99-anihpj.conf
sysctl -p                      # apply immediately
📖 Explanation

sysctl -w changes a kernel parameter immediately but it's lost on reboot. For persistence, add the parameter to /etc/sysctl.conf or a file in /etc/sysctl.d/. Then run sysctl -p to apply all settings from the config file immediately. The drop-in directory approach (/etc/sysctl.d/) is preferred for application-specific settings as it keeps configurations organized.

Chapter 2: The Terminal — Your Primary Tool

The terminal is the most important tool in a Linux sysadmin's arsenal. 100% of the LFCS exam is command-line. If you can't navigate a terminal efficiently, you can't pass. This chapter teaches you everything from basic navigation to Bash shell mastery, keyboard shortcuts that save minutes on the exam, and tmux — the tool that keeps your sessions alive even when SSH disconnects. For your anihpj/jobpost server, the terminal is your ONLY window in.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours ⌨️ Hands-On 🎯 LFCS Essential

  • ✅ tmux & screen — terminal multiplexers that keep your sessions alive

The LFCS is a 2-hour performance exam with ~24 tasks. Every second counts. The keyboard shortcuts and navigation skills in this chapter aren't just "nice to know" — they're essential survival skills. The difference between passing and failing often comes down to how efficiently you can move around the terminal.

2.1 Why the Terminal? GUI vs CLI 🎯

If you're coming from Windows or macOS, you're used to clicking, pointing, and dragging. In the Linux server world, the GUI does not exist. Remote servers have no monitor, no mouse, no desktop environment — the terminal is your ONLY window into the machine. Here's why CLI dominates server administration:

Task🖱️ GUI Action⌨️ CLI Command
Create a directoryRight-click → New Foldermkdir /var/www/anihpj
Read a config fileDouble-click, open in editorcat /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
Restart a serviceFind in system menu...systemctl restart anihpj
Check disk usageRight-click → Propertiesdf -h
Search file contentsOpen search tool, type querygrep "ERROR" /var/log/*.log
Remote administrationVPN + RDP (heavy, slow)ssh user@anihpj-server
Automate 100 serversImpossible with GUIfor server in $(cat servers.txt); do ssh $server 'uptime'; done

Terminal Anatomy — Understanding the Prompt

Decoding Your Shell Prompt
  owner@ubuntu-server:~$  ls -la /var/www/anihpj
  │     │              │   │
  │     │              │   └─ The command you type
  │     │              └───── Shell prompt ($ = user, # = root)
  │     └──────────────────── Current directory (~ = home)
  └────────────────────────── username@hostname
🖱️ GUI — Why It Fails for Servers
  • Requires a monitor, mouse, keyboard
  • Cannot be automated or scripted
  • Slow over remote connections
  • Not reproducible — clicks aren't code
  • Servers don't even HAVE a GUI installed
⌨️ CLI — Why It Wins
  • Works over SSH from anywhere
  • Fully scriptable and automatable
  • Lightning-fast over any connection
  • 100% reproducible — commands are code
  • 100% of the LFCS exam is CLI-based

2.2 Bash — The Default Shell 🎯

A shell is the program that interprets and executes your typed commands. Bash (Bourne Again SHell) is the default shell on Ubuntu, Rocky Linux, and virtually every Linux distribution. Other shells exist (zsh, fish) but for the LFCS exam, Bash is what you'll use.

How the Shell Works — From Keystroke to Execution
  ⌨️ You type "ls -la /var/www/anihpj"
       │
       ▼
  🐚 Bash parses the command (splits by spaces, expands wildcards)
       │
       ▼
  ⚙️ Bash finds "ls" in PATH (/usr/bin/ls), prepares arguments
       │
       ▼
  🧠 Kernel executes /usr/bin/ls with the arguments
       │
       ▼
  📋 Output sent back to Bash → displayed in your terminal

Bash Startup Files — Loading Order

Which Files Load When?
  LOGIN SHELL (ssh user@host or tty login):
    /etc/profile  →  ~/.bash_profile  (or ~/.profile)

  INTERACTIVE NON-LOGIN (new terminal tab, typing "bash"):
    /etc/bash.bashrc  →  ~/.bashrc

  WHAT TO PUT WHERE:
    ~/.bashrc       → aliases, PS1 prompt, custom functions
    ~/.bash_profile → PATH additions, environment variables

  For anihpj work, add to ~/.bashrc:
    alias ll='ls -lah'
    alias cdw='cd /var/www/anihpj'
    export DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE=anihpj.settings.production
💡 Check Your Shell

Run echo $SHELL to see which shell you're using (should be /bin/bash). To change your default shell: chsh -s /bin/zsh. But for the LFCS exam, stick with Bash — that's what's tested.

Environment Variables — How the Shell Remembers Things 🎯

Environment variables are named values that programs and the shell use for configuration. They're critical for application deployment — Django, PostgreSQL, and Nginx all rely on them.

Essential Environment Variables for anihpj Administration
  VIEW ALL:       env | sort              → Every variable currently set
  VIEW ONE:       echo $HOME              → /home/owner
  SET TEMP:       export APP_ENV=production  → Available to child processes
  SET PERSISTENT: Add to ~/.bashrc or ~/.bash_profile

  CRITICAL VARIABLES:
    $HOME         → Your home directory (/home/owner)
    $PATH         → Where shell looks for commands (colon-separated dirs)
    $USER         → Current username
    $SHELL        → Your shell path (/bin/bash)
    $PWD          → Current working directory
    $OLDPWD       → Previous working directory
    $?            → Exit code of last command (0=success, non-zero=error)

  ANIHPJ PRODUCTION VARIABLES (set in ~/.bashrc or systemd service):
    export DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE=anihpj.settings.production
    export DATABASE_URL=postgresql://anihpj_user:pass@localhost/anihpj_db
    export PYTHONPATH=/var/www/anihpj

  ADD TO PATH:
    export PATH=$PATH:/opt/anihpj/bin     → Add custom bin directory

2.3 Essential Terminal Navigation 🎯

These are the commands you'll type hundreds of times a day as a Linux sysadmin. Master them completely — they're the foundation of everything else.

CommandWhat It DoesExample
pwdPrint Working Directory — where am I?/var/www/anihpj
lsList directory contentsls /var/log
ls -laLong format + hidden filesShows permissions, owner, size, date
ls -lhHuman-readable sizes (KB, MB, GB)4.0K instead of 4096

cd /pathChange directory (absolute)cd /var/www/anihpj
cd ..Go up one level/var/www → /var
cd ~Go to your home directory/home/owner
cd -Go back to PREVIOUS directoryToggle between two dirs!
clearClear screen (or Ctrl+L)Clean slate
historyShow last 500 commandsFind that command you forgot
!!Repeat last commandsudo !! = redo with sudo

Understanding ls -la Output

Every Field Explained
  $ ls -la /var/www/anihpj
  total 48
  drwxr-xr-x  6 anihpj www-data 4096 May 24 18:00 .
  drwxr-xr-x  3 root   root     4096 May 24 17:00 ..
  -rw-r--r--  1 anihpj anihpj   1204 May 24 18:00 manage.py
  │              │     │         │    │              └── filename
  │              │     │         │    └────────────── date/time
  │              │     │         └─────────────────── file size
  │              │     └───────────────────────────── group
  │              └─────────────────────────────────── owner
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────── permissions
💡 History Tricks for Exam Speed

!! — repeat last command. !string — repeat last command starting with "string". !123 — repeat command #123 from history. Ctrl+R — interactive reverse search. These save MINUTES during the exam!

I/O Redirection & Pipes — The Power of Connecting Commands 🎯

One of Linux's superpowers is the ability to chain commands together using pipes and redirect their input/output. This is tested heavily on the LFCS exam and is essential for real-world troubleshooting.

I/O Redirection — Complete Reference
  >  file     → Redirect stdout to file (OVERWRITES)
  >> file     → Redirect stdout to file (APPENDS)
  2> file     → Redirect stderr (errors) to file
  2>&1        → Merge stderr into stdout (send errors to same place)
  &> file     → Redirect BOTH stdout and stderr to file (Bash shorthand)
  < file      → Use file as stdin (input) for a command
  |           → PIPE: send stdout of left command to stdin of right
  |&          → Pipe BOTH stdout and stderr to next command

  ANIHPJ PRODUCTION EXAMPLES:
    # Save deployment output AND errors to a log:
    python manage.py migrate >> /var/log/anihpj/deploy.log 2>&1

    # Count running gunicorn workers:
    ps aux | grep gunicorn | grep -v grep | wc -l

    # Find which port nginx is listening on:
    ss -tulnp | grep nginx

    # Discard all error messages (send to /dev/null):
    find / -name "*.pyc" 2>/dev/null

    # Top 10 IPs hitting anihpj (from nginx access log):
    awk '{print $1}' /var/log/nginx/access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10
⚠️ > vs >> — The #1 Redirection Mistake

> file OVERWRITES the file completely — all previous content is lost. >> file APPENDS to the end. Beginners often use > when they mean >> and accidentally destroy log files or configuration data. Always double-check!

2.4 Getting Help — man, --help, info, apropos

The LFCS exam allows you to use man pages and --help. Knowing how to quickly find the information you need is a critical exam skill. You won't memorize every command option — you'll look them up.

Man Page Sections — What the Numbers Mean
  man 1 ls      →  Section 1: User commands (most common)
  man 2 open    →  Section 2: System calls (C programming)
  man 3 printf  →  Section 3: Library functions
  man 5 fstab   →  Section 5: File formats (/etc/fstab config)
  man 7 signal  →  Section 7: Overviews and special topics
  man 8 fdisk   →  Section 8: System administration commands

  💡 man 5 fstab is CRUCIAL for the LFCS exam!
     It shows the /etc/fstab format for persistent mounts.
📖
man command

Full manual page. Press q to quit, / to search, n for next match.

⚡
command --help

Quick summary of options. Faster than man for simple lookups.

🔍
man -k keyword

Search ALL man pages for a keyword. Same as apropos.

📚
/usr/share/doc/

Package-installed documentation. Additional examples and guides.

2.5 Terminal Keyboard Shortcuts 🎯

These shortcuts aren't optional — they're essential survival skills for the LFCS exam. The 2-hour time limit means every second counts. Mastering these will save you 5-10 minutes during the exam.

ShortcutWhat It DoesUse Case
Ctrl+CKill the running process immediatelyStop a hung command
Ctrl+ZSuspend (pause) the running processPause, then bg or fg
Ctrl+DEnd of input / logout from shellSame as typing "exit"
Ctrl+LClear screenSame as clear command
Ctrl+AGo to beginning of lineEdit start of long command
Ctrl+EGo to end of lineAppend to command
Ctrl+RReverse search through historyFind previous commands
Ctrl+WDelete one word backwardQuick word deletion
Ctrl+UDelete from cursor to line startClear mistyped command
Ctrl+KDelete from cursor to line endRemove rest of line
TabAuto-complete filenames/commandsUse CONSTANTLY!
↑ / ↓Previous/next command in historyRe-run recent commands
⚡ Exam Speed Tricks — These 5 Save Minutes
  • 1. Ctrl+R → Reverse search history. Type a few letters → finds the last matching command. Way faster than history | grep.
  • 2. Tab Tab → See all completions. man sys[TAB][TAB] shows every man page starting with "sys".
  • 3. !! → Repeat last command. sudo !! re-runs the last command with sudo. Classic!
  • 4. Ctrl+A then Ctrl+K → Clear entire line instantly. Faster than holding Backspace.
  • 5. Alt+. → Insert last argument from previous command. mkdir /long/path then cd Alt+. = cd /long/path.

2.6 Terminal Multiplexer — tmux & screen 🎯

Here's a scenario every sysadmin faces: you SSH into a server, start a long-running deployment, and... your WiFi drops. Without tmux, your deployment dies with your SSH connection. With tmux, the session survives on the server — you just reconnect and pick up where you left off.

tmux for anihpj — Practical Deployment Workflow
  SSH into server → start tmux → name it "deploy"

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WINDOW 1: deployment        WINDOW 2: logs            │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────┐      │
  │  │ git pull         │        │ tail -f /var/log/ │      │
  │  │ pip install -r..  │        │ nginx/error.log  │      │
  │  │ python manage.py │        │ (live log view)  │      │
  │  │ migrate          │        │                  │      │
  │  └──────────────────┘        └──────────────────┘      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  If SSH drops → tmux session SURVIVES on server!
  → Reconnect: ssh server → tmux attach -t deploy
  → See exactly where deployment left off
Command / ShortcutWhat It Does
tmux new -s nameCreate a named session
tmux lsList all sessions
tmux attach -t nameReattach to existing session
tmux kill-session -t nameKill a session
Ctrl+B dDETACH — session keeps running!
Ctrl+B %Split pane vertically
Ctrl+B "Split pane horizontally
Ctrl+B arrow keysMove between panes
Ctrl+B cCreate new window
Ctrl+B n / pNext / previous window
💡 The tmux Prefix Key: Ctrl+B

Every tmux command starts with the prefix key: Ctrl+B. Press and release Ctrl+B, THEN press the command key. For example: Ctrl+B then d = detach. Ctrl+B then % = split pane. It takes practice but becomes muscle memory quickly.

🚀 Beyond the LFCS — From Terminal User to Shell Power User

Professional sysadmins don't just use the terminal — they customize it. Master these advanced skills: I/O redirection (>, >>, 2>&1, |) to chain commands into pipelines. Shell scripting to automate repetitive tasks (covered in Chapters 10-11). Custom PS1 prompts showing git branch, exit codes, and hostname. ssh config files (~/.ssh/config) for connection shortcuts. The terminal isn't just a tool — it's your primary development environment. Invest time mastering it and you'll work 10x faster than GUI-dependent colleagues.

Chapter 2 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
You're SSH'd into the anihpj production server. What command shows you the current directory you're in? What command takes you back to the previous directory you were in?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
pwd         # Print Working Directory — shows current path
cd -        # Go back to PREVIOUS directory (toggles!)
📖 Explanation

pwd prints the absolute path of your current directory. cd - is one of the most useful but underused commands — it takes you back to wherever you were before, and running it again takes you back. Think of it as an undo for cd.

Q2
You need to find help on the /etc/fstab file format during the LFCS exam. What exact command shows you the man page? Which section number is it in?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
man 5 fstab    # Section 5 = File formats
# Inside man: press / to search, n for next match, q to quit
📖 Explanation

Man pages are organized into sections. Section 5 covers file formats and configuration files — /etc/fstab, /etc/passwd, /etc/sudoers. Knowing man 5 fstab is critical for the LFCS because many exam tasks require editing /etc/fstab for persistent mounts.

Q3
What's the difference between ~/.bashrc and ~/.bash_profile? On the anihpj server, where should you put an alias cdw='cd /var/www/anihpj'?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

~/.bashrc runs for interactive non-login shells (new terminal tabs). ~/.bash_profile runs for login shells (SSH sessions). Put aliases in ~/.bashrc. Put PATH and environment variables in ~/.bash_profile.

Q4
You typed a long find command but forgot to prefix it with sudo. It failed with "Permission denied." What's the fastest way to re-run it with sudo?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo !!     # !! = "the last command"
# This expands to: sudo find / -name "*.conf"
📖 Explanation

!! is a Bash history expansion that means "the previous command." sudo !! is the classic sysadmin combo — it re-runs whatever you just typed, but with sudo privileges. Saves retyping the entire command.

Q5
How do you search your command history for a previous command that contained "nginx"? Show both the interactive method and the command-line method.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Interactive: Ctrl+R, then type "nginx"
# (press Ctrl+R again to cycle through matches)

# Command-line:
history | grep nginx     # Find the command number
!123                     # Re-run command #123
Q6
You start a long deployment of anihpj over SSH. Your WiFi might drop. What tool do you use to ensure the deployment keeps running even if you disconnect?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
tmux new -s deploy-anihpj     # Create named session
# ... run your deployment commands ...
Ctrl+B d                        # DETACH (session keeps running!)
# ... WiFi drops, you reconnect ...
tmux attach -t deploy-anihpj   # Reattach — see where it left off
Q7
In tmux, you want to see your deployment logs on the left pane and run commands on the right pane. What keystrokes create this layout?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
Ctrl+B %          # Split pane vertically (left/right)
Ctrl+B ← / →      # Move between panes
Ctrl+B "           # Split pane horizontally (top/bottom)
Q8
What command lists ALL files (including hidden dotfiles) in /var/www/anihpj/ with human-readable sizes, sorted by modification time (newest first)?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ls -laht /var/www/anihpj/
# -l = long format, -a = all (including .hidden)
# -h = human-readable sizes, -t = sort by time (newest first)
Q9
Write a Bash alias that would be useful for an anihpj developer. Then show where you'd add it so it persists across all terminal sessions.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Add to ~/.bashrc:
alias cda='cd /var/www/anihpj'
alias manage='python /var/www/anihpj/manage.py'
alias restart-anihpj='sudo systemctl restart anihpj'

# After editing, reload:
source ~/.bashrc
Q10
You need to kill a frozen process. What's the difference between Ctrl+C and Ctrl+Z? When would you use each during LFCS troubleshooting?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
Ctrl+C  → SIGINT  — KILL the process (terminates it)
Ctrl+Z  → SIGTSTP — SUSPEND the process (pauses it)

# Ctrl+Z workflow:
Ctrl+Z          # Suspend the process
bg              # Resume it in the BACKGROUND
fg              # Bring it back to FOREGROUND
jobs            # List all suspended/background jobs
📖 Explanation

Ctrl+C sends SIGINT (interrupt signal) — the process terminates. Ctrl+Z sends SIGTSTP (terminal stop) — the process pauses but stays alive. Use Ctrl+Z when you want to temporarily pause something (like stopping a log tail to run a quick command), then resume with fg. Use Ctrl+C when you want it gone permanently.

Chapter 3: The Linux Filesystem Hierarchy — Where Everything Lives

Unlike Windows with its C:\ and D:\ drives, Linux has one unified tree starting at /. Every file, device, and process lives somewhere in this tree. Understanding the Filesystem Hierarchy Standard (FHS) means you'll always know where to find configuration files, where to put application data, and where to look when something breaks. For your anihpj/jobpost server, this is the map of everything.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~1.5 hours📁 Filesystem🎯 Foundation

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The complete Linux Filesystem Hierarchy Standard — what every top-level directory is for
  • ✅ Exactly where every component of the anihpj Django app lives on the server
  • ✅ Absolute vs relative paths — and when to use each (critical for scripts!)
  • ✅ All 7 Linux file types and how to identify them from ls -la output
  • ✅ Hidden dotfiles — what they are, why they exist, and which ones matter for anihpj
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Essential Commands 20% Storage 20%

3.1 The Filesystem Hierarchy Standard (FHS)

Every Linux system follows the Filesystem Hierarchy Standard (FHS) — a specification that defines the purpose of every top-level directory. Once you learn this map, you can navigate ANY Linux system with confidence.

The Complete Linux Filesystem Tree
  /                      ← ROOT (the beginning of everything)
  ├── bin  → /usr/bin    ← Essential user commands (ls, cp, mv)
  ├── boot               ← Bootloader, kernel images
  ├── dev                ← Device files (/dev/sda, /dev/null)
  ├── etc                ← ALL system configuration files ← KEY
  │   ├── nginx/         ← Nginx config
  │   ├── systemd/       ← Service definitions
  │   ├── ssh/           ← SSH daemon config
  │   └── passwd, fstab  ← Users, mounts
  ├── home               ← User home directories
  │   └── owner/         ← Your files are here
  ├── lib  → /usr/lib    ← Shared libraries (.so files)
  ├── media              ← Auto-mounted removable media
  ├── mnt                ← Manual mount point
  ├── opt                ← Optional/third-party software
  ├── proc               ← Virtual: process & kernel info
  ├── root               ← root user's home directory
  ├── run                ← Runtime data (PIDs, sockets)
  ├── sbin → /usr/sbin   ← System admin commands (fdisk, etc.)
  ├── srv                ← Service data (web files, FTP files)
  ├── sys                ← Virtual: kernel/hardware info
  ├── tmp                ← Temporary files (cleared on reboot)
  ├── usr                ← User programs and data (read-only)
  │   ├── bin/           ← All user commands
  │   ├── local/         ← Locally compiled software
  │   └── share/         ← Documentation, man pages
  └── var                ← Variable data (logs, databases, mail)
      ├── log/           ← System log files ← KEY for LFCS
      ├── www/           ← Web server document roots
      └── lib/           ← Application state data
⚙️
/etc

ALL configuration files. The most important directory for LFCS. Every config change goes here.

📋
/var/log

System and application logs. First place to look when troubleshooting.

🏠
/home

User home directories. Your personal files, dotfiles, and SSH keys live here.

🔧
/proc & /sys

Virtual filesystems. Kernel and process information exposed as files.

📖 No Drive Letters — Disks Mount INTO the Tree

Unlike Windows (C:\, D:\), Linux has ONE unified filesystem tree starting at /. Every disk, partition, USB drive, and network share is mounted (attached) into a directory within this single tree. For example, a second hard disk /dev/sdb1 might be mounted at /data — you access it by simply cd /data, not by switching to a "D: drive." This is why /etc/fstab (filesystem table) is so critical — it defines what gets mounted where at boot time.

3.2 Where anihpj Lives on the Server

Let's map exactly where every component of the anihpj/jobpost Django application lives on a properly configured Linux server:

anihpj File Map — Every File's Location
  /var/www/anihpj/                          → Application source code
  /var/www/anihpj/.env                      → Django SECRET_KEY, DB password (chmod 600!)
  /var/www/anihpj/jobpost/                  → The jobpost Django app
  /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj         → Nginx virtual host config
  /etc/systemd/system/anihpj.service        → Systemd service unit for Gunicorn
  /var/log/anihpj/                          → Application logs
  /etc/postgresql/                          → PostgreSQL configuration
  /home/anihpj/                             → Service user home directory
  /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock                   → Gunicorn Unix socket (runtime)
💡 Why This Matters for LFCS

When a task says "configure the anihpj service to start on boot," you know to look in /etc/systemd/system/. When it says "check the application logs," you head to /var/log/anihpj/. When it says "fix the web server config," you edit /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj. The FHS map makes every LFCS task predictable.

3.3 Absolute vs Relative Paths 🎯

Absolute vs Relative — Side by Side (Current dir: /var/www)
  ABSOLUTE PATHS (always start with /):
    cd /var/www/anihpj        ← always works, anywhere
    cat /etc/nginx/nginx.conf ← always works, anywhere
    ls /home/owner/           ← always works, anywhere

  RELATIVE PATHS (depend on current dir = /var/www):
    cd anihpj                 ← works (anihpj is IN /var/www)
    cd ../log                 ← go up to /var, then into log
    cat ./anihpj/manage.py    ← ./ = same as just anihpj/
    cd ~                      ← always goes to your home dir

  FOR SCRIPTS & CRON: ALWAYS use absolute paths!
  Cron and systemd don't know your "current directory"
⚠️ The #1 Scripting Mistake

Using relative paths in scripts and cron jobs. When a cron job runs, its working directory is /root (or the user's home), NOT where you tested the script. Always use absolute paths (/var/www/anihpj/manage.py) in scripts, cron jobs, and systemd service files.

3.4 File Types in Linux

The first character of ls -la output tells you the file type. There are 7 types you need to know:

CharacterTypeExampleDescription
-Regular filemanage.pyText, binary, image — normal files
dDirectory/var/www/A container for other files
lSymbolic linkpython → python3.11A shortcut/pointer to another file
bBlock device/dev/sdaHard disk, SSD — buffered I/O
cCharacter device/dev/nullTerminal, null device — unbuffered I/O
pNamed pipe (FIFO)Inter-process communicationData flows one direction between processes
sSocket/run/anihpj.sockNetwork or IPC endpoint

3.5 Hidden Files and Dotfiles

Files starting with a dot (.) are hidden — invisible to plain ls. Use ls -a to see them. Dotfiles store user preferences, shell config, SSH keys, and application secrets.

Critical anihpj Dotfiles on the Server
  /var/www/anihpj/.env          → Django SECRET_KEY, DB password
  /var/www/anihpj/.gitignore    → files excluded from git
  /home/anihpj/.bashrc          → service user shell config
  /home/anihpj/.ssh/            → deploy key for git pull
  /home/anihpj/.profile         → service user env vars

  IMPORTANT: .env should be chmod 600 AND owned by the app user
    chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env
    chown anihpj:anihpj /var/www/anihpj/.env
⚠️ Hiding ≠ Security

Dotfiles are hidden from ls but are still world-readable unless you explicitly set restrictive permissions. ls -la reveals them to anyone. For sensitive files like .env, always use chmod 600 (owner-only read/write).

Disk Usage Analysis — Finding What's Eating Your Space 🎯

When the anihpj server runs out of disk space at 3 AM (and it will), you need to find the culprit fast. These are the professional tools:

Disk Usage Commands — The Troubleshooting Toolkit
  df -hT              → Show ALL mounted filesystems with type, used, available
                        Look for Use% near 100% — that's your problem!

  du -sh /*            → Show size of each top-level directory (run as root)
                        Which directory is consuming the most space?

  du -ah /var/log | sort -rh | head -20
                       → Top 20 largest files/dirs under /var/log, sorted biggest first

  du -sh /var/www/anihpj/ --exclude=__pycache__
                       → Size of anihpj project, excluding Python cache

  ncdu /               → Interactive disk usage browser (apt install ncdu)
                        Navigate with arrow keys, press d to delete — beautiful!

  find / -type f -size +100M -exec ls -lh {} \; 2>/dev/null
                       → Find ALL files larger than 100MB anywhere on the system

  journalctl --disk-usage
                       → How much space are systemd journals consuming?
  journalctl --vacuum-size=500M
                       → Shrink journals to 500MB (safe cleanup!)

🔬 Beyond Basics: Inodes — How Linux Actually Stores Files

Every file in Linux has an inode (index node) — a data structure that stores metadata about the file (permissions, owner, size, timestamps, disk block locations) but NOT the filename. The filename is stored separately in directory entries that point to inodes. Understanding inodes is essential for professional-level troubleshooting:

Inodes Explained — The Key to Understanding Links and Disk Space
  Directory Entry          Inode Table              Data Blocks
  ┌──────────────┐        ┌──────────────┐        ┌──────────────┐
  │ manage.py    │───────▶│ Inode 524308 │───────▶│ print("hello")│
  │ (filename)   │        │ • owner: 1001│        │ (actual data) │
  └──────────────┘        │ • group: 33  │        └──────────────┘
                           │ • size: 1204 │
  ┌──────────────┐        │ • perms: 0644│
  │ manage_link  │───────▶│ • timestamps │
  │ (hard link!) │        │ • 8 blocks   │
  └──────────────┘        └──────────────┘
  Same inode = SAME FILE. Two names, one file.

  KEY COMMANDS:
    ls -i file         → Show inode number
    stat file          → Full inode details (size, perms, timestamps, links)
    df -i              → Inode usage (running out of inodes = can't create files!)
    find / -inum 524308 → Find all hard links to a file by inode number
💡 "No space left on device" — But df shows free space?

Check inodes! df -i shows inode usage. If you have millions of tiny files (email servers, cache directories), you can run out of inodes even with free disk space. This is a classic senior-level troubleshooting scenario.

🚀 Beyond the LFCS — Filesystem Mastery for Production

Professional sysadmins know the filesystem inside out. When anihpj's PostgreSQL database complains "disk full" but df -h shows space, you check df -i for inode exhaustion. When a deployment script fails with "permission denied," you know to trace the full path from / down, checking every directory's execute permission. When you need to recover a deleted file that's still open by a process, you look in /proc/PID/fd/. The FHS isn't just theory — it's your map for every troubleshooting scenario you'll face in production.

📊 Chapter 3 Visual Summary

🌳

⚙️
/etcALL configs live here. Most important LFCS directory.
📋
/var/logSystem + app logs. First stop when troubleshooting.
🛤️
PathsAbsolute (/var/www) for scripts. Relative (../) for interactive use.
📄
7 File TypesRegular, dir, symlink, block, char, pipe, socket — first char of ls -la.
🔒

Q1
Which directory contains ALL system configuration files? Name three critical config files you'd find there.

/etc — the configuration directory. Critical files: /etc/fstab (mounts), /etc/passwd (users), /etc/ssh/sshd_config (SSH daemon), /etc/nginx/ (web server), /etc/systemd/system/ (services).

Q2
Where would you find the anihpj application logs? What about the Nginx access logs? What about system-wide messages?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

/var/log/nginx/access.log   # Nginx access log
/var/log/syslog             # System-wide messages (Ubuntu)
/var/log/messages           # System-wide messages (Rocky)
Q3
What's the difference between an absolute path and a relative path? Why must cron jobs and scripts use absolute paths?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Absolute paths start with / and work from anywhere (/var/www/anihpj). Relative paths depend on the current directory (../anihpj). Cron jobs don't have a predictable working directory, so relative paths may resolve to the wrong location. Always use absolute paths in scripts.

Q4
What does the first character of ls -la output indicate? Identify the file type for: drwxr-xr-x, -rw-r--r--, lrwxrwxrwx, and brw-rw----.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

d = Directory, - = Regular file, l = Symbolic link, b = Block device (disk). The first character is the file type indicator.

Q5
You need to secure the anihpj .env file so only the application user can read it. What commands do you run?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env
chown anihpj:anihpj /var/www/anihpj/.env
ls -la /var/www/anihpj/.env  # verify: -rw------- 1 anihpj anihpj
Q6
What is the purpose of the /proc directory? How is it different from /etc?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

/proc is a virtual filesystem — it doesn't exist on disk. It exposes kernel and process information as files. /etc contains real configuration files stored on disk. /proc is recreated fresh on every boot.

Q7
Where is the systemd service file for anihpj's Gunicorn process located? What command enables it to start on boot?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Service file location:
/etc/systemd/system/anihpj.service

# Enable on boot:
systemctl enable anihpj.service
systemctl start anihpj.service
Q8
What's the difference between /bin, /sbin, and /usr/local/bin?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

/bin (now → /usr/bin) = essential user commands. /sbin (now → /usr/sbin) = system admin commands (require root). /usr/local/bin = locally compiled/installed software, not managed by the package manager.

Q9
You need to find which directory a process is running from. Where in /proc would you look for a process with PID 5678?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ls -la /proc/5678/cwd    # Shows the process's current working directory
cat /proc/5678/cmdline   # Shows the full command that started it
cat /proc/5678/environ   # Shows environment variables
Q10
What's wrong with using a relative path in this crontab entry? Fix it: 0 2 * * * ./backup.sh
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# WRONG — relative path depends on cron's working directory:
0 2 * * * ./backup.sh

# CORRECT — absolute path always works:
0 2 * * * /opt/scripts/backup.sh

Chapter 4: Linux Permissions — Understanding Who Can Do What 🎯

Linux is a multi-user operating system — permissions control who can read, write, and execute every single file and directory. This is arguably the most important LFCS topic because it affects everything: web servers need read access to static files, SSH keys must be private, and service users need exactly the right level of access. Getting permissions wrong on your anihpj/jobpost server means the app breaks — or worse, exposes secrets.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~2 hours🔐 Security🎯 LFCS Core

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The Linux permission model — owner, group, other — and how to decode permission strings
  • ✅ chmod mastery — both symbolic mode (u+x) and octal mode (755)
  • ✅ chown and chgrp — setting correct ownership for the anihpj web application
  • ✅ SUID, SGID, and the Sticky Bit — the three special permission bits
  • ✅ umask — understanding and configuring default permissions for new files
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Users & Groups 10% Essential Commands 20%
Permissions Are Tested in EVERY LFCS Domain

Every LFCS task that involves files — creating users, configuring services, mounting filesystems, setting up web servers — requires correct permissions. If your answer has the right config but wrong permissions, you lose points. Always run ls -la after every file operation to verify.

4.1 The Permission Model — Three Levels

Every file and directory in Linux has permissions for three levels: Owner (the user who owns it), Group (members of the file's group), and Other (everyone else). Three permission bits apply to each level: Read (r), Write (w), and Execute (x).

Permission String Decoded — Every Bit Explained
   - r w x   r - x   r - -
   │ │ │ │   │ │ │   │ │ │
   │ └─┴─┘   └─┴─┘   └─┴─┘
   │  OWNER   GROUP   OTHER
   │
   └─ File type: - = regular, d = dir, l = link

  r = Read    (4) → view file contents / list directory
  w = Write   (2) → modify file / create files in directory
  x = Execute (1) → run as program / enter directory
  - = No permission (0)

  EXAMPLE: -rwxr-xr--
    Owner  (rwx) = 7  → can read, write, execute
    Group  (r-x) = 5  → can read and execute
    Other  (r--) = 4  → can only read
    Written as octal: 754
👤
Owner (u)

The user who owns the file. Typically the creator. Only root can change ownership.

👥
Group (g)

Members of the file's group. Used for team access to shared directories.

🌍
Other (o)

Everyone else on the system. Usually the most restricted level.

🔢
Octal

r=4, w=2, x=1. Add them: rwx=7, r-x=5, r--=4. chmod 754 = rwxr-xr--.

4.2 chmod — Changing Permissions 🎯

There are two ways to use chmod: symbolic mode (letters) and octal mode (numbers). Both are tested on the LFCS exam.

chmod — Symbolic Mode vs Octal Mode
  SYMBOLIC MODE (letters — intuitive, good for single changes):
    chmod u+x script.sh       # Add execute for user (owner)
    chmod g-w file.txt        # Remove write from group
    chmod o=rx dir/           # Set other to exactly r-x
    chmod a+r *.py            # Add read for ALL (user+group+other)
    chmod ug=rwx,o=rx script  # User+group=rwx, other=rx

  OCTAL MODE (numbers — precise, set ALL bits at once):
    chmod 755 script.sh       # rwxr-xr-x (common for executables)
    chmod 644 file.txt        # rw-r--r-- (common for config files)
    chmod 600 secret.key      # rw------- (private keys/secrets)
    chmod 750 dir/            # rwxr-x--- (group access, no other)

  RECURSIVE (apply to all files/dirs under a path):
    chmod -R 755 /var/www/anihpj/   # ⚠️ makes ALL files executable!
    find /var/www -type f -exec chmod 644 {} \;  # files only
    find /var/www -type d -exec chmod 755 {} \;  # dirs only
PermissionOctalUse Case
-rw-------600Private SSH key (~/.ssh/id_rsa), .env secrets
-rw-r--r--644Config files, static HTML, source code
-rwxr-xr-x755Scripts, directories, executables
drwx------700Private directory (home dir)
drwxr-xr-x755Public-read directory (/var/www)
-rwxrwxrwx777⚠️ DANGEROUS — never use in production!
chmod for anihpj — Correct Permissions
  chmod 644 /var/www/anihpj/*.py      # source files (owner rw, others r)
  chmod 755 /var/www/anihpj/           # project directory (must be executable to cd into)
  chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env       # secrets! owner-only read/write
  chmod 700 /home/anihpj/.ssh/         # SSH directory — private
  chmod 600 /home/anihpj/.ssh/id_rsa   # private key — EXTREMELY restrictive
⚠️ 777 = "Everyone Can Do Everything" — NEVER in Production

chmod 777 gives read, write, and execute to every user on the system. It's the most common security mistake beginners make. If you're tempted to use 777 to "fix" a permission problem, you're masking the real issue. Find the correct owner/group and use the minimum permissions needed.

4.3 chown & chgrp — Changing Ownership 🎯

Setting Up anihpj Ownership Correctly
  # Static files: anihpj user owns, www-data group can read
  chown -R anihpj:www-data /var/www/anihpj/
  chmod -R 750 /var/www/anihpj/

  # .env: ONLY anihpj user can read (not even www-data!)
  chown anihpj:anihpj /var/www/anihpj/.env
  chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env

  # Verify:
  ls -la /var/www/anihpj/.env
  # → -rw------- 1 anihpj anihpj 512 May 24 /var/www/anihpj/.env

  # Add yourself to www-data group for read access:
  usermod -aG www-data owner
📖 Only Root Can Change Owner

Regular users can change the group of files they own (to a group they belong to), but only root can change the owner. If you get "Operation not permitted" from chown, you need sudo.

4.4 SUID, SGID, Sticky Bit 🎯

The Three Special Permission Bits
  SUID (Set User ID) — chmod 4xxx or chmod u+s
    File runs as its OWNER, not the caller.
    $ ls -la /usr/bin/passwd
    -rwsr-xr-x 1 root root 68208 /usr/bin/passwd
         ^── 's' = SUID: runs as root even when called by normal users

  SGID (Set Group ID) — chmod 2xxx or chmod g+s
    New files inherit the DIRECTORY'S group (not the creator's).
    Perfect for shared team directories.
    $ ls -la /projects/
    drwxr-sr-x 2 root developers 4096 /projects/anihpj/
           ^── 's' = SGID: new files auto-get 'developers' group

  Sticky Bit — chmod 1xxx or chmod +t
    Only the FILE OWNER can delete their files from the directory.
    $ ls -la /tmp
    drwxrwxrwt 12 root root 4096 /tmp
              ^── 't' = Sticky: you can't delete other people's files
💡 Find SUID Files (Security Audit)

find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null — lists all SUID binaries. SUID files are potential security risks because they run with elevated privileges. Regularly audit this list on production servers.

🔬 Beyond Basics: File Attributes — Making Files Immutable

Beyond standard permissions, Linux supports extended file attributes that add another layer of protection. The most powerful is the immutable attribute — even root cannot modify or delete an immutable file without first removing the attribute.

File Attributes — chattr & lsattr
  lsattr file              → List extended attributes on a file
  chattr +i file           → Make file IMMUTABLE (cannot modify, delete, rename, or link)
  chattr -i file           → Remove immutable attribute
  chattr +a file           → Append-only (can only add data, cannot modify existing)
  chattr +i /etc/ssh/sshd_config   → Lock SSH config from tampering
  lsattr /etc/ssh/sshd_config      → ----i---------e-- /etc/ssh/sshd_config
  # The 'i' flag means: even root cannot delete this file without chattr -i first!

  PRODUCTION SECURITY:
    chattr +i /etc/resolv.conf     → Prevent DNS hijacking by malware
    chattr +i /etc/shadow          → Prevent unauthorized password changes
    chattr +a /var/log/anihpj/app.log  → Log file: append-only (can't erase evidence!)

  ⚠️ WARNING: chattr +i makes a file truly immutable. You must chattr -i first
     before any update, including system package upgrades. Use sparingly.

🔬 Beyond Basics: Access Control Lists (ACLs) — Preview

Standard Linux permissions (owner-group-other) only allow three levels of access. What if you need to give Alice read access, Bob write access, and no one else anything? Standard permissions can't do this — but ACLs can. This is covered in detail in Chapter 45, but here's a preview so you understand what's possible beyond basic permissions:

ACLs vs Standard Permissions — The Difference
  STANDARD PERMISSIONS (only 3 levels):
    -rw-r----- 1 root developers /projects/anihpj/config.ini
    Owner(root)=rw, Group(developers)=r, Other=nothing
    ❌ Can't give Alice (in developers) write but Bob (also in developers) read-only

  WITH ACLs (per-user, per-group control):
    $ getfacl /projects/anihpj/config.ini
    user::rw-           # Owner permissions
    user:alice:rw-      # Alice gets read+write
    user:bob:r--        # Bob gets read only
    group::r--          # Group default
    mask::rw-           # Maximum for named users/groups
    other::---          # Everyone else gets nothing

    setfacl -m u:alice:rw /projects/anihpj/config.ini   # Give Alice rw
    setfacl -m u:bob:r /projects/anihpj/config.ini      # Give Bob read
    getfacl /projects/anihpj/config.ini                  # Verify ACLs

4.5 umask — Default Permission Mask 🎯

How umask Works — Subtraction from Maximum
  New file max = 666 (files are NEVER executable by default)
  New dir  max = 777

  umask = 022 (Ubuntu default):
    File: 666 - 022 = 644  (-rw-r--r--)
    Dir:  777 - 022 = 755  (drwxr-xr-x)

  umask = 027 (more restrictive):
    File: 666 - 027 = 640  (-rw-r-----)  ← group read, other nothing
    Dir:  777 - 027 = 750  (drwxr-x---)

  umask = 077 (most restrictive):
    File: 666 - 077 = 600  (-rw-------)  ← owner only
    Dir:  777 - 077 = 700  (drwx------)

  For anihpj service user: set umask 027 in ~/.bashrc
🚀 Beyond the LFCS — Permissions Mastery for Security Engineering

Professional sysadmins think about permissions before creating files, not after. For anihpj in production: the .env file is chmod 600, the Gunicorn socket is chmod 660 with group www-data, static files are chmod 644, and the source code directory is chmod 750. Every file has a least-privilege permission that allows the application to function while minimizing the attack surface. After LFCS, consider the CKS (Certified Kubernetes Security Specialist) certification — Linux permissions and security hardening are the prerequisite foundation.

📊 Chapter 4 Visual Summary

👤👥🌍
Three LevelsOwner (u), Group (g), Other (o). Every file has all three.
🔢
Octal Notationr=4, w=2, x=1. 644 = rw-r--r--, 755 = rwxr-xr-x, 600 = rw-------.
🔧
chmodSymbolic: u+x, g-w, o=rx. Octal: 755, 644, 600. Recursive: -R.
👑

⭐
Special BitsSUID(4)=run as owner, SGID(2)=inherit group, Sticky(1)=only owner deletes.

Chapter 4 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Decode this permission string: -rwxr-x---. What can the owner do? The group? Other users?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Owner: rwx (read, write, execute = 7). Group: r-x (read, execute = 5). Other: --- (no permissions = 0). Octal: 750. This is a typical permission for a script that the owner can run and edit, the group can run but not edit, and other users can't access at all.

Q2
Set the anihpj .env file so ONLY the anihpj user can read and write it. No one else should have any access. Show the command and the resulting permission string.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env
ls -la /var/www/anihpj/.env
# → -rw------- 1 anihpj anihpj 512 May 24 /var/www/anihpj/.env
Q3
What command recursively changes ownership of /var/www/anihpj/ to user 'anihpj' and group 'www-data'?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
chown -R anihpj:www-data /var/www/anihpj/
Q4
Why does /usr/bin/passwd have the SUID bit set? What would happen if it didn't?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

passwd needs to write to /etc/shadow, which is only writable by root. SUID makes passwd run as root regardless of who calls it. Without SUID, normal users couldn't change their own passwords — only root could.

Q5
Your team shares /projects/anihpj/. New files created by Alice should automatically belong to the 'developers' group. How?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
chgrp developers /projects/anihpj/
chmod g+s /projects/anihpj/        # SGID bit
# OR: chmod 2775 /projects/anihpj/
ls -la /projects/                  # Verify: drwxr-sr-x
Q6
What's the current umask on your system? If you create a new file, what permissions will it have?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
umask        # Shows current mask (e.g., 0022)
# With umask 022: new files = 644, new dirs = 755
touch testfile && ls -la testfile  # Verify
Q7
What's wrong with chmod 777 /var/www/anihpj/? Why is this dangerous?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

777 gives read, write, and execute to EVERY user on the system. Any user could modify your source code, delete files, or upload malicious scripts. This is the #1 security mistake. Instead, use the minimum permissions needed: typically 755 for directories, 644 for files, 600 for secrets.

Q8
Find all SUID binaries on the system. Why would a security auditor run this command?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null

SUID binaries run with elevated privileges and are prime targets for privilege escalation attacks. A security audit checks for unexpected or unnecessary SUID binaries.

Q9
Set the default umask for the anihpj service user to 027 so new files are NOT world-readable. Make it persistent.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
echo "umask 027" >> /home/anihpj/.bashrc
# Verify after re-login: umask shows 0027
# New files will be 640 (-rw-r-----), dirs 750 (drwxr-x---)
Q10
A user reports "Permission denied" when trying to cd /var/www/anihpj/. The directory has drwxr-xr-- permissions. What's wrong?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

The 'other' permissions are r-- (read only, no execute). For directories, execute (x) means "can enter". Without x on a directory, you cannot cd into it even if you can read its contents. Fix: chmod o+x /var/www/anihpj/ or add the user to the www-data group.

🟢 Part 2: Essential Commands

🛠️ A Day in the Life of a Linux Sysadmin — The Command Workflow

Every command in Part 2 is something you'll use daily as a professional sysadmin. Here's how they fit into a real day managing the anihpj server:

Your Daily Command Workflow
  MORNING — Check server health:
    ssh admin@anihpj-prod → uptime → df -hT → systemctl status anihpj nginx postgresql
    journalctl -u anihpj --since "1 hour ago" → tail -F /var/log/nginx/error.log

  MID-DAY — Deploy new anihpj version:
    cd /var/www/anihpj → git pull → pip install -r requirements.txt
    python manage.py migrate → python manage.py collectstatic
    sudo systemctl restart anihpj → tail -f /var/log/anihpj/app.log

  AFTERNOON — Troubleshoot 502 errors on /jobs/:
    grep "502" /var/log/nginx/access.log → journalctl -u anihpj -n 30
    ls -la /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock → ss -tulnp | grep 8000
    sudo systemctl restart anihpj → curl -I http://localhost/jobs/

  END OF DAY — Backup & security:
    sudo /scripts/backup-anihpj.sh → sudo ufw status verbose
    sudo apt update && sudo apt upgrade -y → exit

🎯 EVERY command in this workflow is an LFCS exam topic! Chapters 5-8 teach you ALL of them.

Chapter 5: File & Directory Operations — The Daily Toolkit 🎯

File operations are the bread and butter of Linux system administration. You'll create, copy, move, delete, find, and inspect files hundreds of times a day. This chapter covers every essential file operation — from basic cp and mv to the powerful find command that can locate any file on your system based on name, size, permissions, or modification time. Every example uses your anihpj/jobpost project as context.

🟢 Beginner⏱️ ~2 hours⌨️ Hands-On🎯 LFCS 20%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Create, copy, move, and delete files and directories — the fundamental operations
  • ✅ List and inspect files with ls options, stat, and file
  • ✅ Master the find command — the most powerful file search tool on Linux
  • ✅ Understand hard links vs symbolic links and when to use each
  • ✅ View file contents with cat, less, head, tail, and wc
  • ✅ Master I/O redirection and pipes — connect commands into powerful pipelines
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Essential Commands 20%

5.1 Creating, Copying, Moving, Deleting

CommandWhat It DoesExample
touch fileCreate empty file or update timestamptouch /var/www/anihpj/deploy.log
mkdir dirCreate directorymkdir /var/www/anihpj/static
mkdir -p a/b/cCreate nested directoriesmkdir -p /var/www/anihpj/media/uploads
cp src dstCopy filecp nginx.conf nginx.conf.bak
cp -r dir dstCopy directory recursivelycp -r /tmp/anihpj-v2/ /var/www/anihpj/
mv src dstMove or renamemv app.log app.log.old
rm fileDelete filerm /tmp/test.txt
rm -rf dirDelete directory + all contents⚠️ NO UNDO!
⚠️ rm -rf Has No Undo — The Most Dangerous Command

rm -rf / would delete your ENTIRE system. rm -rf * deletes everything in the current directory. Always double-check the path before pressing Enter. Professional sysadmins use ls on the target first, then replace ls with rm. Consider using trash-cli (apt install trash-cli) for a safety net.

5.2 Listing and Inspecting Files

FlagMeaningExample Output
-lLong format (detail view)shows perms, owner, size, date
-aAll files including hiddenshows .env, .bashrc etc
-hHuman-readable sizes4.0K instead of 4096
-tSort by modification timenewest files at top
-rReverse sort orderoldest first (with -t)
-RRecursiveshows entire directory tree
-SSort by file SIZElargest files first
-iShow inode number524308 manage.py
stat Command — Every Field Explained
  $ stat /var/www/anihpj/manage.py
    File: /var/www/anihpj/manage.py
    Size: 686         Blocks: 8    IO Block: 4096  regular file
  Device: fd01h/64769d  Inode: 524308  Links: 1
  Access: (0644/-rw-r--r--)  Uid: (1001/anihpj) Gid:(33/www-data)
  Access: 2026-05-24 18:00:01 ← last READ time (atime)
  Modify: 2026-05-20 14:30:45 ← last CONTENT change (mtime)
  Change: 2026-05-20 14:30:45 ← last METADATA change (ctime)

  KEY: mtime = data changed | ctime = permissions/owner changed
  LFCS Exam: find -mtime (content) vs find -ctime (metadata)

5.3 Finding Files — find, locate, which, whereis 🎯

What You Wantfind Command
All Python files in projectfind /var/www/anihpj -name "*.py"
Files modified todayfind /var/log -mtime 0
Directories onlyfind /etc -type d
Files owned by nginxfind /var/www -user www-data
Files larger than 100MBfind / -size +100M 2>/dev/null
Empty filesfind /tmp -empty
SUID binaries (security audit)find / -perm -4000 -type f
Execute on each resultfind . -name "*.log" -exec rm {} \;
💡 locate vs find — Speed Trade-off

locate filename is INSTANT because it searches a pre-built database (run updatedb to refresh). find searches the actual filesystem in real-time — slower but always current. Use locate for quick lookups, find when you need precise filters (size, permissions, time).

5.4 Links — Hard Links and Symbolic Links 🎯

Hard Link vs Symbolic Link — Illustrated
  HARD LINK: Same inode = SAME FILE
  manage.py  ──┐
               ├──▶  inode 524308  ──▶  DISK DATA
  manage_link  ──┘
  → Delete one: other still works. Data survives. Cannot cross filesystems.

  SYMBOLIC LINK: Own inode, stores a PATH
  python3  ──▶  inode 99001  ──▶  "points to /usr/bin/python3.11"
  → Delete target: symlink becomes BROKEN (dangling). Can cross filesystems.

  REAL EXAMPLE — Nginx config with symlinks:
    ln -s /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/anihpj
  (disable site: just remove the symlink, keep the real config)
PropertyHard LinkSymbolic Link
Same inode?YES ✅NO — own inode
Cross filesystem?NO ❌YES ✅
Link to directory?NO ❌YES ✅
Original deleted?Data survives ✅Link breaks ❌
ls -la shows-rw-r--r-- 2lrwxrwxrwx ... -> f

5.5 Viewing & Reading Files 🎯

ToolBest ForKey Controls
catSmall files, pipingcat file | grep pattern
lessLarge files, searching/term n q G g Space b
head -n 50First N linesDefault: 10 lines
tail -n 50Last N linesDefault: 10 lines
tail -fLIVE log monitoringCtrl+C to stop; follows new lines
tail -FLog-rotated filesReopens if file is rotated
wc -lCount lineswc -l /var/log/nginx/access.log
💡 tail -F > tail -f for Production Logs

tail -F (capital F) follows the file by NAME, not by inode. When logrotate renames app.log to app.log.1 and creates a new app.log, tail -F will follow the new file automatically. tail -f keeps reading the old (rotated) file and misses new log entries.

5.6 I/O Redirection and Pipes 🎯

Redirection Operators — Complete Reference
  > file      → stdout to file (OVERWRITES)     ⚠️ Destroys old content!
  >> file     → stdout to file (APPENDS)         Safe for logs
  2> file     → stderr to file
  2>&1        → Merge stderr into stdout
  &> file     → Both stdout+stderr to file
  < file      → Use file as stdin
  |           → PIPE: stdout of left → stdin of right

  ANIHPJ PRODUCTION EXAMPLES:
    pip install -r reqs.txt > /var/log/anihpj/install.log
    python manage.py migrate >> /var/log/anihpj/deploy.log 2>&1
    ps aux | grep gunicorn | grep -v grep | wc -l
    find / -name "*.pyc" 2>/dev/null
    awk '{print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10

📊 Chapter 5 Visual Summary

📄
CRUDtouch, mkdir, cp, mv, rm. rm -rf has NO undo — always double-check!

🔎
