🟡 Part 4: Users & Groups

👥 Anihpj Permission Blueprint — Every User, Every File

Understanding who needs access to what is the foundation of Linux security. Here's the complete user/group/resource map for the anihpj production server:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                   │
  │  👤 USERS (who)              │  📁 RESOURCES (what)               │
  │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │                              │                                    │
  │  owner (you)                 │  SSH access, sudo ALL              │
  │  UID: 1000                   │  Full system administration         │
  │                              │                                    │
  │  anihpj                      │  /var/www/anihpj/ (source code)    │
  │  UID: 1001, no shell         │  /run/anihpj/ (gunicorn socket)    │
  │  "service user"              │  Systemd: anihpj.service           │
  │                              │  Read .env (chmod 600)              │
  │                              │                                    │
  │  www-data                    │  /var/www/anihpj/static/ (read)    │
  │  UID: 33 (system)            │  /var/www/anihpj/media/ (read)     │
  │  "nginx worker"              │  /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock (r/w)    │
  │                              │                                    │
  │  postgres                    │  /var/lib/postgresql/ (data dir)   │
  │  UID: 111 (system)           │  /etc/postgresql/ (config)         │
  │  "database server"           │  Port 5432 (localhost only)        │
  │                              │                                    │
  │  root                        │  EVERYTHING (use sudo instead!)    │
  │  UID: 0                      │  ⚠️ SSH login DISABLED             │
  │                              │                                    │
  │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │                              │                                    │
  │  👥 GROUPS                   │  MEMBERS                          │
  │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  sudo (GID: 27)              │  owner                             │
  │  → run commands as root       │                                    │
  │                              │                                    │
  │  www-data (GID: 33)          │  anihpj, nginx worker              │
  │  → read web-accessible files │                                    │
  │                              │                                    │
  │  developers (GID: 2500)      │  alice, bob, owner                 │
  │  → deploy code to production │                                    │
  │                                                                   │
  │  🎯 KEY PRINCIPLE: LEAST PRIVILEGE                                │
  │  • anihpj: can run Django, CANNOT ssh or sudo                     │
  │  • www-data: can read static files, CANNOT modify source          │
  │  • postgres: can manage DB data, CANNOT access web files          │
  │  • Each user gets the MINIMUM permissions to do its job           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Chapter 12: User Accounts — Creating and Managing Users

Every process in Linux runs as a user. Every file has an owner. Understanding the Linux user model is the foundation of system security. In this chapter, you'll learn to create, modify, and delete user accounts, configure sudo access, and manage the authentication files (/etc/passwd, /etc/shadow, /etc/sudoers) — all through the lens of setting up the anihpj/jobpost service user with proper security isolation.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔐 Security

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the Linux user model: UIDs, /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow
  • ✅ Create, modify, and delete users with useradd, usermod, userdel
  • ✅ Manage passwords and account aging with passwd and chage
  • ✅ Switch users and run privileged commands with su and sudo
  • ✅ Configure fine-grained sudo access in /etc/sudoers
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Users & Groups 10%Security Cross-Domain
🎯 User Management on the LFCS Exam

User management is tested in EVERY LFCS exam. You'll be asked to create a user with specific UID, home directory, shell, and group memberships — all in one command. Common pitfalls:

  • Forgetting -m — the user gets no home directory. Always useradd -m -s /bin/bash username for regular users.
  • Forgetting -a with usermod -G — without -a, you REPLACE all supplementary groups instead of adding.
  • Editing /etc/sudoers directly instead of using visudo — a syntax error locks you out of sudo!

12.1 The Linux User Model

The Linux User Architecture — UIDs, Passwd, Shadow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    THE LINUX USER MODEL                       │
  │                                                               │
  │   ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐             │
  │   │ Process │────→│   UID    │────→│ /etc/    │  User info   │
  │   │ (PID)   │     │ (number) │     │ passwd   │  (public)    │
  │   └─────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘             │
  │                         │                                     │
  │                         │  UID ranges:                        │
  │                         │  • 0         = root (all-powerful) │
  │                         │  • 1-999     = system users        │
  │                         │  • 1000+     = regular users       │
  │                         ▼                                     │
  │                    ┌──────────┐                               │
  │                    │ /etc/    │  Password hashes              │
  │                    │ shadow   │  (root-only!)                 │
  │                    └──────────┘                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
/etc/passwd Format — Decoded Field by Field
  username : x : 1000 : 1000 : Full Name : /home/username : /bin/bash
     │       │    │      │       │              │               │
     │       │    │      │       │              └── ⑦ Login shell
     │       │    │      │       └───────────────── ⑥ Home directory
     │       │    │      └────────────────────────── ⑤ GECOS (full name, phone, etc.)
     │       │    └────────────────────────────────── ④ Primary Group ID (GID)
     │       └─────────────────────────────────────── ③ User ID (UID)
     └─────────────────────────────────────────────── ② 'x' = password in /etc/shadow
                                                    ① Username

  💡 ANIHPJ SERVICE USER:
  anihpj : x : 1001 : 1001 : anihpj Service : /var/www/anihpj : /usr/sbin/nologin
  → No shell access (nologin), isolated home, dedicated UID for file ownership
FilePurposePermissionsFields
/etc/passwdUser database — names, UIDs, shells-rw-r--r-- (world-readable)name:password:UID:GID:GECOS:home:shell
/etc/shadowPassword hashes + aging info-rw-r----- (root-only!)name:hash:lastchange:min:max:warn:inactive:expire
/etc/groupGroup memberships-rw-r--r--name:password:GID:member1,member2
/etc/shadow — The 9 Fields Decoded
  alice:$6$salt$hash...:19845:7:90:14:30:20123:
    │        │          │    │  │  │  │   │    │
    │        │          │    │  │  │  │   │    └── ⑨ Reserved (future use)
    │        │          │    │  │  │  │   └─────── ⑧ Account expiry (days since epoch)
    │        │          │    │  │  │  └─────────── ⑦ Inactivity period (days after expiry)
    │        │          │    │  │  └────────────── ⑥ Warning period (days before expiry)
    │        │          │    │  └─────────────────── ⑤ Maximum password age (days)
    │        │          │    └─────────────────────── ④ Minimum password age (days)
    │        │          └──────────────────────────── ③ Last password change (epoch days)
    │        └─────────────────────────────────────── ② Hashed password ($type$salt$hash)
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────── ① Username

  ⚠️ CRITICAL: If field ② is empty → account has NO password (anyone can log in!)
  If field ② starts with ! or !! → account is LOCKED
  $6$ = SHA-512 hash  |  $5$ = SHA-256  |  $1$ = MD5 (weak!)

12.2 useradd, usermod, userdel

CommandWhat It DoesExample
useradd usernameCreate user (minimal — no home, no shell)useradd alice
useradd -m -s /bin/bash userCreate with home dir + bash shelluseradd -m -s /bin/bash bob
useradd -u 1500 usernameCreate with specific UIDuseradd -u 1500 carol
useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin svcCreate SYSTEM user (no login, UID < 1000)useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin anihpj
usermod -aG sudo usernameAdd to supplementary groupusermod -aG sudo alice
usermod -L usernameLock account (prefix ! in shadow)usermod -L bob
usermod -U usernameUnlock accountusermod -U bob
usermod -s /usr/sbin/nologin uDisable login shellusermod -s /usr/sbin/nologin olduser
userdel usernameDelete user (home stays)userdel alice
userdel -r usernameDelete user + home + mail spooluserdel -r bob
🔧 Scenario: Create the Dedicated anihpj Service User
# Create a system user for running the Django application
sudo useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /var/www/anihpj -c "anihpj Service" anihpj

# Create the app directory with correct ownership
sudo mkdir -p /var/www/anihpj
sudo chown -R anihpj:anihpj /var/www/anihpj

# Verify the user was created correctly
id anihpj
# uid=999(anihpj) gid=999(anihpj) groups=999(anihpj)
grep anihpj /etc/passwd
# anihpj:x:999:999:anihpj Service:/var/www/anihpj:/usr/sbin/nologin

Why: The anihpj user owns the Django code and runs the gunicorn process. It has /usr/sbin/nologin — meaning nobody can log in as this user via SSH. This is least privilege in action: the user can run the app, but nothing else.

1
Create User

useradd -m -s /bin/bash -G developers alice

2
Set Password

passwd alice — set initial password. chage -d 0 alice to force change.

3
Add to Groups

usermod -aG sudo,docker alice — grant needed access.

4
Verify Setup

id alice, ls -la /home/alice, su - alice -c whoami

5
Set Policies

chage -M 90 -m 7 -W 14 alice — enforce password rotation.

📋 /etc/default/useradd — Default Settings for New Users

The file /etc/default/useradd controls what happens when you run useradd without flags. View it: cat /etc/default/useradd. Common settings: SHELL=/bin/bash (default shell), HOME=/home (base directory), CREATE_MAIL_SPOOL=yes. To change the default shell: edit this file or use useradd -D -s /bin/bash.

👀 Who's Logged In? Session Monitoring Commands
CommandWhat It ShowsUse Case
wWho's logged in + what they're running"Is anyone else on this server right now?"
whoWho's logged in (username, terminal, login time)Quick head count of active sessions
lastLogin history from /var/log/wtmp"Who logged in last night at 3 AM?"
lastbFailed login attempts from /var/log/btmp"Is someone brute-forcing our SSH?"
whoamiYour current username"Which account am I using right now?"
🔬 Deep Dive: How the Kernel Resolves UIDs

When you run ls -l, the kernel doesn't store usernames — it stores numbers (UIDs). The kernel calls getpwuid(1000) which reads /etc/passwd (or queries LDAP/NIS) to map 1000 → "alice". This is why: (1) You can have files owned by deleted users (they show the raw UID number), (2) Two users with the same UID on different systems may have different names, (3) UID 0 is root — regardless of the name. A user named "guest" with UID 0 IS root. This is why checking for UID 0 accounts is a critical security audit step.

⚠️ Advanced usermod Gotchas — Beyond -aG
MistakeWhat HappensCorrect Way
usermod -G "" aliceREMOVES all supplementary groups (empty list)Use gpasswd -d to remove one at a time
usermod -d /new/home aliceChanges home dir BUT doesn't move filesAdd -m: usermod -md /new/home alice
usermod -l newname oldnameRenames user BUT home dir stays old nameAlso use -d /home/newname -m
🛑 Emergency Access Control: The file /etc/nologin — if it exists, NO non-root user can log in. Create it with sudo touch /etc/nologin to block all logins during maintenance. Remove it to restore access. System administrators use this during critical upgrades. The message inside the file is displayed to rejected users.
🛠️ Safe File Editing: vipw and vigr

Never edit /etc/passwd or /etc/group directly with vim/nano — if two admins edit simultaneously, changes are lost. Use sudo vipw (edits /etc/passwd with locking) and sudo vigr (edits /etc/group). These commands: (1) Acquire a lock so no other admin can edit, (2) Validate syntax on save, (3) Update shadow files automatically if needed.

12.3 passwd and Account Management

CommandWhat It DoesExample
passwd usernameSet or change passwordsudo passwd alice
passwd -l usernameLock account (adds ! prefix in shadow)sudo passwd -l bob
passwd -u usernameUnlock accountsudo passwd -u bob
passwd -e usernameExpire password — force change at next loginsudo passwd -e alice
chage -l usernameView password aging infochage -l alice
chage -M 90 usernameSet max password age to 90 dayssudo chage -M 90 alice
chage -E 2026-12-31 userSet account expiry datesudo chage -E 2026-12-31 bob
chage -d 0 usernameForce password change at next loginsudo chage -d 0 alice

12.4 Switching Users — su and sudo

CommandWhat It DoesWhen to Use
su - usernameSwitch user with full login environmentDebugging as another user; need their $PATH and env
su -c "cmd" usernameRun one command as another userQuick one-off task without switching
sudo commandRun command as root (requires sudoers entry)The standard way — logged, auditable
sudo -u username commandRun command as specific userTesting: sudo -u anihpj python manage.py check
sudo -iInteractive root shell (login environment)Extended admin session
visudoSafely edit /etc/sudoersALWAYS use this — syntax errors can lock you out!
❌ su (without -) — Dangerous
su
# Keeps YOUR environment, YOUR $PATH
# Commands may not work as expected
# No audit trail of who ran what
✅ sudo — The Right Way
sudo systemctl restart anihpj
# Logged to /var/log/auth.log
# Uses root's environment safely
# Can restrict which commands each user runs

12.5 /etc/sudoers — Configuring Sudo Access

Sudoers Syntax Decoded
  WHO    HOST=(AS_WHOM)  TAG         COMMAND
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────
  alice  ALL=(ALL)                    ALL
         │    │   │                   │
         │    │   └── Run as any user │
         │    └────── On any host     └── Can run ANY command
         └────────── User "alice"

  anihpj ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/systemctl restart anihpj
         │            │                │
         │            └── No password  └── ONLY this specific command
         └── Service user

  %sudo  ALL=(ALL) ALL
  │      │    │    └── Any command
  │      │    └────── As any user
  │      └────────── On any host
  └───────────────── % = GROUP (all members of sudo group)
⚠️ Critical Rules for /etc/sudoers
  • ALWAYS use visudo to edit — it validates syntax before saving. A broken sudoers file locks you out!
  • Use /etc/sudoers.d/ for custom rules — never edit the main file directly. Create /etc/sudoers.d/anihpj
  • Never give NOPASSWD: ALL — it defeats the purpose of sudo. Restrict to specific commands.
  • Least privilege: anihpj ALL=(ALL) /usr/bin/systemctl restart anihpj — the service user can ONLY restart its own service.

📊 Chapter 12 Visual Summary

👤
User ModelUIDs: root=0, system=1-999, regular=1000+. /etc/passwd is public, /etc/shadow is private.
➕
useradd Options-m (home), -s (shell), -r (system), -u (UID). anihpj uses -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin.
🔑
passwd & chage-l/-u lock/unlock, -e expire. chage -M 90 for max age. chage -d 0 forces reset.
🔄
su vs sudosu switches user. sudo runs one command as root. visudo edits safely.
⚙️
sudoers SyntaxWHO HOST=(AS) TAGS: COMMAND. %sudo ALL=(ALL) ALL. Use /etc/sudoers.d/.

Chapter 12 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Create a system user named "anihpj" with no login shell, home at /var/www/anihpj, and a UID of 1001. Write the full command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo useradd -r -u 1001 -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /var/www/anihpj -c "anihpj Service" anihpj

Explanation: -r creates a system account (UID < 1000 unless -u overrides). -u 1001 sets explicit UID. -s /usr/sbin/nologin blocks SSH login. -d sets home directory (even though the user can't log in, the directory is needed for file ownership).

Q2
What's wrong with usermod -G sudo alice? What happens, and what's the correct command?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

usermod -G sudo alice (without -a) REPLACES all of alice's supplementary groups with just "sudo". If alice was also in "developers" and "docker" groups, she is now only in "sudo". The correct command is: sudo usermod -aG sudo alice

Q3
Lock user "bob"'s account and verify it's locked by checking /etc/shadow. Write both commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo passwd -l bob
sudo grep bob /etc/shadow
# Output: bob:!$6$...  ← the ! prefix means LOCKED

Explanation: passwd -l prepends ! to the password hash, making it impossible to match. passwd -u removes the ! to unlock.

Q4
Write a sudoers rule that allows the anihpj user to restart its own service without a password, but nothing else.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Create /etc/sudoers.d/anihpj:
anihpj ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/systemctl restart anihpj, /usr/bin/systemctl status anihpj

Explanation: This follows least privilege: the service user can only restart and check its own service. Place in /etc/sudoers.d/ (use visudo -f /etc/sudoers.d/anihpj) rather than editing the main file.

Q5
What's the difference between su - and su? Why does it matter?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

su - gives you a login shell — it loads the target user's environment ($PATH, $HOME, etc.) as if they had logged in. su (without dash) keeps YOUR environment, which can cause commands to not be found or behave unexpectedly. Always use su - or, better yet, sudo -i.

Q6
How do you force user "alice" to change her password at next login? Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo chage -d 0 alice
# OR
sudo passwd -e alice

Explanation: chage -d 0 sets the "last password change" date to epoch (Jan 1, 1970), which forces a change. passwd -e expires the password immediately. Both achieve the same result.

Q7
What are the UID ranges in Linux? Which range should the anihpj service user be in and why?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

UID ranges: 0 = root, 1-999 = system users (daemons, services), 1000+ = regular human users. The anihpj service user should be in the 1-999 range (created with useradd -r) because it's a system service account — it doesn't need to log in interactively, and isolating it from human UIDs makes auditing clearer.

Q8
Delete user "bob" completely — including his home directory and mail spool. Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo userdel -r bob

Explanation: userdel alone removes the user from /etc/passwd and /etc/shadow but leaves the home directory. -r flag removes the home directory and mail spool too. Always verify before using -r — there's no undo!

Q9
What command shows all available password aging information for user "carol"? Interpret the output.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
chage -l carol

Output interpretation: Last password change = date of last change. Password expires = when it must be changed. Password inactive = grace period after expiry before account is locked. Account expires = hard cutoff date (account becomes unusable). Minimum/Maximum days between changes.

Q10
You accidentally ran usermod -G developers alice and now alice lost sudo access. What happened and how do you fix it?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# The problem: -G without -a REPLACED all groups
# Fix: re-add sudo group (and any others she needs)
sudo usermod -aG sudo alice
# If she's still missing other groups, check with:
id alice
# Then re-add any missing ones

Explanation: The difference between -G (replace) and -aG (append) is one of the most common exam traps. There's no "undo" — you must manually re-add the lost groups.

🚀

Beyond the Exam — Enterprise Identity Management

In production, you rarely manage users one-by-one with useradd. The progression is:

Level 1: Local Users
useradd, /etc/passwd — what the LFCS tests
→
Level 2: Config Mgmt
Ansible/Puppet manage users across 100s of servers
→
Level 3: Central Auth
LDAP/FreeIPA — one user DB for all servers
→
Level 4: SSO + MFA
Keycloak, Okta — users never touch Linux passwords
→
Level 5: Zero Trust
Every access request authenticated + authorized in real-time

Chapter 13: Groups — Organizing Users

Groups are the backbone of multi-user Linux security. They let you grant permissions to collections of users instead of individuals — essential for any server with more than one person (or service) accessing files. In this chapter, you'll create groups for the anihpj/jobpost deployment, manage memberships, and use the SGID bit so that every file created in a shared directory automatically inherits the correct group ownership.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour👥 Collaboration

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand primary vs supplementary groups and how to inspect them
  • ✅ Create, rename, and delete groups with groupadd, groupmod, groupdel
  • ✅ Add and remove users from groups safely
  • ✅ Set up shared directories with SGID for automatic group inheritance
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Users & Groups 10%
🎯 Groups on the LFCS Exam

Group management is tightly coupled with file permissions. A typical exam task: "Create a shared directory where both alice and bob can write, but other users can only read." This requires group creation + usermod + chmod + SGID — all working together. The most common mistake is forgetting -a with usermod -G.

13.1 Understanding Groups

🔐
Permission at Scale

Instead of granting file access to 50 users individually, put them in one group and grant access once. One chgrp instead of 50 chown commands.

📁
Shared Workspaces

SGID directories mean every file created by any team member is automatically accessible to the whole team. No more "I can't edit your file" problems.

🔄
Role-Based Access

Groups represent roles: db-admin, web-deploy, audit-reader. Add a user to a role group and they instantly get all permissions that role needs — on every server.

📋
Auditability

groups username instantly answers "what can this person access?" — critical for security audits and compliance.

ConceptExplanationExample
Primary GroupUser's default group (usually same name as user). Files created by the user belong to this group.alice has primary group alice (GID 1000)
Supplementary GroupsExtra groups the user belongs to. Used for shared access.alice is also in sudo, developers
id usernameShow UID, GID, and ALL group membershipsuid=1000(alice) gid=1000(alice) groups=1000(alice),27(sudo),2500(developers)
groups usernameJust the group names (simpler output)alice : alice sudo developers
/etc/groupGroup database: name:password:GID:member listdevelopers:x:2500:alice,bob,owner
/etc/group Format — Decoded Field by Field
  groupname : x : 2500 : alice,bob,owner
     │         │     │         │
     │         │     │         └── ④ Member list (comma-separated usernames)
     │         │     └────────────── ③ Group ID (GID)
     │         └───────────────────── ② 'x' = password in /etc/gshadow (rarely used)
     └──────────────────────────────── ① Group name

  KEY INSIGHT: Users listed in /etc/group are SUPPLEMENTARY members.
  A user's PRIMARY group is in /etc/passwd field 4 — NOT listed here!

13.2 groupadd, groupmod, groupdel

CommandWhat It DoesExample
groupadd groupnameCreate a new groupsudo groupadd developers
groupadd -g 2500 groupnameCreate with specific GIDsudo groupadd -g 2500 developers
groupmod -n newname oldnameRename a groupsudo groupmod -n devs developers
groupdel groupnameDelete a groupsudo groupdel oldgroup
🔧 Scenario: Create the anihpj Deployment Group
# Create a group for all developers who deploy code
sudo groupadd -g 2500 developers

# Verify it was created
grep developers /etc/group
# developers:x:2500:

# Add the owner and developers to the group
sudo usermod -aG developers owner
sudo usermod -aG developers alice
sudo usermod -aG developers bob

13.3 Managing Group Membership

CommandWhat It Does⚠️ Gotcha
usermod -aG group userADD user to supplementary groupWithout -a, all other groups are REPLACED!
gpasswd -a user groupSame — add user to groupSafe alternative to usermod
gpasswd -d user groupREMOVE user from groupOnly affects this one group
newgrp groupnameSwitch active group in current shellCreates a subshell; type exit to go back
⚠️ The Most Common Exam Trap: usermod -G vs -aG

sudo usermod -G developers alice — This REPLACES all of alice's supplementary groups with just "developers". If she was in "sudo" before, she just lost sudo access! Always use sudo usermod -aG developers alice to APPEND. Think of -a as "add" and -G as "groups".

🔬 Deep Dive: Group Passwords and gpasswd

Groups can have passwords too! When set with gpasswd groupname, a user who is NOT a member can temporarily join the group by providing the password: newgrp groupname (enter password). This is rarely used today (SGID directories are preferred), but it exists on the exam. The password hash is stored in /etc/gshadow (root-only readable). To disable group passwords: gpasswd -r groupname removes the password. To set an administrator: gpasswd -A alice groupname lets alice manage membership without sudo.

🔧 Troubleshooting Group Permission Problems — A Systematic Approach

When a user can't access a file they should be able to:

  1. Check membership: groups username — are they actually in the group?
  2. Check file group: ls -l file — is the file's group what you think?
  3. Check directory: ls -ld /path/to/dir — directory permissions matter MORE than file permissions
  4. Check SGID: ls -ld /shared/dir — does the directory have the SGID bit? (permissions start with drwxr-s---)
  5. Re-login required: Group changes only take effect on NEW login. User must log out and back in, or use newgrp groupname for current session.
  6. Verify integrity: sudo grpck — checks /etc/group and /etc/gshadow for corruption

13.4 Shared Directories with Group Permissions

How nginx, Gunicorn, and Developers Share File Access
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              ANIHPJ SHARED DIRECTORY SETUP                │
  │                                                           │
  │  /var/www/anihpj/                                         │
  │  ├── Owner: owner:developers    (developers can write)    │
  │  ├── SGID bit: chmod 2775       (new files → developers)  │
  │  │                                                         │
  │  ├── static/                    (nginx needs READ only)   │
  │  │   └── Owner: owner:www-data  chmod 755                │
  │  │                                                         │
  │  ├── media/                     (app writes uploads)       │
  │  │   └── Owner: anihpj:www-data chmod 775                │
  │  │                                                         │
  │  └── .env                       (secrets — RESTRICTED)    │
  │      └── Owner: anihpj:anihpj   chmod 600                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  THE SETUP COMMANDS:
  sudo chown -R owner:developers /var/www/anihpj
  sudo chmod 2775 /var/www/anihpj          ← SGID bit!
  sudo chown -R anihpj:www-data /var/www/anihpj/media
  sudo chmod 600 /var/www/anihpj/.env        ← Secrets locked

The SGID bit (chmod 2xxx): When set on a directory, any new file or subdirectory created inside inherits the directory's GROUP ownership — not the creator's primary group. This is how multiple developers can all create files that are automatically owned by the "developers" group. Without SGID, every developer's files would be owned by their personal group, and others couldn't edit them.

📊 Chapter 13 Visual Summary

👥
Group TypesPrimary (user's default) vs Supplementary (shared access). id shows all memberships.
➕
groupadd/modsgroupadd -g 2500 devs. groupmod -n for rename. groupdel to remove.
🔗
Membershipusermod -aG (append!) or gpasswd -a/-d. NEVER use -G without -a.
📁
SGID (chmod 2775)New files inherit directory's group. Essential for shared development directories.

Chapter 13 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Create a group called "developers" with GID 2500, then add users alice and bob to it in one command each.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo groupadd -g 2500 developers
sudo usermod -aG developers alice
sudo usermod -aG developers bob
Q2
What happens if you run usermod -G developers alice (without -a)? How do you fix it?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

All of alice's supplementary groups are replaced with just "developers". To fix: sudo usermod -aG sudo,docker,othergroup alice — manually re-add every group she lost. There's no "undo" command.

Q3
How do you find out which groups user "owner" belongs to? Show two different commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
id owner
# uid=1000(owner) gid=1000(owner) groups=1000(owner),27(sudo),2500(developers)

groups owner
# owner : owner sudo developers
Q4
Set up a shared directory /var/www/anihpj so that any file created inside is automatically owned by the "developers" group. Write all commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo mkdir -p /var/www/anihpj
sudo chown owner:developers /var/www/anihpj
sudo chmod 2775 /var/www/anihpj
# Test: touch /var/www/anihpj/test.txt → ls -la shows owner:developers

Explanation: chmod 2775 sets the SGID bit (2) + owner rwx (7) + group rwx (7) + others rx (5). The SGID bit is the magic — it forces new files to inherit "developers" group.

Q5
Remove user "bob" from the "developers" group without affecting his other groups.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo gpasswd -d bob developers

Explanation: gpasswd -d only removes the specified group — it doesn't touch any other groups. This is safer than usermod for removing a single group membership.

Q6
Rename the group "developers" to "dev-team". What happens to the files owned by the old group name?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo groupmod -n dev-team developers

Files are stored with the GID (number), not the name. Since the GID stays the same (2500), all files remain correctly associated — only the display name changes. ls -l will now show "dev-team" instead of "developers".

Q7
What is the difference between a user's primary group and supplementary groups? How do you change a user's primary group?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Primary group: User's default group (stored in /etc/passwd field 4). New files are created with this group. Supplementary groups: Additional groups the user belongs to (stored in /etc/group). To change primary group: sudo usermod -g newgroup username

Q8
You created a shared directory with chmod 775 but new files still have the creator's primary group. What did you forget?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

You forgot the SGID bit. chmod 775 only sets read/write/execute permissions. You need chmod 2775 — the leading 2 is the SGID bit, which forces new files and directories to inherit the directory's group ownership.

Q9
How do you list ALL members of a specific group? Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Method 1: getent group groupname
getent group developers

# Method 2: grep the group file
grep ^developers /etc/group

# Method 3: awk for clean output
awk -F: '/^developers/ {print $4}' /etc/group

Note: getent is preferred as it queries all name service sources (files, LDAP, etc.), not just /etc/group.

Q10
Delete the group "oldproject" — but only if no files on the system are owned by that group. How do you check first?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Check if any files are owned by the group (by GID):
GID=$(getent group oldproject | cut -d: -f3)
find / -gid "$GID" -ls 2>/dev/null
# If nothing is found, safe to delete:
sudo groupdel oldproject

Explanation: find -gid searches by numeric GID. If the group still owns files, deleting it leaves orphaned GIDs — files show a number instead of a group name in ls -l.

🚀

Beyond the Exam — Group-Driven Permission Architecture

In mature organizations, groups — not users — are the primary permission boundary. Every access decision flows through group membership:

User-Level
chown alice:alice — the LFCS starting point
→
Group-Level
SGID + developers group — shared project access
→
Role-Based
Groups = roles (db-admin, web-deploy, audit-read)
→
Automated
LDAP/FreeIPA pushes group memberships to all servers

Chapter 14: Password Policies & PAM

Creating users is step one. Locking them down is where real security begins. This chapter covers password aging (force users to change passwords regularly), PAM (the pluggable authentication framework that controls HOW every login works), resource limits (prevent one user from crashing the server), and a practical security checklist for the anihpj/jobpost production environment.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour🛡️ Security

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Configure password aging policies with chage
  • ✅ Understand PAM — the authentication framework behind every login
  • ✅ Set resource limits to prevent denial-of-service from runaway processes
  • ✅ Apply account security best practices for production servers
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Users & Groups 10%Security Cross-Domain
🎯 Password Policies on the LFCS Exam

Expect to be given a policy like "Passwords must be changed every 90 days, with a 7-day minimum and 14-day warning" — and you must translate that into chage commands. The exam tests chage flags heavily: -M (max), -m (min), -W (warn), -I (inactive), -E (expire). Memorize these.

14.1 Password Aging with chage

chage OptionWhat It DoesReal Admin Scenario
chage -M 90 userMax password age: 90 daysCompliance requires password rotation every 90 days
chage -m 7 userMin age: 7 days before can change againPrevents users from cycling back to their favorite password
chage -W 14 userWarn 14 days before expiryGives users two weeks to change before it's urgent
chage -I 30 userLock account 30 days AFTER expiryGrace period — account auto-locks if they ignore warnings
chage -E 2026-12-31 userHard account expiration dateContractor access expires at end of year
chage -d 0 userForce password change at next loginNew hire gets temp password — must change immediately
chage -l userView all aging infoAudit: is this account compliant?
Password Aging Timeline (chage -M 90 -m 7 -W 14 -I 30)
  Day 0          Day 7        Day 76          Day 90        Day 120
  │              │            │               │             │
  │ Password     │ Earliest   │ Warnings      │ Password    │ Account
  │ changed      │ re-change  │ begin (-W 14) │ EXPIRES     │ LOCKED (-I 30)
  │              │ (-m 7)     │               │             │
  └──────────────┴────────────┴───────────────┴─────────────┴──────────→
                  ◄────────── 90 days maximum (-M 90) ──────────────────►
                                                                 ◄── 30 day grace ──►

14.2 PAM — Pluggable Authentication Modules

PAM is the framework that controls how users authenticate — whether via password, SSH key, fingerprint, or 2FA. Every login service (SSH, login, sudo, su) consults PAM configuration files in /etc/pam.d/.

How PAM Works — The Authentication Stack
  User tries to SSH in
       │
       ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  sshd → /etc/pam.d/sshd                     │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ auth    required   pam_unix.so        │  │ ← Check password
  │  │ auth    required   pam_faillock.so    │  │ ← Lock after 5 failures
  │  │ account required   pam_unix.so        │  │ ← Is account expired?
  │  │ session required   pam_limits.so      │  │ ← Apply resource limits
  │  │ session optional   pam_motd.so        │  │ ← Show message of the day
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
       │
       ▼
  Access GRANTED or DENIED

  KEY PAM CONTROL FLAGS:
  required   — Must succeed; continue checking other modules
  requisite  — Must succeed; STOP immediately on failure
  sufficient — If this succeeds, skip remaining modules
  optional   — Result only matters if no other module decides
⚠️ PAM Warning

Misconfiguring PAM can lock you out of your server entirely. Always keep a root shell open when testing PAM changes. Common modules: pam_unix.so (standard Unix auth), pam_pwquality.so (password complexity rules), pam_faillock.so (account lockout), pam_limits.so (resource limits).

🔧 Real-World PAM — Enforce Password Complexity with pam_pwquality
# /etc/pam.d/common-password (Ubuntu) or /etc/pam.d/system-auth (Rocky)
# Add this line to enforce strong passwords:
password  required  pam_pwquality.so  retry=3  minlen=12  \
    dcredit=-1  ucredit=-1  lcredit=-1  ocredit=-1

# What this means:
# minlen=12   → Minimum 12 characters
# dcredit=-1  → At least 1 digit
# ucredit=-1  → At least 1 uppercase letter
# lcredit=-1  → At least 1 lowercase letter
# ocredit=-1  → At least 1 special character
# retry=3     → User gets 3 attempts

This is what "must contain uppercase, lowercase, number, and symbol" actually looks like in configuration. Without PAM, you can't enforce this across all login methods (SSH, console, sudo).

🔬 Deep Dive: PAM Stack Execution Order — Why Module Order Matters

PAM processes modules top to bottom. The order is critical because of how control flags interact:

ScenarioStackResult
Allow root + password OR keyauth sufficient pam_unix.so
auth required pam_deny.so		If password succeeds → access granted (sufficient skips rest). If it fails → fall through to pam_deny (always fails) → denied.
Lock after 5 failuresauth required pam_faillock.so preauth
auth sufficient pam_unix.so
auth required pam_faillock.so authfail		preauth checks if already locked → try password → if fail, authfail records the failure. If 5 failures, account locks.
Skip password if key worksauth sufficient pam_ssh.so
auth required pam_unix.so		SSH key success = done (sufficient). Key failure = fall through to password. This is how "key OR password" login works!
🧪 How to Test PAM Safely (Without Locking Yourself Out)

Never edit PAM configs and immediately close your session! Safe testing procedure:

  1. Open a second SSH session as root — keep it ALIVE as your emergency backdoor
  2. Edit the PAM file in the first session
  3. In the second session, test: sudo pamtester sshd alice authenticate (requires apt install pamtester)
  4. If login works → PAM config is correct
  5. If login fails → use the still-open root session to fix the config
  6. NEVER close the backup root session until you've verified login works!
🔍 Viewing and Managing Failed Login Attempts with faillock
# View all failed attempts for all users:
sudo faillock

# View failed attempts for a specific user:
sudo faillock --user alice

# Reset (clear) failed attempts for a user:
sudo faillock --user alice --reset

# The faillock command reads /var/log/btmp (failed attempts log)
# This is the same data that 'lastb' shows, but per-user filtered

14.3 /etc/security/limits.conf — Resource Limits

Resource limits prevent a single user or process from consuming all system resources — essential on shared servers and for containing runaway services.

Limit TypeWhat It ControlsExample
nprocMaximum number of processesanihpj soft nproc 100
nofileMaximum open file descriptors* hard nofile 65536
memlockMaximum locked-in-memory address space (KB)postgres soft memlock unlimited
stackMaximum stack size (KB)* soft stack 8192
fsizeMaximum file size (KB) a user can create@developers hard fsize 1048576 (1GB)
🔧 Anihpj Production Limits
# /etc/security/limits.d/anihpj.conf
anihpj   soft  nproc  100     # Django workers shouldn't spawn unlimited processes
anihpj   hard  nproc  200     # Absolute cap
anihpj   soft  nofile 65536   # Gunicorn needs many file descriptors for connections
*        hard  nofile 65536   # All users get reasonable defaults

14.4 Account Security Best Practices

🔒
Disable Root SSH
/etc/ssh/sshd_config

Set PermitRootLogin no. All admin access goes through sudo — auditable and revocable.

🔑
Use SSH Keys, Not Passwords
ssh-keygen + ssh-copy-id

Keys are immune to brute-force attacks. Set PasswordAuthentication no in sshd_config.

🚫
Lock Unused Accounts
passwd -l + usermod -s nologin

Old employees, test accounts, default system accounts — lock them all.

📋
Audit Regularly
awk + /etc/passwd + /etc/shadow

awk -F: '$3>=1000{print $1,$3}' /etc/passwd — list human users. awk -F: '($2==""||$2=="!"){print $1}' /etc/shadow — find unlocked accounts with empty passwords.

Security Audit One-Liners
  # Find all human users (UID >= 1000):
  awk -F: '$3 >= 1000 {print $1, $3}' /etc/passwd

  # Check for accounts with empty passwords (DANGER!):
  sudo awk -F: '($2 == "" || $2 == "!") && $1 != "root" {print $1}' /etc/shadow

  # Find users with UID 0 (root-equivalent):
  awk -F: '$3 == 0 {print $1}' /etc/passwd

  # List all users who can sudo:
  grep -v '^#' /etc/sudoers /etc/sudoers.d/* | grep 'ALL='
❌ Before Hardening (Default Install)
  • root can SSH in with password
  • No password complexity rules
  • No account lockout after failures
  • Unlimited processes per user
  • Default 1024 file descriptors
  • Unused accounts still active
  • No password expiration
✅ After Hardening (Production Ready)
  • PermitRootLogin no + SSH keys only
  • pam_pwquality: 12 chars, upper/lower/digit/symbol
  • pam_faillock: 5 failures = 10min lockout
  • anihpj: soft 100 / hard 200 processes
  • All users: hard nofile 65536
  • Quarterly audit: awk UID≥1000 + empty passwords
  • chage -M 90 -m 7 -W 14 for all human users
🛡️ Anihpj Server Security Audit Checklist
#CheckCommandPass If
1No empty passwordssudo awk -F: '($2==""){print $1}' /etc/shadowOutput is empty
2Only one UID 0 accountawk -F: '$3==0{print $1}' /etc/passwdOnly "root"
3Root SSH disabledgrep ^PermitRootLogin /etc/ssh/sshd_configno or prohibit-password
4Password policies activegrep pam_pwquality /etc/pam.d/*minlen ≥ 12
5No human UIDs < 1000awk -F: '$3>=1000{print $1,$3}' /etc/passwdOnly expected users
6Password aging enforcedchage -l alice | grep Maximum≤ 90 days
7Limits configuredgrep -v '^#' /etc/security/limits.conf | grep -v '^$'Has nproc + nofile rules
8No world-writable home dirsfind /home -maxdepth 1 -perm -o+wOutput is empty

⏰
chage-M 90 (max), -m 7 (min), -W 14 (warn), -I 30 (inactive), -E date (expire).

Chapter 14 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Configure user "alice" so her password expires every 60 days, with a minimum of 5 days between changes and warnings starting 10 days before expiry.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo chage -M 60 -m 5 -W 10 alice
# Verify:
sudo chage -l alice
Q2
Force user "bob" to change his password at next login. Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo chage -d 0 bob
# OR: sudo passwd -e bob
Q3
What file controls resource limits for users? Set a soft limit of 100 processes and hard limit of 200 for the anihpj user.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# /etc/security/limits.conf or /etc/security/limits.d/anihpj.conf
anihpj   soft  nproc  100
anihpj   hard  nproc  200
Q4
Set an account expiry date of December 31, 2026 for user "tempworker". Explain what happens after that date.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo chage -E 2026-12-31 tempworker

After Dec 31, 2026, the account becomes completely unusable — the user cannot log in via any method (SSH, console, su). Even if the password is correct, PAM's account module rejects the login. This is different from password expiry, which still allows login to change the password.

Q5
Write an awk command to find all human users (UID >= 1000) on the system and display their username and UID.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
awk -F: '$3 >= 1000 {print $1, $3}' /etc/passwd
Q6
Write the awk command to check for accounts with empty passwords in /etc/shadow (a major security risk).
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo awk -F: '($2 == "" || $2 == "!") {print $1, "HAS NO PASSWORD!"}' /etc/shadow

Explanation: Field 2 in /etc/shadow is the password hash. An empty field means no password is set. A single ! means the account is locked (OK). An empty field on a human account is a critical security hole — anyone can log in without a password.

Q7
What PAM file controls SSH authentication? What line would you add to lock an account after 5 failed attempts?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# File: /etc/pam.d/sshd
# Add this line to the auth section:
auth required pam_faillock.so deny=5 unlock_time=600

Explanation: deny=5 locks after 5 failures. unlock_time=600 auto-unlocks after 10 minutes. This prevents brute-force SSH attacks. Always test with a separate root session open!

Q8
Set a hard limit of 65536 open files for all users. Which file do you edit and what line do you add?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# File: /etc/security/limits.conf
# Add:
*  hard  nofile  65536

# Verify with: ulimit -Hn  (shows hard limit for current shell)
# For the change to take effect, user must log out and back in
Q9
What's the difference between chage -d 0 and chage -E? Give real-world scenarios for each.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

chage -d 0 forces password change at next login (sets last change date to epoch). Use for: new hire gets temp password. chage -E 2026-12-31 sets a hard account expiry — after this date, the account cannot be used at all. Use for: contractor whose contract ends Dec 31.

Q10
You need to check which users have UID 0 (root-equivalent). Write the command and explain why this is dangerous.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
awk -F: '$3 == 0 {print $1}' /etc/passwd

Why dangerous: Any user with UID 0 has full root privileges — they bypass all permission checks. There should be exactly ONE entry: root. If you see other usernames with UID 0, it means someone created a backdoor root account. This is one of the first things attackers do after compromising a system.

🚀

Beyond the Exam — Enterprise Security

The LFCS tests local user management. In production, you graduate to centralized identity:

Local Auth
useradd, /etc/passwd — the LFCS foundation
→
Central Directory
FreeIPA/OpenLDAP — one user DB for all servers
→
Policy Engine
PAM + SSSD enforce password policies org-wide
→
MFA + SSO
Keycloak, Okta — users never see Linux passwords
🗓️ Day in the Life: How Chapters 12-14 Work Together

From onboarding a new developer to auditing for a security review — here's how User Management, Groups, and Security Policies form a single workflow on the anihpj/jobpost server:

👤 9:00
Onboard Alice
Ch 12: useradd

useradd -m -s /bin/bash -G developers alice + passwd alice + chage -d 0 alice

👥 9:05
Grant Team Access
Ch 13: usermod -aG

usermod -aG sudo alice — she's a senior dev. id alice confirms: alice sudo developers

📁 9:10
Set Up Workspace
Ch 13: SGID + chown

chown :developers /var/www/anihpj + chmod 2775 — all team files share group

🔑 9:15
Enforce Policy
Ch 14: chage

chage -M 90 -m 7 -W 14 alice — password expires every 90 days

🛡️ 9:20
Security Audit
Ch 14: awk audit

awk -F: '$3>=1000' /etc/passwd + awk -F: '$2==""' /etc/shadow — no surprises

📋 9:30
Document Access
Ch 12-14: Review

Update wiki: Alice has sudo, developers group, password rotation. Audit trail complete.

🟡 Part 5: Process & Services

🌳 Anihpj Process Tree — From systemd to Django Request

Every process on a Linux system descends from systemd (PID 1) — the grandparent of all. Understanding this tree is key to debugging, monitoring, and controlling your server:

  systemd (PID 1) — The Grandparent of ALL processes
  │
  ├── sshd (PID 450) — SSH Daemon
  │   └── sshd (PID 1234) — Your SSH session
  │       └── bash (PID 1235) — Your shell
  │           └── htop (PID 5600) — What you're looking at now!
  │
  ├── nginx (PID 500) — Master process
  │   ├── nginx (PID 501) — Worker 1
  │   ├── nginx (PID 502) — Worker 2
  │   └── nginx (PID 503) — Worker 3
  │
  ├── postgres (PID 600) — PostgreSQL master
  │   ├── postgres (PID 601) — Checkpointer
  │   ├── postgres (PID 602) — Background writer
  │   └── postgres (PID 603) — WAL writer
  │
  ├── gunicorn (PID 800) — Master (anihpj.service)
  │   ├── gunicorn (PID 801) — Worker 1 (handles HTTP)
  │   ├── gunicorn (PID 802) — Worker 2 (handles HTTP)
  │   └── gunicorn (PID 803) — Worker 3 (handles HTTP)
  │
  └── cron (PID 700) — Task Scheduler
      └── backup.sh (PID 9999) — Running nightly backup NOW

  🎯 LFCS EXAM: ps, pstree, kill, nice, top, htop
     "Given PID 800, find its children and send SIGTERM"
⚡ Linux Signals — How to Talk to Processes
SignalNameNumberWhat It DoesAnihpj Example
SIGTERMTerminate15Politely ask process to exit (can clean up & refuse). Default for kill and systemctl stop.kill 800
SIGKILLKill9Force kill — CANNOT be ignored. Kernel terminates immediately. ⚠️ Last resort.kill -9 500 (nginx stuck)
SIGHUPHangup1Reload configuration file. Services restart gracefully, preserving connections.kill -HUP $(cat /run/nginx.pid)
SIGSTOPStop19Pause (suspend) a process. Like Ctrl+Z but for any process.kill -STOP 9999 (pause backup)
SIGCONTContinue18Resume a paused process.kill -CONT 9999 (resume backup)
SIGUSR1User 110Custom action (app-defined). Gunicorn: reopen log files.kill -USR1 800 (rotate logs)

Chapter 15: Process Management — Watching and Controlling Programs

Everything running on your Linux server — from the kernel to your shell to the anihpj/jobpost Django app — is a process. This chapter teaches you to see what's running, understand its state, control it with signals, manage background jobs, and adjust priorities so critical services get the CPU they need.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours⚙️ Core Admin

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand process states (Running, Sleeping, Stopped, Zombie) and the /proc filesystem
  • ✅ Master ps, top, and htop to inspect running processes
  • ✅ Communicate with processes using signals (SIGTERM, SIGKILL, SIGHUP, SIGSTOP, SIGCONT)
  • ✅ Manage background jobs with &, bg, fg, jobs, nohup, and disown
  • ✅ Control process priority with nice and renice
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Operation of Running Systems 20%
🎯 Process Management on the LFCS Exam

You WILL be asked to find a specific process and send it a signal. Common tasks: "Find all processes owned by user anihpj and send SIGTERM" or "A process is stuck — force kill it." Key commands to have in muscle memory: ps aux | grep name, pgrep -u user, kill -9 PID, top (sort by CPU with P, by memory with M).

15.1 Understanding Processes

Process States — What You See in ps/top Output
STAT CodeStateMeaningAnihpj Example
RRunningActively using CPU or ready to runPython worker processing a request
SSleeping (interruptible)Waiting for I/O — disk, network, user inputGunicorn worker waiting for next request
DDisk Sleep (uninterruptible)Waiting for disk I/O — ⚠️ cannot be killed!Backup reading large files from slow disk
TStoppedPaused by Ctrl+Z or SIGSTOP signalYou paused a migration with Ctrl+Z
ZZombieFinished but parent hasn't collected exit statusCron job finished, cron hasn't reaped yet

The /proc filesystem is a virtual filesystem — a window into the kernel. /proc/PID/status shows process state, memory, and UID. /proc/PID/cmdline shows the exact command that started it. /proc/PID/fd/ lists open file descriptors. ls /proc | grep '^[0-9]' lists all running PIDs. This is how tools like ps and top get their data — they read /proc.

🔬 /proc Deep Dive — Other Useful Files
FileWhat It ContainsExample Use
/proc/cpuinfoCPU model, cores, speedgrep "model name" /proc/cpuinfo
/proc/meminfoTotal RAM, free, buffers, cachegrep MemTotal /proc/meminfo
/proc/loadavg1, 5, 15 min load averagescat /proc/loadavg
/proc/uptimeSeconds since boot, idle secondsCalculate uptime, CPU utilization
/proc/mountsCurrently mounted filesystemscat /proc/mounts (kernel's view)
/proc/PID/environEnvironment variables of a processcat /proc/800/environ | tr '\0' '\n'
/proc/PID/limitsResource limits for a processcat /proc/800/limits
🌳 pstree — Visualize the Process Hierarchy

The pstree command shows parent-child relationships as a text tree. pstree -p includes PIDs. pstree -u anihpj shows only anihpj's processes. pstree -s PID shows the ancestors of a specific process. This is faster than tracing with ps --ppid repeatedly and is excellent for understanding how services spawn workers.

15.2 ps — Snapshot of Running Processes

ps aux — Column-by-Column Explanation
ColumnMeaningExample
USEROwner of the processanihpj, root, www-data
PIDProcess ID (unique number)800 (gunicorn master)
%CPUCPU usage as % of one core (can exceed 100%!)350% = using 3.5 cores
%MEMPhysical RAM usage as % of total2.5% of 16GB = 400MB
VSZVirtual memory size (KB) — includes swapped, shared libsOften much larger than RSS
RSSResident Set Size (KB) — actual physical RAM usedThis is what really matters
STATProcess state — R, S, D, T, Z + flagsS = sleeping, Ss = session leader
STARTWhen the process started09:23 or Apr15
TIMETotal CPU time consumed (not wall clock!)12:45 = 12 min 45 sec of CPU
COMMANDCommand with argumentsgunicorn --workers 3
CommandWhat It ShowsWhen to Use
ps auxAll processes, BSD style (USER, PID, %CPU, %MEM, VSZ, RSS, TTY, STAT, START, TIME, COMMAND)Quick overview of everything
ps -efAll processes, System V style (UID, PID, PPID, C, STIME, TTY, TIME, CMD)Shows parent PID explicitly
ps -u anihpjProcesses owned by user anihpj"What is the anihpj service running?"
ps aux --sort=-%memAll processes sorted by memory usage (descending)"Who's eating all the RAM?"
ps -p 1234 -fFull info about specific PIDInspect a known process
ps --ppid 800Children of process 800 (Gunicorn master)"Show me all worker processes"

15.3 top, htop — Interactive Process Monitor

top — Keys You Must Know
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  top KEY    │  WHAT IT DOES                                │
  │────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  P          │  Sort by CPU usage (descending)              │
  │  M          │  Sort by memory usage (descending)           │
  │  u          │  Filter by username (type username + Enter)  │
  │  k          │  Kill a process (enter PID + signal number)  │
  │  r          │  Renice a process (change priority)          │
  │  q          │  Quit top                                    │
  │  1          │  Toggle per-CPU stats (show all cores)       │
  │  c          │  Toggle full command path                    │
  │  Shift+M    │  Sort by memory (alternative to P)           │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  LOAD AVERAGE (top right): 0.50 0.75 0.60
  │ 1-min, 5-min, 15-min averages. If > number of CPU cores,
  │ the system is overloaded — processes are waiting for CPU.
  │ A 4-core server with load > 4 = investigate immediately.
What top Actually Looks Like — Annotated Output
  top - 09:30:15 up 45 days,  2:15,  2 users,  load average: 0.50, 0.75, 0.60
  Tasks: 187 total,   1 running, 186 sleeping,   0 stopped,   0 zombie

    PID USER      PR  NI    VIRT    RES    SHR S  %CPU  %MEM     TIME+ COMMAND
    800 anihpj    20   0  452100  45200   8200 S   2.0   0.3   0:12.45 gunicorn
    801 anihpj    20   0  453200  46100   8300 S   1.5   0.3   0:11.23 gunicorn
    500 www-data  20   0  124500  12300   5500 S   0.3   0.1   0:45.12 nginx

    5600 owner     20   0   18000   6500   4200 R   1.0   0.0   0:00.05 htop  ← YOU!

  LEGEND:
  │ load average: 0.50  0.75  0.60  → 1min, 5min, 15min. All < 4 = healthy
  │ us 2.3% = user CPU  |  sy 1.1% = system  |  id 96.5% = idle (good!)
  │ wa 0.0% = I/O wait   |  st 0.0% = stolen (VM only)
  │ PR = priority  |  NI = nice value  |  VIRT = virtual  |  RES = physical

htop is a modern, color-coded alternative to top. Install with apt install htop. It shows CPU/memory bars, a process tree view (F5), mouse support, and easier killing (F9). The exam terminal will have top; htop may or may not be installed.

🔧 Real-World Scenario: Troubleshooting a Slow Anihpj Server

Situation: Users report the job board is slow. You SSH in and run top. Load average is 8.0 on a 4-core server. What now?

  1. Identify the culprit: Press P in top to sort by CPU. PostgreSQL is using 350% CPU.
  2. Check logs: journalctl -u postgresql -b -p err — reveals "too many idle connections" and a poorly optimized query.
  3. Quick fix: sudo systemctl restart postgresql — clears idle connections, CPU drops to normal.
  4. Long-term fix: Configure max_connections=100 in postgresql.conf and add connection pooling (PgBouncer).
  5. Protect anihpj: sudo renice -n -5 -u anihpj — give Django workers higher priority so they stay responsive even when the DB spikes.

Tools used: top (sort), journalctl (filter), systemctl (restart), renice (prioritize) — all from Chapters 15-17 in one troubleshooting session.

15.4 Signals — Communicating with Processes

CommandSignalUse Case
kill PID or kill -15 PIDSIGTERM (15)Graceful shutdown — give process chance to clean up
kill -9 PIDSIGKILL (9)Force kill — process cannot ignore. ⚠️ Last resort!
kill -HUP PID or kill -1 PIDSIGHUP (1)Reload configuration without restarting
kill -STOP PIDSIGSTOP (19)Pause (suspend) a process
kill -CONT PIDSIGCONT (18)Resume a paused process
killall nginxSIGTERMKill by process NAME (all matching)
pkill -u anihpjSIGTERMKill ALL processes owned by user anihpj

15.5 Background Jobs — &, bg, fg, jobs, nohup

ActionCommandWhat Happens
Run in backgroundcommand &Starts command, returns shell prompt immediately
Suspend current jobCtrl+ZPauses foreground process, returns to shell
Send to backgroundbg %1Resumes suspended job in background
Bring to foregroundfg %1Brings background job to foreground
List jobsjobsShows all background jobs in current shell
Survive logoutnohup command &Process continues after you log out
Detach permanentlydisown %1Removes job from shell's job table, can't fg it back
🔧 Scenario: Run Django Migration in Background, Survive SSH Drop
# Start the migration in background, immune to hangup:
nohup python manage.py migrate > /tmp/migrate.log 2>&1 &

# Check progress:
tail -f /tmp/migrate.log

# Even if SSH drops, migration continues.
# Reconnect and check: ps aux | grep migrate

15.6 Process Priority — nice and renice

CommandEffectExample
nice -n 10 commandStart with lower priority (10 = nice, be nice to others)nice -n 10 tar -czf backup.tar.gz /data
renice -n 5 -p PIDChange priority of running processrenice -n 19 -p 9999 (make backup lowest priority)
renice -n -5 -u anihpjChange priority for all processes of a userrenice -n -5 -u anihpj (boost Django workers)
🧠 How Nice Values Work

Nice values range from -20 (highest priority, not nice at all) to 19 (lowest priority, very nice). Default is 0. Only root can set negative nice values (higher priority). The kernel's CPU scheduler uses nice values as a weight — a process with nice 0 gets roughly 2x the CPU time of nice 10. Real-world: Give database and web server negative nice values; give backup scripts positive nice values.

💀 The OOM Killer — When Memory Runs Out

When the system runs out of RAM and swap, the kernel invokes the Out-Of-Memory (OOM) Killer. It selects a process to kill based on an "oom_score" — higher score = more likely to be killed. cat /proc/PID/oom_score shows the current score. cat /proc/PID/oom_score_adj lets you adjust it: -1000 means "never kill this" (protect critical services like sshd, postgres). 1000 means "kill this first". The OOM killer's choice is logged: dmesg | grep -i "killed process" or journalctl -k | grep oom. For anihpj: Set echo -500 > /proc/$(pgrep gunicorn | head -1)/oom_score_adj to protect Django from being killed before less important processes.

🔬 Deep Dive: VSZ vs RSS — Why Virtual Memory Is Misleading

VSZ (Virtual Size) includes: actual RAM + swapped memory + shared libraries + memory-mapped files + allocated-but-never-used memory. It can be gigabytes for a simple process because it counts shared libraries (like libc) that many processes share. RSS (Resident Set Size) is the actual physical RAM this process occupies — this is what matters for capacity planning. STAT column extra flags: Ss = sleeping session leader, Ssl = sleeping + multi-threaded, Ss+ = foreground process group. ps -eo pid,stat,comm shows just these columns.

🔍 Quick Process Lookup: pgrep and pidof
CommandWhat It Does
pgrep nginxReturns PIDs of all processes matching "nginx" — cleaner than ps aux | grep
pgrep -u anihpjAll PIDs owned by user anihpj
pgrep -f "python manage"Match against full command line (not just process name)
pidof nginxSimilar — returns PIDs by exact process name
watch -n 2 'ps aux --sort=-%cpu | head -10'Re-run the command every 2 seconds — like a poor-man's top with custom output

Chapter 15 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Find all processes owned by the anihpj user and display their PID and command. Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ps -u anihpj -o pid,comm
# OR: pgrep -lu anihpj
Q2
Nginx is stuck and won't respond to systemctl stop. Force kill it. Write the commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# First, find the PIDs:
ps aux | grep nginx
# OR: pgrep nginx

# Try graceful first:
sudo kill 500
# If still running after 5 seconds, force kill:
sudo kill -9 500 501 502 503
# OR: sudo pkill -9 nginx
Q3
You need to reload Nginx configuration without dropping any connections. What signal do you send?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo kill -HUP $(cat /run/nginx.pid)
# OR: sudo systemctl reload nginx

Explanation: SIGHUP (signal 1) tells Nginx to re-read its configuration and gracefully restart workers — old workers finish their connections, new workers pick up the updated config. Zero downtime.

Q4
Start a long-running backup script in the background, make it survive SSH logout, and verify it's still running after reconnection.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
nohup /usr/local/bin/anihpj_backup.sh > /tmp/backup.log 2>&1 &
# Note the PID: [1] 12345
# Log out, log back in, check:
ps -p 12345
tail -f /tmp/backup.log
Q5
What does a load average of 5.0 mean on a 4-core server? Is it a problem?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Load average represents the number of processes waiting for CPU + processes actively using CPU. On a 4-core server, load > 4 means processes are queuing — they're ready to run but all cores are busy. 5.0 means on average 1 process is waiting. This is a mild problem — investigate with top (sort by CPU with P) to find the culprit.

Q6
What is a zombie process? How do you find them, and how do you get rid of them?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Find zombies:
ps aux | awk '$8 == "Z" {print $2, $11}'
# OR: top (look for Z in STAT column)

# To remove: kill the PARENT process (PPID)
ps -o pid,ppid,stat,comm -p ZOMBIE_PID
kill PARENT_PID   # parent reaps the zombie

Explanation: A zombie has finished executing but its parent hasn't called wait() to collect its exit status. You can't kill a zombie directly (it's already dead). Find and restart/kill the parent process.

Q7
Write a command to lower the priority of a running backup script (PID 9999) so it doesn't starve the web server.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo renice -n 19 -p 9999
# Verify:
ps -o pid,ni,comm -p 9999
# NI should now show 19 (lowest priority)
Q8
Suspend a running process with Ctrl+Z, then resume it in the background. Write the sequence.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# While process is running in foreground:
Ctrl+Z    # Suspend (shows [1]+ Stopped)
jobs      # List: [1]+ Stopped command
bg %1     # Resume in background
# Process continues running, shell is free
Q9
What information can you find in /proc/PID/status? Show how to check a process's memory usage and UID.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# View full status:
cat /proc/800/status

# Extract specific fields:
grep -E '^(Name|Uid|VmRSS|State):' /proc/800/status
# Name: gunicorn
# State: S (sleeping)
# Uid: 1001 (anihpj)
# VmRSS: 45200 kB (actual physical RAM used)
Q10
How do you list all processes sorted by memory usage, showing only PID, memory %, and command? Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ps aux --sort=-%mem | awk '{print $2, $4, $11}' | head -20
# OR:
ps -eo pid,%mem,comm --sort=-%mem | head -20

Explanation: --sort=-%mem sorts descending by memory. -eo lets you specify exactly which columns to display — cleaner than parsing BSD output.

🚀

Beyond the Exam — Advanced Process Management

Professional sysadmins go beyond ps and top:

→
htop/atop
Color-coded, historical data, disk I/O per process
→
Prometheus
Metrics collected every 15s, graphed, alerted on
→
eBPF
Kernel-level observability — see every syscall, network packet

Chapter 16: Boot Process & systemd — Managing the OS Lifecycle

systemd is PID 1 — the first process the kernel starts, and the parent of every other process on your system. It manages services, sockets, timers, and the entire boot sequence. In this chapter, you'll learn the boot process from power-on to login, configure GRUB, master systemctl, write a custom service unit for anihpj, and understand systemd targets.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🔧 Core Admin

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the complete Linux boot sequence from BIOS to login prompt
  • ✅ Configure GRUB2 bootloader settings and recovery options
  • ✅ Master systemctl for starting, stopping, enabling, and checking services
  • ✅ Write a production systemd service unit file for anihpj/Gunicorn
  • ✅ Understand systemd targets and how they replace SysV runlevels
  • ✅ Analyze boot performance with systemd-analyze
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Operation of Running Systems 20%
🎯 systemd on the LFCS Exam

systemd is tested heavily. You'll be asked to: enable/disable services, check service status, write a simple service unit, and troubleshoot why a service won't start. The most common mistake: editing a unit file and forgetting to run systemctl daemon-reload before starting it. The exam expects you to know this.

16.1 The Linux Boot Process — From Power On to Login

The Complete Linux Boot Sequence
  ① BIOS/UEFI ──────── Hardware self-test (POST), find boot disk
       │
  ② GRUB2 ──────────── Boot menu, load kernel + initramfs
       │               /boot/grub/grub.cfg (auto-generated)
       │               /etc/default/grub (EDIT THIS ONE!)
       │
  ③ Linux Kernel ───── Initialize hardware, mount root FS
       │               Kernel messages: dmesg
       │
  ④ initramfs ──────── Temporary filesystem for boot-time tools
       │               Mount real root filesystem
       │
  ⑤ systemd (PID 1) ── Parent of ALL processes, manages services
       │               /etc/systemd/system/ for unit files
       │
  ⑥ getty / login ──── Login prompt or SSH daemon
       │
  ⑦ User session ───── Your shell, your services, anihpj runs!

16.2 GRUB2 — The Bootloader

File/CommandPurposeExample
/etc/default/grubGRUB settings — EDIT THIS, not grub.cfgGRUB_TIMEOUT=5
/boot/grub/grub.cfgAuto-generated config — DO NOT EDIT DIRECTLYRegenerated by update-grub
/etc/grub.d/Scripts that generate grub.cfgCustom entries go here
update-grub (Ubuntu)Regenerate grub.cfg after changessudo update-grub
grub2-mkconfig (RHEL)Same — regenerate grub.cfgsudo grub2-mkconfig -o /boot/grub2/grub.cfg
🔧 Recovery Mode

Hold Shift at boot (or press Esc repeatedly) to access the GRUB menu. Select "Advanced options → recovery mode" to boot into a root shell for troubleshooting. Use this when: forgot root password, filesystem won't mount, or a bad config prevents booting.

16.3 systemd — The Init System

systemd is the modern init system that replaced SysV init. Key concepts:

⚡
Parallel Startup

SysV init started services one-by-one (A→B→C). systemd starts them simultaneously based on dependencies — boots 5-10x faster on modern hardware.

🔌
Socket Activation

Services don't start until the first connection arrives. A web server can be "enabled" but consume zero resources until someone visits the site.

⏱️
Timer Units

Built-in cron replacement. Timers are .timer files that trigger .service files on a schedule. systemd handles logging, failure recovery, and dependencies automatically.

🔄
Process Tracking

systemd uses cgroups to track every process a service spawns. Kill a service and ALL its children die — no orphaned processes. SysV init couldn't do this reliably.

1️⃣
PID 1

First process started by kernel. Parent of ALL other processes. If it dies, the kernel panics.

⚡
Parallel Startup

Services start simultaneously based on dependencies — much faster than old sequential init.

📦
Units

Everything is a unit: .service, .socket, .timer, .target, .mount, .device. Consistent management.

🔗
Dependencies

Units declare what they need (Requires=, After=, Wants=). systemd resolves the order.

16.4 systemctl — Managing Services

CommandWhat It DoesExample
systemctl start nginxStart service NOWsudo systemctl start anihpj
systemctl stop nginxStop service NOWsudo systemctl stop nginx
systemctl restart nginxStop + start (brief downtime)sudo systemctl restart anihpj
systemctl reload nginxReload config WITHOUT stoppingsudo systemctl reload nginx
systemctl status nginxCurrent state + recent log linessystemctl status anihpj
systemctl enable nginxStart automatically on bootsudo systemctl enable anihpj
systemctl disable nginxDon't start on bootsudo systemctl disable anihpj
systemctl is-active nginxReturns 0 if active (for scripts)systemctl is-active --quiet anihpj && echo "up"
systemctl is-enabled nginxReturns 0 if enabled at bootsystemctl is-enabled anihpj
systemctl list-units --type=serviceAll loaded servicessystemctl list-units --type=service --state=running
systemctl daemon-reloadReload unit files after editingALWAYS run after editing a .service file!

16.5 Writing a systemd Service Unit File

Complete anihpj.service — Production Django + Gunicorn
  [Unit]
  Description=anihpj Django Application (Gunicorn)
  After=network.target postgresql.service
  Requires=postgresql.service

  [Service]
  User=anihpj
  Group=www-data
  WorkingDirectory=/var/www/anihpj
  EnvironmentFile=/var/www/anihpj/.env
  ExecStart=/var/www/anihpj/venv/bin/gunicorn \
            --workers 3 \
            --bind unix:/run/anihpj/anihpj.sock \
            anihpj.wsgi:application
  ExecReload=/bin/kill -s HUP $MAINPID
  Restart=on-failure
  RestartSec=5s

  [Install]
  WantedBy=multi-user.target
📋 Installation Steps
# 1. Create the service file
sudo vim /etc/systemd/system/anihpj.service

# 2. Reload systemd to pick up the new file
sudo systemctl daemon-reload

# 3. Enable it (start on boot)
sudo systemctl enable anihpj

# 4. Start it now
sudo systemctl start anihpj

# 5. Verify it's running
systemctl status anihpj

Key sections explained: [Unit] — metadata and dependencies. After= means "start after these" (ordering only). Requires= means "these MUST be running" (hard dependency). [Service] — how to run. ExecStart is the command. ExecReload handles systemctl reload. Restart=on-failure auto-restarts if it crashes. [Install] — which target pulls this in at boot.

16.6 systemd Targets — Runlevels Reimagined

systemd Target Dependency Map — The Boot Flow
  POWER ON
     │
     ▼
  emergency.target  ← Minimal: root shell only (runlevel 1)
     │                 Use: filesystem corruption
     ▼
  rescue.target     ← Single-user: root + basic FS (runlevel S/1)
     │                 Use: reset root password
     ▼
  multi-user.target ← TEXT MODE: all services, no GUI (runlevel 3)
     │                 • sshd, nginx, postgresql, cron
     │                 • DEFAULT FOR ANIHPJ SERVER 🎯
     │                 • systemctl set-default multi-user.target
     ▼  (optional — only if GUI installed)
  graphical.target  ← GUI MODE: multi-user + display manager (runlevel 5)

  SPECIAL TARGETS:
  poweroff.target (0)  |  reboot.target (6)  |  suspend.target
SysV Runlevelsystemd TargetPurpose
0poweroff.targetShutdown
1 / Srescue.targetSingle-user recovery
3multi-user.targetText mode (servers!)
5graphical.targetGUI desktop
6reboot.targetReboot
🔧 Key Commands

systemctl get-default — shows current default target. systemctl set-default multi-user.target — makes server boot to text mode. systemctl isolate rescue.target — switch to rescue mode NOW (existing services are stopped).

16.7 Analyzing Boot Performance

CommandWhat It Shows
systemd-analyzeTotal boot time (kernel + userspace)
systemd-analyze blameTime each service took to start — sorted slowest first
systemd-analyze critical-chainCritical path — which services blocked others from starting
systemd-analyze plot > boot.svgGenerate a visual SVG timeline chart of the entire boot sequence
🔧 Advanced systemctl: mask, list-timers, and list-sockets
CommandWhat It Does
systemctl mask servicenameCompletely prevent a service from starting — symlinks it to /dev/null. Even manually starting it fails.
systemctl unmask servicenameReverse mask — allow the service again.
systemctl list-timersShow all scheduled timer units (cron alternatives). Shows when they last ran and next run.
systemctl list-socketsShow all socket-activated services (start on first connection, not at boot).
🔧 Scenario: Speed Up Anihpj Server Boot

Run systemd-analyze blame. If you see a service taking 30 seconds that you don't need (like snapd, bluetooth, or network-manager-wait-online on a cloud server), disable it: sudo systemctl disable servicename. On the anihpj server, only essential services should start at boot: sshd, nginx, postgresql, anihpj, and cron.

🔬 Deep Dive: systemd cgroups — How Process Tracking Really Works

systemd uses Linux cgroups (control groups) to organize processes. Every service gets its own cgroup at /sys/fs/cgroup/system.slice/anihpj.service/. Inside: cgroup.procs lists all PIDs in the service. When you run systemctl stop anihpj, systemd reads this file and kills every PID — guaranteed no orphans. You can inspect live: cat /sys/fs/cgroup/system.slice/anihpj.service/cgroup.procs. This is also how resource limits (CPU, memory) are enforced — systemd writes limits into the cgroup and the kernel enforces them.

Wants= vs Requires= — The Critical Distinction
DirectiveBehavior If Dependency FailsUse Case
Wants=postgresql.serviceStarts postgresql but doesn't fail if it can't start. Your service still runs.Optional dependency — nice to have but not critical
Requires=postgresql.serviceIf postgresql fails, your service is also stopped. Hard dependency.Essential dependency — your service cannot function without it
After=postgresql.serviceOnly affects ORDERING, not dependency. Your service starts AFTER postgres.Always pair with Wants= or Requires= for correct dependency

For anihpj: Use Requires=postgresql.service + After=network.target postgresql.service. If the database is down, Django should stop too — rather than serving errors.

👤 systemd User Units — Services for Non-Root Users

systemd also manages per-user services with systemctl --user. User units live in ~/.config/systemd/user/. They start when the user logs in and stop when they log out. Useful for: user-specific timers, background sync scripts, or development servers. systemctl --user enable myservice enables it. systemctl --user status shows your personal services. Check presets: systemctl preset-all resets all services to their vendor-preset state (which services should be enabled by default).

Chapter 16 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Enable the anihpj service to start automatically at boot, then start it immediately. Write both commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo systemctl enable anihpj
sudo systemctl start anihpj
Q2
You edited /etc/systemd/system/anihpj.service. What command must you run before starting it, and why?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo systemctl daemon-reload

Explanation: systemd caches unit files in memory. daemon-reload tells systemd to re-read all unit files from disk. Without it, systemd uses the old cached version — your changes are ignored. This is the #1 reason "I edited the file but it didn't work."

Q3
Set the default boot target to multi-user.target (text mode). How do you verify it's set correctly?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo systemctl set-default multi-user.target
systemctl get-default
# Output: multi-user.target
Q4
How do you change the GRUB boot timeout from 5 seconds to 2 seconds? Write the steps.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Edit /etc/default/grub:
GRUB_TIMEOUT=2

# Regenerate grub.cfg:
sudo update-grub   # Ubuntu
# OR: sudo grub2-mkconfig -o /boot/grub2/grub.cfg  # RHEL
Q5
Find which service is slowing down your boot the most. Write the command and explain the output.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
systemd-analyze blame

The output shows each service and how long it took to start, sorted slowest first. The top entry is your bottleneck. If it's a non-essential service (bluetooth, snapd on a server), disable it to speed up boot.

Q6
Write a minimal systemd service unit for a Python script at /usr/local/bin/cleanup.sh that runs as user anihpj and restarts on failure.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
[Unit]
Description=Anihpj Cleanup Script

[Service]
Type=oneshot
User=anihpj
ExecStart=/usr/local/bin/cleanup.sh

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
Q7
Switch the system to rescue mode (single-user) for maintenance. How do you return to normal multi-user mode?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Switch to rescue mode:
sudo systemctl isolate rescue.target
# (Most services stop, you get a root shell)

# Return to normal:
sudo systemctl isolate multi-user.target
# OR simply reboot:
sudo systemctl reboot
Q8
How do you check whether a service is enabled to start at boot AND whether it's currently running? Write both commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
systemctl is-enabled anihpj   # returns "enabled" or "disabled"
systemctl is-active anihpj    # returns "active" or "inactive"
# Exit codes: 0=yes, non-zero=no — perfect for scripts
systemctl is-active --quiet anihpj && echo "running" || echo "down"
Q9
List all currently running services on the system. Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
systemctl list-units --type=service --state=running
Q10
What's the difference between systemctl restart and systemctl reload? When would you use each for nginx?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

restart = stop + start — there is a brief moment where the service is DOWN. Use for: major config changes, after software updates. reload = tell the running process to re-read its config WITHOUT stopping. Use for: adding a new virtual host, changing cache settings. Nginx handles reload gracefully — old workers finish their connections, new workers pick up the config. Zero downtime.
