Chapter 17: System Logging & Log Management

Logs tell the story of your server. They reveal why a service crashed, who logged in at 3 AM, and whether your anihpj/jobpost app is handling traffic correctly. This chapter covers where Linux stores logs, how to query them with journalctl, how to configure the traditional rsyslog daemon, and how to prevent logs from filling your disk with logrotate.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours📋 Operations

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Know where every important log file lives on the system
  • ✅ Query and filter the systemd journal with journalctl
  • ✅ Configure rsyslog for traditional syslog logging
  • ✅ Set up logrotate to compress and rotate application logs
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Operation of Running Systems 20%
🎯 Logging on the LFCS Exam

Expect tasks like: "Find all SSH login failures in the last hour" or "Configure logrotate to keep 30 days of nginx logs." The exam tests journalctl heavily — know how to filter by unit, time range, and priority. Also know the difference between journalctl (binary, systemd) and traditional text logs in /var/log.

17.1 Where Linux Stores Logs

Log LocationWhat It TracksHow to Monitor
/var/log/syslogGeneral system messages (Ubuntu)tail -f /var/log/syslog
/var/log/messagesGeneral system messages (RHEL/Rocky)tail -f /var/log/messages
/var/log/auth.logAuthentication events — SSH logins, sudo, sugrep "Failed password" /var/log/auth.log
/var/log/kern.logKernel messages — hardware, driversgrep -i error /var/log/kern.log
/var/log/nginx/Nginx access + error logstail -f /var/log/nginx/access.log
/var/log/postgresql/PostgreSQL query and error logstail -f /var/log/postgresql/postgresql-*.log
/run/systemd/journal/systemd journal (binary format)journalctl (not direct file read)

17.2 journalctl — systemd Journal

15 Most Useful journalctl Commands
  # All logs, oldest first:
  journalctl

  # Logs since last boot:
  journalctl -b

  # Logs from PREVIOUS boot (investigate crash!):
  journalctl -b -1

  # Logs for a specific service:
  journalctl -u nginx
  journalctl -u anihpj -n 50    # last 50 lines

  # Follow logs in real-time (like tail -f):
  journalctl -f

  # Filter by time:
  journalctl --since "1 hour ago"
  journalctl --since "2026-06-10 08:00:00" --until "09:00:00"

  # Filter by severity:
  journalctl -p err              # errors and worse only
  journalctl -p warning          # warnings and worse

  # Disk management:
  journalctl --disk-usage        # how much space?
  journalctl --vacuum-size=500M  # limit to 500 MB
  journalctl --vacuum-time=7d    # keep only 7 days

  # Kernel messages:
  journalctl -k                  # same as dmesg

  # By user:
  journalctl _UID=1001           # logs from user anihpj

17.3 rsyslog — Traditional Syslog Daemon

rsyslog is the traditional logging daemon that writes text log files to /var/log. It's still widely used alongside (or instead of) journald.

ConfigurationPurposeExample
/etc/rsyslog.confMain configuration fileGlobal settings, default rules
/etc/rsyslog.d/*.confDrop-in configuration directoryCreate /etc/rsyslog.d/anihpj.conf
Facility.Severity formatFilter what gets logged whereauth.warning /var/log/auth-warnings.log
Remote loggingForward logs to central server*.* @192.168.1.10:514 (UDP) or @@ (TCP)
🔧 Scenario: Forward All Anihpj App Errors to Central Log Server
# Create /etc/rsyslog.d/anihpj.conf:
# Only log anihpj errors and warnings to remote server
:programname, isequal, "anihpj"  @@logserver.anihpj.com:514
& stop   # Don't also write locally (avoid duplicates)
🔬 Deep Dive: How journald and rsyslog Coexist

On modern systems, both journald and rsyslog run simultaneously. Applications log to journald (via systemd-journald.service), and rsyslog reads from journald to create traditional text files in /var/log. The chain: App → journald (binary) → rsyslog → /var/log/syslog (text). If you remove rsyslog, journalctl still works but /var/log/syslog stops updating. If you remove journald, rsyslog has no input. Test logging: logger -p local0.info "Test message from anihpj" — generates a log entry you can find with journalctl -f.

🐍 Configuring Django to Log to syslog/journald
# In Django's settings.py — add syslog logging:
LOGGING = {
    'version': 1,
    'handlers': {
        'syslog': {
            'class': 'logging.handlers.SysLogHandler',
            'facility': 'local0',
            'address': '/dev/log',  # Unix socket to journald
        },
    },
    'root': {
        'handlers': ['syslog'],
        'level': 'INFO',
    },
}

# Now anihpj logs appear in:
journalctl -f  # Follow live
journalctl -t anihpj  # Filter by syslog identifier

This means every Django request, error, and warning is captured by journald — searchable, filterable, and forwardable to central logging without any file I/O overhead.

17.4 logrotate — Rotating Log Files

Without rotation, log files grow indefinitely until they fill your disk. logrotate compresses old logs and deletes ancient ones automatically.

OptionWhat It Does
daily / weekly / monthlyHow often to rotate
rotate 7Keep 7 rotated copies (then delete oldest)
compressGzip rotated logs (saves disk space)
delaycompressDon't compress the most recent rotation
missingokDon't error if log file doesn't exist
notifemptyDon't rotate empty log files
postrotate ... endscriptRun commands after rotation (e.g., reload service)
Complete logrotate Config for Anihpj Application Logs
  # /etc/logrotate.d/anihpj
  /var/log/anihpj/*.log {
      daily
      rotate 30
      compress
      delaycompress
      missingok
      notifempty
      create 640 anihpj www-data
      postrotate
          systemctl reload anihpj > /dev/null 2>&1 || true
      endscript
  }
🔧 Testing and Forcing Rotation

sudo logrotate -d /etc/logrotate.d/anihpj — debug mode: shows what WOULD happen without actually rotating. sudo logrotate -f /etc/logrotate.d/anihpj — force rotation NOW, regardless of schedule. Always test with -d first!

Syslog Facility Codes — What Each Facility Tracks
FacilityWhat It LogsAnihpj Example
auth / authprivAuthentication and authorizationSSH logins, sudo attempts
cronCron job executionNightly backup start/finish
daemonSystem daemons (no separate facility)sshd, nginx (if not using own log)
kernKernel messagesOOM killer, driver issues
local0 — local7Custom application useConfigure Django to log to local0
mailMail server logsPostfix delivery status
syslogsyslogd internal messagesrsyslog restart notices
userUser-level messagesDefault for most apps

Severity levels (highest to lowest): emerg (0), alert (1), crit (2), err (3), warning (4), notice (5), info (6), debug (7). Use *.err to capture everything at error level or worse. Use auth.warning to capture auth warnings and above.

💾 Persistent Journal Storage

By default, the journal is stored in /run/systemd/journal/ (tmpfs — lost on reboot). To make it persistent across reboots: sudo mkdir -p /var/log/journal and sudo systemctl restart systemd-journald. Now journalctl --list-boots will show ALL previous boots. Manage size: journalctl --vacuum-size=500M or journalctl --vacuum-time=30d.

📊 Chapter 17 Visual Summary

📁
Log Filessyslog/messages for system, auth.log for logins, /var/log/nginx/ for web, journal for binary.
🔍
journalctl-b for current boot, -u for service, -f to follow, --since for time range, -p for priority.
📡
rsyslogFacility.Severity format. /etc/rsyslog.d/ for custom rules. @@ for remote TCP forwarding.
🔄
logrotatedaily/weekly, rotate N, compress, postrotate scripts. -d for debug, -f to force.

Q3
Configure logrotate for /var/log/anihpj/*.log to rotate daily, keep 30 days, compress, and reload anihpj after rotation.

✅ Answer
# /etc/logrotate.d/anihpj
/var/log/anihpj/*.log {
    daily
    rotate 30

    missingok

        systemctl reload anihpj > /dev/null 2>&1 || true

Q4

Q5
Your server crashed. How do you view the logs from the PREVIOUS boot to investigate what happened?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
journalctl -b -1
# -b = current boot, -b -1 = previous boot, -b -2 = two boots ago
# List available boots:
journalctl --list-boots

Explanation: systemd's journal is persistent across reboots (if /var/log/journal exists). The -b -1 flag lets you go back in time to see what happened before the crash.

Q6
Show all ERROR and CRITICAL level messages from the current boot for the anihpj service.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
journalctl -u anihpj -b -p err

Explanation: -p err filters to priority "error" (3) and above — which includes err, crit, alert, and emerg. This is the fastest way to find what's broken.

Q7
Follow the nginx access log in real time and filter to show only 500 errors. Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
tail -f /var/log/nginx/access.log | grep " 500 "
Q8
How do you forward all authentication log messages (auth.*) to a remote syslog server at 192.168.1.100? Write the rsyslog config line.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# In /etc/rsyslog.d/remote.conf:
auth.*  @@192.168.1.100:514
# @@ = TCP (reliable), @ = UDP (faster but may lose messages)
# Then restart: sudo systemctl restart rsyslog
Q9
Force logrotate to rotate the anihpj logs NOW, and explain when you'd use the -d flag instead.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo logrotate -f /etc/logrotate.d/anihpj

-f forces rotation immediately regardless of schedule. Use -d (debug) first to see what WOULD happen without actually rotating — it's a dry run. Always logrotate -d before logrotate -f when testing new configs.

Q10
Where are kernel messages stored? Show two ways to view them — one with a file and one with journalctl.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Method 1: Traditional text file
cat /var/log/kern.log    # Ubuntu
tail -f /var/log/messages  # RHEL (kern.* goes here)

# Method 2: journalctl (works on all systemd systems)
journalctl -k
# OR: journalctl -k -b  (current boot only)

Beyond the Exam — Observability at Scale

🗓️ Day in the Life: How Chapters 15-17 Work Together

From spotting a problem to fixing it — here's how Process Management, systemd, and Logging form a single troubleshooting workflow:

🔍 8:00
Spot the Issue
Ch 15: top / htop

Load average is 8.0 on a 4-core server. Sort by CPU: top then press P. Postgres is using 350% CPU.

📋 8:05
Check the Logs
Ch 17: journalctl

journalctl -u postgresql -b -p err — reveals "too many connections" errors.

🔧 8:10
Restart the Service
Ch 16: systemctl

sudo systemctl restart postgresql — CPU drops back to normal. Problem solved for now.

⚙️ 8:15
Check Dependencies
Ch 16: systemctl status

systemctl status anihpj — verify Django reconnects. systemctl is-active anihpj → active.

🔄 8:20
Set Up Rotation
Ch 17: logrotate

Postgres logs are 2GB. Add rotation: daily, keep 7 days, compress. logrotate -f /etc/logrotate.d/postgresql

📊 8:30
Document & Monitor
Ch 15-17: All Together

Update runbook: "If load > 4, check postgres connections first." Add cron job to alert on high load.

🟡 Part 6: Networking

🌐 Network Troubleshooting Ladder — From Cable to Application

"I can't reach anihpj.com!" — Start at Layer 1 and go UP. Don't skip layers. The LFCS exam tests this systematic approach.

  LAYER 1 — PHYSICAL (cable, Wi-Fi, NIC)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ip link show           → Is the interface UP?               │
  │ eth0:  → ✅ UP                       │
  │ eth0:     → ❌ DOWN → ip link set up   │
  │ dmesg | grep eth0     → any hardware errors?                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  LAYER 2 — DATA LINK (MAC address, ARP)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ip neigh show          → ARP table (MAC addresses)         │
  │ Can you see your gateway's MAC? → ✅ L2 works               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  LAYER 3 — NETWORK (IP address, routing)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ip addr show           → Do you HAVE a valid IP?            │
  │ ip route show          → Is the default gateway correct?   │
  │ ping 8.8.8.8           → Can you reach the internet?       │
  │ traceroute 8.8.8.8     → Where do packets STOP?            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  LAYER 4 — TRANSPORT (TCP/UDP ports)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ss -tulnp              → Is the service LISTENING?          │
  │ nc -zv localhost 80    → Can you connect to port 80?       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  LAYER 5-7 — APPLICATION (HTTP, DNS, TLS)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ curl -I http://localhost  → HTTP 200 OK?                    │
  │ dig anihpj.com           → DNS resolves correctly?          │
  │ tail -f /var/log/nginx/error.log → app-level errors?       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔌 Anihpj Ports & Services — Quick Reference Map
PortServiceProtocolDirectionLFCS Chapter
22SSHTCPIN (admin)Ch 26
80HTTP (Nginx)TCPIN (web)Ch 27
443HTTPS (Nginx)TCPIN (web)Ch 27, 33
5432PostgreSQLTCPlocalhostCh 28
8000GunicornTCPlocalhostCh 16, 30
6379RedisTCPlocalhostCh 30

Firewall rule logic: INCOMING: ✅ Port 22 (SSH, restrict to your IP!), ✅ 80/443 (web). ❌ Port 5432 (PostgreSQL — NEVER expose!), ❌ Port 8000 (Gunicorn — only Nginx talks locally). OUTGOING: default allow (apt, NTP, DNS, git).

Chapter 18: Networking Fundamentals

Every service on your anihpj/jobpost server — from SSH to Nginx to PostgreSQL — communicates over the network. This chapter builds your foundation: the TCP/IP model, IP addressing and subnetting, ports and services, and DNS — the internet's phonebook. You'll trace a real HTTP request from a user's browser all the way to the Django application.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🌐 Core

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the 4-layer TCP/IP model and how data flows through each layer
  • ✅ Master IPv4 addressing, subnet masks, CIDR notation, and private ranges
  • ✅ Know common service ports and how to check what's listening
  • ✅ Understand DNS resolution and use dig/nslookup for troubleshooting
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Networking 25%
🎯 Networking on the LFCS Exam

Networking is the LARGEST single domain at 25%. You'll configure IP addresses, troubleshoot connectivity, and work with firewalls. The #1 mistake: configuring an IP with ip addr add and wondering why it disappears after reboot. Always know which tool makes permanent changes (netplan/nmcli) vs temporary (ip).

18.1 The TCP/IP Model — How the Internet Works

HTTP Request to anihpj — Full Network Path
  Browser (user)
    │ HTTP GET /jobs/
    ▼
  DNS lookup: anihpj.com → 203.0.113.10
    │
    ▼ TCP connection to port 80
  Linux Firewall (iptables/ufw) → allows port 80
    │
    ▼
  Nginx (port 80/443) → reverse proxy
    │
    ▼ Unix socket: /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock
  Gunicorn (Django WSGI) → handles request
    │
    ▼ PostgreSQL connection on port 5432
  PostgreSQL → returns job listings
    │
    ▼
  Response travels back up → browser shows /jobs/ page
4️⃣
Transport (TCP/UDP)

TCP: reliable, ordered, connections. UDP: fast, no guarantee. Ports identify which app.

3️⃣
Network (IP)

IP addresses route packets between networks. IPv4: 32-bit. IPv6: 128-bit.

2️⃣
Data Link (MAC)

MAC addresses identify physical network cards on the local network segment.

1️⃣
Physical

Cables, Wi-Fi, fiber optics. The actual electrical/optical signals.

🔬 Deep Dive: How TCP Connections Actually Work — The Three-Way Handshake

Before any HTTP request, TCP establishes a connection:

  1. Client → Server: SYN ("I want to talk. Here's my starting sequence number X.")
  2. Server → Client: SYN-ACK ("OK, I received your SYN. Here's MY sequence number Y. I acknowledge your X+1.")
  3. Client → Server: ACK ("Got your SYN-ACK. Connection established!")

After these 3 packets, data flows. Connection termination uses FIN packets (4-way handshake). TCP states visible with ss -tan: LISTEN (waiting), ESTABLISHED (active), TIME_WAIT (closing), CLOSE_WAIT (remote closed). Too many TIME_WAIT sockets = high connection churn.

🔬 ARP — How IP Addresses Become MAC Addresses

When your server wants to send a packet to 192.168.1.1, it needs the MAC address. It broadcasts an ARP request: "Who has 192.168.1.1? Tell 192.168.1.10." The owner replies with its MAC. The result is cached in the ARP table: ip neigh show. ARP only works on the local network segment — it cannot cross routers. ARP spoofing is when an attacker replies with a fake MAC, redirecting traffic. Defense: static ARP entries or switch-level port security.

🔬 ICMP — The Internet's Messenger Protocol

ICMP is neither TCP nor UDP — it's a control protocol at Layer 3. ping sends ICMP Echo Request (Type 8), target replies with Echo Reply (Type 0). traceroute sends UDP packets with increasing TTL — each router that expires the TTL sends back ICMP Time Exceeded (Type 11), revealing the path. Path MTU Discovery uses ICMP to find the largest packet size that fits without fragmentation. Blocking ALL ICMP breaks Path MTU Discovery and causes hard-to-debug connectivity issues.

18.2 IP Addressing — IPv4 and IPv6

NotationMaskUsable HostsExample Use
/8255.0.0.016,777,214Huge networks (10.0.0.0/8 = private)
/16255.255.0.065,534Large enterprise (172.16.0.0/12 = private)
/24255.255.255.0254Small office/home (192.168.1.0/24)
/32255.255.255.2551Single host (loopback, firewall rules)
📋 Private IP Ranges (RFC 1918)

10.0.0.0/8 (10.x.x.x), 172.16.0.0/12 (172.16-31.x.x), 192.168.0.0/16 (192.168.x.x). These are NOT routable on the public internet — used for internal networks. 127.0.0.1 is loopback (localhost). 169.254.0.0/16 is link-local (self-assigned when DHCP fails).

CIDR Quick Reference — Common Subnet Sizes
CIDRMaskBinaryHostsUse
/32255.255.255.25511111111.11111111.11111111.111111111Single host
/30255.255.255.252...111111002Point-to-point link
/29255.255.255.248...111110006Small subnet
/28255.255.255.240...1111000014Small office
/24255.255.255.0...00000000254Standard LAN
/16255.255.0.011111111.11111111.00000000.0000000065,534Large network
/8255.0.0.011111111.00000000.00000000.0000000016,777,214Huge (Class A)
🔬 IPv6 Basics

IPv6 uses 128-bit addresses in 8 groups of 4 hex digits: 2001:0db8:0000:0000:0000:ff00:0042:8329. Shorthand: leading zeros can be omitted, and consecutive zero groups can be replaced with :: once: 2001:db8::ff00:42:8329. Loopback: ::1. Private: fc00::/7 (Unique Local). Link-local: fe80::/10 (every interface auto-configures one). ip -6 addr show shows IPv6 addresses.

18.3 Ports and Services

PortServiceProtocolNotes
22SSHTCPSecure remote administration
80HTTPTCPUnencrypted web (Nginx serves this)
443HTTPSTCPEncrypted web (TLS/SSL)
5432PostgreSQLTCPDatabase connections
6379RedisTCPIn-memory cache
8000Gunicorn (dev)TCPDjango development server

Ports 1-1024 are privileged — only root can bind to them. ss -tulnp shows all listening ports with process names. nc -zv localhost 80 tests if a port is reachable.

18.4 DNS — Domain Name System

CommandWhat It DoesExample
dig anihpj.com AQuery DNS for IPv4 addressShows full DNS response with TTL
dig +short A anihpj.comJust the IP, nothing else203.0.113.10
dig -x 203.0.113.10Reverse DNS (IP → name)PTR record lookup
nslookup anihpj.comInteractive DNS querySimpler output than dig
cat /etc/resolv.confWhich DNS server is usednameserver 1.1.1.1
cat /etc/hostsStatic name resolution (overrides DNS)127.0.0.1 anihpj.local

Chapter 18 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
What command shows all listening TCP ports on your system with the process names?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ss -tlnp
Q2
Query the A record for anihpj.com using dig and show only the IP address.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
dig +short A anihpj.com
Q3
What private IP ranges are defined in RFC 1918? List all three.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

10.0.0.0/8 (10.x.x.x), 172.16.0.0/12 (172.16-31.x.x), 192.168.0.0/16 (192.168.x.x).

Q4
How many usable host addresses are in a /24 network? Show the calculation.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

A /24 has 2^(32-24) = 256 total addresses. Subtract 2 (network address + broadcast address) = 254 usable hosts.

Q5
Add a static entry to /etc/hosts so that "anihpj.local" resolves to 127.0.0.1 for local development.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
echo "127.0.0.1 anihpj.local" | sudo tee -a /etc/hosts
Q6
What is the difference between TCP and UDP? Give an example of a service that uses each.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

TCP is connection-oriented, reliable, ordered — it guarantees delivery through acknowledgments and retransmission. UDP is connectionless, fast, no guarantees — packets may arrive out of order or not at all. TCP: HTTP/HTTPS (Nginx), SSH, PostgreSQL. UDP: DNS queries, NTP (time sync), streaming video.

Q7
What subnet mask corresponds to /26? How many usable hosts?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

/26 = 255.255.255.192. 2^(32-26) = 64 total addresses. Subtract 2 = 62 usable hosts.

Q8
You can ping 8.8.8.8 but can't reach anihpj.com. What layer is the problem at and how do you diagnose it?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Layer 3 (IP routing) works (ping succeeds), so the problem is at Layer 7 (DNS). Diagnose: dig anihpj.com — if it returns no answer, check /etc/resolv.conf for correct nameserver. Then try dig @8.8.8.8 anihpj.com to bypass local DNS.

Q9
What command shows the ARP table (MAC addresses of devices on your local network)?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ip neigh show

Legacy equivalent: arp -a. The ARP table maps IP addresses to MAC addresses on the local network segment.

Q10
Test if port 5432 (PostgreSQL) is reachable on localhost. Write the command using nc.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
nc -zv localhost 5432

-z = zero-I/O mode (just test, don't send data). -v = verbose. Output: "Connection to localhost 5432 port [tcp/postgresql] succeeded!"

Chapter 19: Network Configuration

Configuring network interfaces is one of the most common sysadmin tasks — and one of the easiest to get wrong. This chapter covers the modern ip command (replacing ifconfig), persistent configuration with Netplan (Ubuntu) and nmcli (Rocky/RHEL), and a complete troubleshooting toolkit for when your anihpj server goes offline.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔧 Hands-On

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Master the ip command for viewing and temporarily configuring interfaces
  • ✅ Configure persistent static IPs with Netplan (Ubuntu) and nmcli (Rocky)
  • ✅ Troubleshoot network connectivity with ping, traceroute, ss, tcpdump, and curl
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Networking 25%
🎯 Network Config on the LFCS Exam

Expect to be given a broken network configuration and asked to fix it. You'll need to know both Ubuntu (netplan) and Rocky (nmcli) approaches. The exam provides man pages — man netplan and man nmcli are your friends. Remember: ip changes are temporary; netplan apply or nmcli con up make them permanent.

19.1 The ip Command — Modern Network Configuration

CommandWhat It DoesLegacy Equivalent
ip addr showAll interfaces with IP addressesifconfig
ip addr add 192.168.1.10/24 dev eth0Add IP (temporary!)ifconfig eth0 192.168.1.10
ip link set eth0 upBring interface upifconfig eth0 up
ip link set eth0 downBring interface downifconfig eth0 down
ip route showRouting tableroute -n
ip route add default via 192.168.1.1Add default gatewayroute add default gw 192.168.1.1
ip neigh showARP table (MAC addresses)arp -a
⚠️ ip changes are TEMPORARY

Every change made with ip is lost on reboot. Use Netplan (Ubuntu) or nmcli (Rocky) for persistent configuration. This is the #1 exam trap — you configure networking, reboot, and it's gone!

19.2 Netplan — Ubuntu Persistent Network Config

Netplan Static IP — Anihpj Production Server
  # /etc/netplan/01-netcfg.yaml
  network:
    version: 2
    renderer: networkd
    ethernets:
      eth0:
        dhcp4: no
        addresses:
          - 203.0.113.10/24
        gateway4: 203.0.113.1
        nameservers:
          addresses: [1.1.1.1, 8.8.8.8]
CommandWhat It Does
sudo netplan applyApply configuration immediately
sudo netplan tryApply with 120s auto-rollback — lifesaver for remote config!
sudo netplan --debug applyVerbose output for troubleshooting

19.3 nmcli — NetworkManager CLI (RHEL/Rocky)

Tasknetplan (Ubuntu)nmcli (Rocky)
List devicesip addr shownmcli device status
List connectionsls /etc/netplan/nmcli connection show
Set static IPEdit .yaml + netplan applynmcli con mod eth0 ipv4.addresses 10.0.0.5/24
Set gatewaygateway4 in YAMLnmcli con mod eth0 ipv4.gateway 10.0.0.1
Activatenetplan applynmcli con up eth0
🖥️ nmtui — NetworkManager Text User Interface

On Rocky/RHEL, nmtui opens a curses-based interactive network manager in the terminal. Use arrow keys to navigate: Edit a connection → select interface → set IP/mask/gateway/DNS → OK → Back → Quit. Changes are persistent. This is much easier than nmcli for complex configurations and is available on the LFCS exam terminal.

🔗 Network Bonding — Combine Interfaces for Redundancy

Bonding joins multiple physical interfaces into one logical interface for failover or increased throughput. Active-backup mode: if eth0 fails, eth1 takes over automatically. Config in netplan: bond0 with interfaces: [eth0, eth1] and mode: active-backup. Check: cat /proc/net/bonding/bond0. Essential for high-availability servers.

🏷️ Network Interface Naming — Why eth0 Became enp0s3

Modern Linux uses Predictable Network Interface Names: enp0s3 = Ethernet, PCI bus 0, slot 3. ens33 = Ethernet, PCI slot 33. wlx... = Wireless LAN by MAC address. Legacy eth0 naming was unpredictable — interface order could change between boots. ip link show lists current names. /etc/udev/rules.d/70-persistent-net.rules maps MAC→name if needed.

🔧 DHCP Client — Getting an IP Automatically

DHCP assigns IP, gateway, DNS automatically. Netplan: dhcp4: yes. Rocky: nmcli con mod eth0 ipv4.method auto. Manual renew: sudo dhclient -r eth0 && sudo dhclient eth0. Check lease: cat /var/lib/dhcp/dhclient.leases. Static DHCP (reservation) maps MAC→fixed IP on the DHCP server — best of both worlds.

🔬 VLANs — Virtual LANs on One Physical Cable

VLANs tag Ethernet frames with a VLAN ID (1-4094), allowing multiple isolated networks on one physical interface. Linux creates virtual interfaces: eth0.10 = VLAN 10 on eth0. Netplan: add vlans: vlan10: {id: 10, link: eth0, addresses: [10.0.10.1/24]}. Rocky: nmcli con add type vlan con-name vlan10 dev eth0 id 10. Use for: separating management, storage, and application traffic on one cable.

🔍 ethtool — Low-Level Interface Diagnostics

ethtool eth0 shows: speed (1000Mb/s), duplex (Full), link detected (yes). ethtool -S eth0 shows statistics: packets sent/received, errors, drops. ethtool -p eth0 blinks the physical port LED for 5 seconds — find which cable is which in a crowded rack! If link is "no" → check cable, switch port, or NIC driver.

🔧 Real-World: "I Can't Reach the Anihpj Server!"

Situation: A developer reports the job board is unreachable. You SSH to the server and start from Layer 1:

  1. ip link show eth0 → state UP ✅
  2. ip addr show eth0 → 203.0.113.10/24 ✅ Has IP
  3. ping 8.8.8.8 → 0% packet loss ✅ Internet works
  4. ss -tulnp | grep :80 → (nothing) ❌ Nginx not listening!
  5. systemctl status nginx → inactive (dead) — someone stopped it!
  6. sudo systemctl start nginx → site is back up. Root cause: config change without reload.

Lesson: Follow the layers. The problem was at Layer 4 (no service listening), not Layer 3 (IP/routing).

19.4 Network Troubleshooting Tools

CommandWhat It TestsExample
ping -c 4 8.8.8.8Basic connectivity (Layer 3)Is the internet reachable?
traceroute 8.8.8.8Path packets take (each hop)Where do packets stop?
mtr 8.8.8.8Combined ping + traceroute (live)Continuous path monitoring
ss -tulnpListening ports + process namesIs nginx actually listening?
tcpdump -i eth0 port 80Capture packets on interfaceSee exact HTTP traffic
curl -v http://localhostHTTP response with headersIs the web app responding?

📊 Chapter 19 Visual Summary

🔧

📝
NetplanUbuntu: YAML in /etc/netplan/. netplan try = 120s safety net.
🔌

🔍
Troubleshootingping→traceroute→ss→tcpdump→curl. Start at Layer 3, go up.

Chapter 20: DNS Configuration & Hostname Management

DNS is the internet's phonebook — it translates human-readable names like anihpj.com into machine-routable IP addresses. This chapter covers the full DNS resolution stack on Linux: from /etc/hosts (local overrides) through /etc/resolv.conf (which server to ask) to running your own Bind9 DNS server for internal infrastructure.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour📖 Configuration

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the DNS resolution order: /etc/hosts → /etc/resolv.conf → DNS server
  • ✅ Configure system hostname and manage /etc/hosts entries
  • ✅ Set up a Bind9 DNS server for internal infrastructure
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Networking 25%
🎯 DNS on the LFCS Exam

You'll be asked to configure DNS resolution — either by editing /etc/hosts for a quick fix, or by configuring systemd-resolved. Know the resolution order: /etc/nsswitch.conf controls whether files (hosts) or dns is checked first. The default is "files dns" — hosts file first, then DNS.

20.1 DNS Resolution Stack

How Linux Resolves a Hostname — Step by Step
  Application calls getaddrinfo("anihpj.com")
       │
       ▼
  /etc/nsswitch.conf → "hosts: files dns"
       │
       ├── ① Check /etc/hosts first (files)
       │     127.0.0.1   localhost
       │     203.0.113.10 anihpj.com   ← Match! Return this.
       │
       └── ② If not found, query DNS (dns)
              Read /etc/resolv.conf → nameserver 1.1.1.1
              Send query to 1.1.1.1 → get response → return IP
FilePurposeExample
/etc/hostsStatic name-to-IP mapping. Checked BEFORE DNS.127.0.0.1 anihpj.local
/etc/resolv.confWhich DNS servers to query. Managed by systemd-resolved on modern systems.nameserver 1.1.1.1
/etc/nsswitch.confName Service Switch — controls resolution ORDER.hosts: files dns
🔧 systemd-resolved

On modern Ubuntu, /etc/resolv.conf is a symlink to /run/systemd/resolve/stub-resolv.conf. Use resolvectl status to see current DNS configuration. To add a DNS server permanently: edit /etc/systemd/resolved.conf or use netplan's nameservers section.

20.2 Hostname Configuration

CommandWhat It Does
hostnamectlShow current hostname and system info
sudo hostnamectl set-hostname anihpj-prodSet hostname permanently (writes /etc/hostname)
hostnameShow current hostname (temporary view)
cat /etc/hostnameStatic hostname file (read at boot)

After changing hostname, add it to /etc/hosts: 127.0.1.1 anihpj-prod. This prevents sudo warnings about being unable to resolve the hostname.

20.3 Configuring a DNS Server (Bind9) — Basics

Internal DNS for anihpj.local with Bind9
  # Install: sudo apt install bind9

  # /etc/bind/named.conf.local — zone declarations:
  zone "anihpj.local" {
      type master;
      file "/etc/bind/db.anihpj.local";
  };

  # /etc/bind/db.anihpj.local — forward zone file:
  $TTL 86400
  @   IN  SOA  ns1.anihpj.local. admin.anihpj.local. (
              2026061001  ; Serial (YYYYMMDD + revision)
              3600        ; Refresh
              1800        ; Retry
              604800      ; Expire
              86400 )     ; Minimum TTL
  @       IN  NS   ns1.anihpj.local.
  ns1     IN  A    192.168.1.10
  web     IN  A    192.168.1.20
  db      IN  A    192.168.1.30
  # Now: ping web.anihpj.local → 192.168.1.20

Key Bind9 files: /etc/bind/named.conf.local (zone declarations), /etc/bind/named.conf.options (forwarders, ACLs). Forward zone = name → IP. Reverse zone = IP → name (PTR records). After editing: sudo systemctl restart bind9. Test: dig @localhost web.anihpj.local.

🔬 How DNS Caching Works — And Why It Matters

DNS caching happens at multiple levels: (1) Application: browsers cache for ~1 minute. (2) systemd-resolved: caches per the record's TTL. Clear with resolvectl flush-caches. (3) Recursive resolver: your ISP's DNS caches for TTL. (4) Authoritative: the source server sets TTL. Troubleshooting: if a DNS change "isn't working," check each cache layer. dig +trace anihpj.com shows the full resolution path from root servers down. DNSSEC adds cryptographic signatures to DNS records — prevents cache poisoning. Enable with dnssec-validation yes; in Bind9 options.

Common DNS Record Types
TypeMapsExampleUse
AName → IPv4anihpj.com. A 203.0.113.10Standard web hosting
AAAAName → IPv6anihpj.com. AAAA 2001:db8::1IPv6 web hosting
CNAMEAlias → Canonical Namewww.anihpj.com. CNAME anihpj.com.www is same server
MXDomain → Mail Serveranihpj.com. MX 10 mail.anihpj.com.Email routing
TXTArbitrary textanihpj.com. TXT "v=spf1 ..."SPF, DKIM, verification
PTRIP → Name (reverse)10.113.0.203.in-addr.arpa. PTR anihpj.com.Reverse DNS
NSDomain → Nameserveranihpj.com. NS ns1.anihpj.com.Delegation

Q1
Set the system hostname to \"anihpj-prod\" permanently. Write the command.

✅ Answer

Q2
Add a static entry so that \"db.local\" resolves to 192.168.1.100 without querying DNS.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
echo "192.168.1.100 db.local" | sudo tee -a /etc/hosts
Q3
What file controls whether /etc/hosts or DNS is checked first? What is the default order?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

/etc/nsswitch.conf — the line hosts: files dns means /etc/hosts is checked FIRST, then DNS. Default order is \"files dns\".

Q4
Query the DNS server at 8.8.8.8 for the A record of example.com. Write the dig command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
dig @8.8.8.8 example.com A
Q5
What command shows the current DNS servers configured on a systemd-resolved system?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
resolvectl status
Q6
What is the purpose of /etc/nsswitch.conf? What does "hosts: files dns" mean?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

/etc/nsswitch.conf controls the ORDER of name resolution. "hosts: files dns" means: check /etc/hosts FIRST, then query DNS. This is the default on all Linux systems. Other options include "dns files" (DNS first), "files" (only hosts file), or "dns" (only DNS).

Q7
Perform a reverse DNS lookup on IP 8.8.8.8. Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
dig -x 8.8.8.8
# OR: nslookup 8.8.8.8

Reverse DNS maps IP → hostname using PTR records. Useful for: verifying a mail server's identity, troubleshooting suspicious connections.

Q8
Set the hostname to "web01.anihpj.com" permanently. Then verify it was applied.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

# Shows: Static hostname: web01.anihpj.com
Q9
You added "192.168.1.100 db.local" to /etc/hosts but ping still resolves to a different IP. Why?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Possible causes: (1) Another entry for db.local exists LATER in the file (first match wins). (2) DNS is checked FIRST if nsswitch.conf says "hosts: dns files". (3) The application caches DNS. (4) systemd-resolved has its own cache — try resolvectl flush-caches.

Q10
Configure Bind9 to forward all external queries to Google DNS (8.8.8.8). Which file do you edit and what do you add?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Edit /etc/bind/named.conf.options:
options {
    forwarders {
        8.8.8.8;
        8.8.4.4;
    };
};
# Then: sudo systemctl restart bind9

Chapter 21: Firewall & Network Security

A server without a firewall is like a house with no doors — anyone can walk in. This chapter covers Linux firewall fundamentals: how netfilter/iptables/nftables work at the kernel level, the user-friendly ufw (Ubuntu) and firewalld (Rocky) frontends, and critical SSH hardening to protect your anihpj server from attackers.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔥 Security

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand how Linux firewalls work at the kernel level (netfilter)
  • ✅ Configure ufw for Ubuntu servers and firewalld for Rocky/RHEL
  • ✅ Write raw iptables rules (still tested on LFCS)
  • ✅ Harden SSH with key-only auth, non-root login, and rate limiting
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Networking 25%
🎯 Firewalls on the LFCS Exam

You'll be asked to configure firewall rules to allow/deny specific ports. Ubuntu uses ufw, Rocky uses firewalld. Both may appear. Also know raw iptables — it's still tested. The #1 mistake: enabling a firewall remotely without first allowing SSH (port 22). You'll lock yourself out!

21.1 How Linux Firewalls Work — iptables & nftables

Packet Flow Through iptables Chains
  INCOMING PACKET
       │
       ▼
  ┌──────────┐     ┌───────────┐     ┌───────────┐
  │ PREROUTING│────→│  FORWARD  │────→│POSTROUTING│  (routing through this host)
  │ (NAT)     │     │ (routing) │     │ (NAT)     │
  └──────────┘     └───────────┘     └───────────┘
       │                                      ↑
       │ (for local processes)                │
       ▼                                      │
  ┌──────────┐                          (outgoing packets
  │  INPUT   │                           go through OUTPUT
  │ (to us)  │                           then POSTROUTING)
  └──────────┘
       │
       ▼
  Local Process (nginx, sshd, etc.)

  iptables -L -n -v          → list all rules with packet counts
  iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 22 -j ACCEPT   → allow SSH
  iptables -A INPUT -j DROP                         → default deny (LAST rule!)
🔧
1990s
ipchains

Original Linux firewall. Stateless. Replaced by iptables in kernel 2.4.

Obsolete
📜
2000s
iptables

Stateful firewall. Separate tools for IPv4, IPv6, ARP. Still widely used.

Legacy
✨
2014+
nftables

Unified framework. One tool for all protocols. Better syntax and performance.

Modern
🛡️
Always
ufw / firewalld

User-friendly frontends. ufw for Ubuntu, firewalld for Rocky. Production standard.

Recommended

21.2 ufw — Uncomplicated Firewall (Ubuntu)

Complete UFW Configuration for Anihpj Production Server
  # Reset and configure fresh
  sudo ufw reset
  sudo ufw default deny incoming
  sudo ufw default allow outgoing

  # Allow SSH (restrict to your IP in production!)
  sudo ufw allow 22/tcp

  # Allow web traffic
  sudo ufw allow 80/tcp
  sudo ufw allow 443/tcp

  # PostgreSQL: ONLY from localhost (default deny handles this)

  sudo ufw enable
  sudo ufw status verbose
CommandWhat It Does
ufw allow 22/tcpAllow SSH from anywhere
ufw allow from 10.0.0.0/8 to any port 22Allow SSH only from private network
ufw deny 5432/tcpExplicitly block PostgreSQL
ufw delete 2Delete rule #2
ufw resetClear ALL rules and disable

21.3 firewalld — Zone-Based Firewall (Rocky Linux)

ZoneDefault PolicyUse Case
publicReject incoming, allow outgoingDefault for public-facing servers
trustedAccept allInternal network, fully trusted
internalReject incoming (less strict)Internal servers with some trust
dmzReject all except selectedPublic-facing services, isolated
dropDrop all (no response)Stealth — don't even reply to scans
blockReject all (with response)Explicit rejection
CommandWhat It Does
firewall-cmd --get-active-zonesShow active zones
firewall-cmd --permanent --add-service=httpAllow HTTP permanently
firewall-cmd --permanent --add-port=8000/tcpAllow port 8000
firewall-cmd --reloadApply permanent changes
firewall-cmd --list-allShow current zone rules

21.4 iptables — The Classic Approach

CommandWhat It Does
iptables -L -n -vList rules with packet/byte counts
iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 22 -j ACCEPTAllow SSH (append to INPUT chain)
iptables -A INPUT -j DROPDefault deny — DROP everything else
iptables -I INPUT 1 -p tcp --dport 443 -j ACCEPTInsert HTTPS rule at position 1
iptables-save > /etc/iptables/rules.v4Save rules to survive reboot
⚠️ Modern systems prefer ufw/firewalld

iptables is still tested on the LFCS exam, but in production, use ufw (Ubuntu) or firewalld (Rocky). They're simpler, less error-prone, and integrate with systemd. iptables rules are also lost on reboot unless saved with iptables-save.

🔬 nftables — The Modern Replacement for iptables

nftables replaces iptables, ip6tables, arptables, and ebtables with ONE unified framework. Basic syntax: nft add table inet filter → nft add chain inet filter input '{ type filter hook input priority 0; }' → nft add rule inet filter input tcp dport 22 accept. nft list ruleset shows all rules. nft -f /etc/nftables.conf loads from file. Simpler syntax, better performance, IPv4+IPv6 in one table.

📜 iptables (Legacy)
# Allow SSH, HTTP, HTTPS — 6 rules for 3 services:
iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 22 -j ACCEPT
ip6tables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 22 -j ACCEPT
iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 80 -j ACCEPT
ip6tables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 80 -j ACCEPT
iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 443 -j ACCEPT
ip6tables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 443 -j ACCEPT
# Must save separately:
iptables-save > /etc/iptables/rules.v4
ip6tables-save > /etc/iptables/rules.v6
✨ nftables (Modern)
# Same rules — ONE table handles IPv4 AND IPv6:
table inet filter {
    chain input {
        type filter hook input priority 0;
        tcp dport {22, 80, 443} accept
        drop
    }
}
# Load from file:
nft -f /etc/nftables.conf
# One command, one file, both protocols.
🔀 NAT & Port Forwarding with iptables

Network Address Translation allows private IPs to access the internet through one public IP. Masquerading: iptables -t nat -A POSTROUTING -o eth0 -j MASQUERADE — all outgoing traffic appears to come from eth0's IP. Port forwarding: iptables -t nat -A PREROUTING -p tcp --dport 8080 -j DNAT --to-destination 192.168.1.100:80 — forward external port 8080 to internal server. Enable IP forwarding: echo 1 > /proc/sys/net/ipv4/ip_forward.

🚦 Rate Limiting with iptables — Prevent Brute Force Attacks
# Limit SSH connections to 5 per minute per IP:
iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 22 -m state --state NEW -m recent --set
iptables -A INPUT -p tcp --dport 22 -m state --state NEW -m recent \
    --update --seconds 60 --hitcount 5 -j DROP
# After 5 new SSH connections in 60 seconds, DROP all further attempts
# This stops brute-force attacks without blocking legitimate users
🔬 Connection Tracking — How Stateful Firewalls Work

Modern firewalls are stateful — they track connections, not just individual packets. The kernel's conntrack module maintains a table of all active connections: conntrack -L lists them. conntrack -S shows statistics. Typical rule: allow ESTABLISHED and RELATED traffic back in — this automatically allows responses to outgoing connections without opening ports. iptables -A INPUT -m conntrack --ctstate ESTABLISHED,RELATED -j ACCEPT. This is the foundation of "default deny" policies that still allow internet access.

📜 TCP Wrappers — Host-Based Access Control (Legacy)

TCP Wrappers (/etc/hosts.allow and /etc/hosts.deny) provide an additional layer of access control for services compiled with libwrap. Format: sshd: 192.168.1.0/255.255.255.0 (allow SSH from local subnet). ALL: ALL in hosts.deny blocks everything not explicitly allowed. Check if a service uses wrappers: ldd /usr/sbin/sshd | grep libwrap. Note: modern systems prefer firewall rules; TCP wrappers are deprecated but still appear on the LFCS exam.

21.5 SSH Security — Hardening sshd_config

Complete Hardened sshd_config for Anihpj Production
  # /etc/ssh/sshd_config — security hardening
  PermitRootLogin no              # NEVER allow direct root login
  PasswordAuthentication no       # Force SSH key authentication only
  PubkeyAuthentication yes        # Enable public key auth
  AllowUsers anihpj owner         # Whitelist: ONLY these users can SSH
  MaxAuthTries 3                  # Limit brute-force (default is 6)
  MaxSessions 3                   # Limit concurrent sessions per user
  LoginGraceTime 30               # 30 seconds to authenticate
  ClientAliveInterval 300         # Disconnect idle clients after 5 min
  X11Forwarding no                # Disable X11 (not needed on servers)
  PermitEmptyPasswords no         # No password = no login
⚠️ Before Applying: Test Your Config!

sudo sshd -t — tests the config file syntax. Always keep one SSH session open when changing sshd_config. In a second terminal: sudo systemctl reload sshd. If the new session can connect, the config is good. If not, fix it from the still-open session.

Q9
What is the difference between DROP and REJECT in iptables? When would you use each?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

DROP: Silently discards the packet — no response sent. The sender hangs until timeout. Use for: stealth (don't reveal the server exists). REJECT: Discards packet but sends an ICMP \"port unreachable\" response. The sender knows immediately. Use for: internal networks where you want fast failure.

Q10
You enabled ufw remotely but forgot to allow SSH. Now you're locked out. How do you recover?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

If you have console access (VPS provider's web console, IPMI, physical access): log in locally and run sudo ufw disable. If no console: you're locked out — the server must be rebooted into recovery mode or the disk mounted on another system. Prevention: Always ufw allow 22/tcp BEFORE ufw enable!

💿 Storage Architecture Map — From Raw Disk to Usable Data
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① PHYSICAL / VIRTUAL DISK                                       │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────┐                  │

  │  │ 50GB SSD │  │ 100GB HDD│  │ 500GB NVMe   │                  │
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────────┘                  │
  │  COMMANDS: lsblk, fdisk -l, lshw -class disk                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ② PARTITION TABLE                                               │
  │  MBR (legacy): max 4 primary, 2TB │ GPT (modern): 128, 9ZB+     │
  │  COMMANDS: fdisk (MBR), gdisk/parted (GPT)                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ③ LVM (Optional Flexibility Layer)                              │
  │  PV (Physical Volume) → VG (Volume Group) → LV (Logical Volume) │
  │  💡 Benefit: grow/shrink WITHOUT reformatting!                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ④ FILESYSTEM                                                    │
  │  mkfs.ext4 /dev/vg/lv  │  ext4: stable  │  xfs: performance     │
  │  COMMANDS: mkfs, tune2fs, fsck, xfs_repair                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ⑤ MOUNT POINT (Access)                                         │
  │  /etc/fstab: UUID=xxx /data ext4 defaults 0 2                   │
  │  COMMANDS: mount, umount, df, findmnt                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Chapter 22: Disks & Partitions — Dividing Your Storage

Every byte of data on your anihpj server lives on a disk — and that disk must be partitioned before it can be used. This chapter covers how Linux names storage devices, the two partition table formats (MBR vs GPT), and the tools to create and manage partitions with fdisk, gdisk, and parted.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour💿 Storage

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand Linux disk device naming (/dev/sda, nvme0n1, vda)
  • ✅ Compare MBR vs GPT partition tables and choose the right one
  • ✅ Create partitions with fdisk (MBR) and gdisk/parted (GPT)
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Storage Management 20%
🎯 Disks on the LFCS Exam

You WILL be given a raw disk and asked to partition it, format it, and mount it. Know the difference between fdisk (MBR) and gdisk (GPT). The exam may present either. Always verify you're working on the RIGHT disk — lsblk before any destructive operation!

22.1 How Linux Sees Storage Devices

Understanding lsblk Output
  $ lsblk -f
  NAME   FSTYPE  LABEL  UUID              MOUNTPOINT
  sda
  ├─sda1  vfat          AAAA-BBBB         /boot/efi
  ├─sda2  ext4          xxxx-yyyy         /boot
  └─sda3  LVM2_member   zzzz-aaaa
    ├─ubuntu--vg-root   ext4    /
    └─ubuntu--vg-swap   swap    [SWAP]
  sdb
  └─sdb1  (empty — new disk for anihpj data)
Device NameWhat It IsCommon In
/dev/sda, /dev/sdbSATA/SCSI/SAS disksPhysical servers, VMs
/dev/nvme0n1NVMe SSDModern servers, cloud
/dev/vdaVirtual diskKVM/QEMU VMs
/dev/sda1Partition 1 on sdaAll systems

lsblk = tree view of all block devices. lsblk -f adds filesystem and UUID. blkid shows UUIDs and filesystem types for all devices.

🔬 udev — Why /dev/sda Can Become /dev/sdb After Reboot

The Linux kernel does NOT guarantee persistent device names. /dev/sda and /dev/sdb are assigned in the ORDER the kernel discovers them — which depends on timing, controller scan order, and which disk spins up first. After adding a new disk, /dev/sdb might become /dev/sdc and your new disk becomes /dev/sdb. This is why /etc/fstab uses UUIDs, not device names. Persistent alternative paths (udev-created symlinks): /dev/disk/by-uuid/ — filesystem UUID (what fstab uses), /dev/disk/by-id/ — manufacturer+serial (e.g., ata-Samsung_SSD_860_SERIAL), /dev/disk/by-path/ — PCI/physical port location, /dev/disk/by-label/ — human-readable label (if set with mkfs -L). Best practice: Always use /dev/disk/by-id/ in scripts that must address a specific physical disk — it survives reboots and device reordering.

22.2 Partition Tables — MBR vs GPT

FeatureMBR (Legacy)GPT (Modern)
Max partitions4 primary (or 3 + extended)128 (practically unlimited)
Max disk size2 TB9.4 ZB (zettabytes)
Boot modeBIOS / LegacyUEFI (required for UEFI boot)
RedundancySingle copy of tableBackup copy at end of disk
When to useOld systems, small disksALWAYS for new installations

22.3 fdisk & gdisk — Partitioning Tools

CommandWhat It DoesNotes
fdisk -lList all disks and partitionsRead-only, safe
fdisk /dev/sdbOpen disk for MBR partitioningInteractive: n=new, d=delete, w=write, q=quit
gdisk /dev/sdbOpen disk for GPT partitioningSame commands as fdisk
parted /dev/sdb printList partitions (both MBR/GPT)Non-interactive mode
parted /dev/sdb mklabel gptCreate GPT partition tableDestroys all existing data!
🚀 Walkthrough: Adding a New Data Disk for Anihpj
  # SCENARIO: Cloud provider just attached a new 200GB volume to your VM.
  # Goal: Create a single GPT partition spanning the entire disk for anihpj data.

  # Step 1: Find the new disk
  $ lsblk
  NAME   MAJ:MIN RM   SIZE RO TYPE MOUNTPOINT
  sda      8:0    0    50G  0 disk
  ├─sda1   8:1    0   512M  0 part /boot/efi
  ├─sda2   8:2    0     2G  0 part /boot
  └─sda3   8:3    0  47.5G  0 part
  sdb      8:16   0   200G  0 disk          ← NEW DISK! No partitions yet.

  # Step 2: Create GPT partition table
  $ sudo parted /dev/sdb mklabel gpt
  $ sudo parted /dev/sdb print
  Partition Table: gpt                           ← GPT is now active

  # Step 3: Create a single partition using the ENTIRE disk
  $ sudo parted /dev/sdb mkpart primary ext4 0% 100%
  $ sudo parted /dev/sdb print
  Number  Start   End     Size    File system  Name     Flags
   1      1049kB  200GB   200GB                primary

  # Step 4: Tell kernel to re-read the partition table
  $ sudo partprobe /dev/sdb
  $ lsblk /dev/sdb
  NAME   MAJ:MIN RM   SIZE RO TYPE MOUNTPOINT
  sdb      8:16   0   200G  0 disk
  └─sdb1   8:17   0   200G  0 part             ← PARTITION READY!

  # Step 5: Now ready for mkfs (Chapter 23) or pvcreate (Chapter 24)

22.4 parted — Advanced Partitioning

CommandWhat It Does
parted /dev/sdb mkpart primary ext4 0% 50%Create partition using first 50% of disk
parted /dev/sdb mkpart primary ext4 50% 100%Create partition using remaining 50%
parted /dev/sdb rm 1Delete partition number 1
parted /dev/sdb resizepart 1 100%Grow partition 1 to fill disk
⚠️ ALWAYS verify the target disk before partitioning!

lsblk before any fdisk/parted command. Writing a partition table destroys existing data. A single typo (/dev/sda instead of /dev/sdb) can wipe your OS. On production servers, take a backup before partitioning.

🔬 Disk I/O Schedulers — Optimizing for Your Workload

The I/O scheduler decides the ORDER in which disk read/write requests are processed. View current: cat /sys/block/sda/queue/scheduler. Common options: mq-deadline (default, good for SSDs — low latency), kyber (modern, latency-targeted), bfq (fairness, good for HDDs). Change: echo kyber > /sys/block/sda/queue/scheduler. For anihpj: use mq-deadline for the PostgreSQL SSD, bfq for the backup HDD.

🩺 SMART — Monitor Disk Health Before It Fails

SMART (Self-Monitoring, Analysis and Reporting Technology) predicts disk failures. smartctl -a /dev/sda shows: temperature, power-on hours, reallocated sectors, pending sectors. Key fields: Reallocated_Sector_Ct > 0 → disk is failing. smartctl -t short /dev/sda runs a quick self-test. smartctl -l selftest /dev/sda shows results. Production tip: configure smartd to email alerts on disk health changes.

💿

Spinning platters. Cheap per TB. Slow random I/O. Good for backups.

Flash storage. 500MB/s. No moving parts. Standard for servers.

Chapter 22 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
List all block devices with their filesystem types and mount points. Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
lsblk -f
Q2
Create a GPT partition table on /dev/sdb and verify it was created.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo parted /dev/sdb mklabel gpt
sudo parted /dev/sdb print
Q3
What's the maximum disk size MBR supports? Why is this a problem today?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

MBR supports a maximum of 2 TB because it uses 32-bit sector addressing (2^32 × 512 bytes = 2TB). Any disk larger than 2TB MUST use GPT. This is a problem because modern drives (4TB, 8TB, 16TB+) are common.

Q4
Create a single partition on /dev/sdb that uses the entire disk using parted.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo parted /dev/sdb mkpart primary ext4 0% 100%
Q5
What command shows the UUID of all storage devices? Why use UUID instead of /dev/sdb1 in fstab?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
blkid

UUIDs are unique and persistent. /dev/sdb1 can change if you add/remove disks — the kernel assigns device names in order. Using UUID in /etc/fstab ensures the correct filesystem is always mounted regardless of device name.

Q6
Create a 200GB partition on /dev/sdb using fdisk. Walk through the interactive steps.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo fdisk /dev/sdb
# Interactive:
n → new partition
p → primary
1 → partition number
Enter → default first sector
+200G → size
t → change type (83=Linux)
w → write and exit
sudo partprobe /dev/sdb  # tell kernel to re-read
Q7
What's the difference between a primary, extended, and logical partition in MBR?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

MBR allows 4 primary partitions. If you need more than 4, one primary becomes an extended partition (a container), which holds multiple logical partitions. Only primary and logical partitions can hold filesystems. GPT eliminates this complexity with 128 equal partitions.

Q8
How do you check if a disk is using MBR or GPT partition table?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo parted /dev/sda print | grep "Partition Table"
# OR:
sudo fdisk -l /dev/sda | grep "Disklabel type"
# Output: "Partition Table: gpt" or "Disklabel type: dos" (MBR)
Q9
Delete partition 2 on /dev/sdb using fdisk. What happens to the data?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo fdisk /dev/sdb
# Interactive: d → 2 → w
# The partition table entry is removed. Data still exists on disk
# but the filesystem is no longer accessible. Data recovery is
# possible if you recreate the partition at the EXACT same boundaries.
Q10
You added a new disk /dev/sdc. How do you make the kernel recognize it without rebooting?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Rescan SCSI bus:
echo "- - -" | sudo tee /sys/class/scsi_host/host*/scan
# OR for a specific disk:
sudo partprobe /dev/sdc
# Then verify:
lsblk

Chapter 23: Filesystems — Formatting and Mounting Storage

A partition is just raw space until you put a filesystem on it. This chapter covers Linux filesystem types (ext4, xfs, btrfs), how to format partitions with mkfs, mount them with mount, make mounts permanent with /etc/fstab, and check/repair filesystems with fsck.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours📁 Storage

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Compare ext4, xfs, and btrfs — when to use each
  • ✅ Format partitions with mkfs.ext4 and mkfs.xfs
  • ✅ Mount and unmount filesystems with mount/umount
  • ✅ Configure persistent mounts in /etc/fstab
  • ✅ Check and repair filesystems with fsck and xfs_repair
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Storage Management 20%
🎯 Filesystems on the LFCS Exam

You'll be asked to format a partition, mount it, and add it to /etc/fstab. The exam tests both ext4 (Ubuntu default) and xfs (Rocky default). Key commands: mkfs.ext4, mount, umount, df -h. Always test fstab with mount -a before rebooting!

23.1 Linux Filesystem Types

FilesystemBest ForMax SizeKey Feature
ext4General purpose, OS root1 EB (filesystem)Journaling, stable, default on Ubuntu
xfsLarge files, databases, high I/O8 EBHigh performance, default on RHEL/Rocky
btrfsAdvanced features needed16 EBCopy-on-write, snapshots, compression
vfatCross-platform (USB, EFI)2 TBCompatible with Windows/macOS
tmpfsTemporary files in RAMRAM sizeVolatile (lost on reboot), fast

23.2 mkfs — Formatting Partitions

CommandWhat It Does
mkfs.ext4 /dev/sdb1Format partition as ext4
mkfs.xfs /dev/sdb1Format partition as xfs
mkfs.ext4 -L "anihpj-data" /dev/sdb1Format with label (human-readable name)
tune2fs -l /dev/sda1View ext4 filesystem info (creation date, mount count, features)
xfs_info /dev/sdb1View xfs filesystem info
Reading Filesystem Metadata: tune2fs -l & xfs_info Output
  $ sudo tune2fs -l /dev/sda2 | head -20
  Filesystem volume name:   
  Last mounted on:          /boot
  Filesystem UUID:          a1b2c3d4-5678-90ab-cdef-1234567890ab
  Filesystem magic number:  0xEF53
  Filesystem revision #:    1 (dynamic)
  Filesystem features:      has_journal ext_attr resize_inode dir_index
  Filesystem state:         clean
  Errors behavior:          Continue
  Filesystem OS type:       Linux
  Inode count:              131072
  Block count:              524288
  Last mount time:          Mon Jun  9 08:15:22 2026
  Last write time:          Mon Jun  9 08:15:22 2026
  Mount count:              42               ← mounted 42 times since last fsck
  Maximum mount count:      -1               ← fsck disabled (modern default)

  $ sudo xfs_info /dev/vg-data/media
  meta-data=/dev/vg-data/media  isize=512    agcount=4, agsize=6553600 blks
  data     =                     bsize=4096   blocks=26214400
  naming   =version 2            bsize=4096   ascii-ci=0, ftype=1
  log      =internal             bsize=4096   blocks=12800, version=2
  realtime =none                 extsz=4096   blocks=0, rtextents=0

  KEY INSIGHTS:
  - tune2fs shows mount count, last check, features like has_journal
  - xfs_info shows AG (Allocation Groups) — xfs splits disk into
    independent regions for parallel I/O. More AGs = more parallelism.
  - "Filesystem state: clean" = no fsck needed on next boot
🔬 /proc and /sys — The Kernel's Windows into Your System

Not all "filesystems" are on disk. /proc is a virtual filesystem (procfs) exposing kernel and process information as files. Every process has a directory: /proc/[PID]/ — check /proc/1/cmdline (how init was started), /proc/cpuinfo (CPU details), /proc/meminfo (detailed RAM breakdown), /proc/mounts (currently mounted filesystems — more accurate than /etc/mtab). /sys (sysfs) exposes kernel objects, drivers, and hardware: /sys/block/sda/ (disk properties), /sys/class/net/eth0/ (network interface), /sys/kernel/ (kernel parameters). Key difference: /proc is mostly read-only process/kernel info. /sys is more hardware/driver focused and some entries are writable (e.g., echo 1 > /sys/block/sda/device/rescan). Both are created in RAM at boot — they don't exist on any physical disk.

⚠️ mkfs DESTROYS all data on the partition!

Double-check the device name before running mkfs. Use lsblk to verify. There is NO undo. On production: blkid first to confirm the partition is empty/expected.

🔬 How Journaling Works — Why ext4/xfs Survive Power Failures

A journaling filesystem writes changes to a journal (circular log) BEFORE writing to the main filesystem. Sequence: (1) Write intended change to journal, (2) Write to main FS, (3) Mark journal entry complete. If power fails after step 1, on boot the journal is replayed — unfinished changes are completed. If power fails before step 1, nothing is lost. This is why journaling filesystems don't need fsck after a crash. ext4 journal modes: data=ordered (default, metadata journaled, data ordered), data=journal (all data journaled, slowest, safest), data=writeback (metadata only, fastest, riskiest).

🔬 Inodes — The Filesystem's Index Cards

Every file and directory has an inode — a data structure storing: permissions, owner, size, timestamps, and pointers to data blocks. NOT the filename (that's in the directory). df -i shows inode usage. Running out of inodes means "no space left" even if df -h shows free space — you can't create new files. Common cause: millions of tiny files (cache, emails, temp). Fix: delete old files, or recreate FS with more inodes: mkfs.ext4 -N 10000000 /dev/sdb1.

23.3 mount and umount — Using Filesystems

CommandWhat It Does
mount /dev/sdb1 /mnt/dataTemporary mount (lost on reboot)
mount -t ext4 /dev/sdb1 /mntSpecify filesystem type explicitly
mount -o ro /dev/sdb1 /mntRead-only mount (safe for inspection)
umount /mnt/dataUnmount (must not be in use)
umount -l /mnt/dataLazy unmount — detach now, cleanup later
df -hTShow all mounted filesystems with type and usage
findmnt /mnt/dataDetailed mount info for specific directory
🔧 Scenario: Mount the Anihpj PostgreSQL Data Disk
  # SCENARIO: Your anihpj server just got a freshly formatted 100GB xfs disk
  # for PostgreSQL data at /dev/sdb1. Mount it and make it database-ready.

  # Step 1: Create mount point
  $ sudo mkdir -p /var/lib/postgresql

  # Step 2: Get the UUID (for permanent fstab entry)
  $ sudo blkid /dev/sdb1
  /dev/sdb1: UUID="b8f3c9d1-4567-89ab-cdef-0123456789ab" TYPE="xfs"

  # Step 3: Temporary mount (test it works)
  $ sudo mount /dev/sdb1 /var/lib/postgresql
  $ df -hT /var/lib/postgresql
  Filesystem     Type  Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
  /dev/sdb1      xfs   100G   33M  100G   1% /var/lib/postgresql

  # Step 4: Set ownership for PostgreSQL
  $ sudo chown postgres:postgres /var/lib/postgresql
  $ sudo chmod 700 /var/lib/postgresql

  # Step 5: Make permanent — add to /etc/fstab
  $ echo "UUID=b8f3c9d1-4567-89ab-cdef-0123456789ab /var/lib/postgresql xfs defaults,nofail 0 2" | sudo tee -a /etc/fstab

  # Step 6: Test fstab before rebooting
  $ sudo mount -a          # Remounts everything from fstab — if silent, it's correct!

  # Step 7: Verify after reboot simulation
  $ findmnt /var/lib/postgresql
  TARGET               SOURCE    FSTYPE OPTIONS
  /var/lib/postgresql  /dev/sdb1 xfs    rw,relatime,attr2,inode64,noquota

23.4 /etc/fstab — Persistent Mounts

Annotated /etc/fstab for Anihpj Server
  #                        
  UUID=xxxx-yyyy        /             ext4    errors=remount-ro  0     1
  UUID=aaaa-bbbb        /boot         ext4    defaults           0     2
  UUID=cccc-dddd        /boot/efi     vfat    umask=0077         0     1

  # anihpj PostgreSQL data disk
  UUID=1234-5678  /var/lib/postgresql  ext4   defaults,nofail   0     2

  # anihpj media uploads (LVM, growable)
  UUID=8765-4321  /var/www/anihpj/media  ext4  defaults,nofail  0     2

  FIELD EXPLANATION:
  ① device: UUID (preferred) or /dev/sdb1 or LABEL=mylabel
  ② mountpoint: where to attach in the filesystem tree
  ③ type: ext4, xfs, vfat, swap, nfs, etc.
  ④ options: defaults (rw,suid,dev,exec,auto,nouser,async)
     nofail = don't halt boot if this drive is missing
  ⑤ dump: 0=don't dump, 1=dump (for backup utility)
  ⑥ pass: fsck order — 0=skip, 1=root first, 2=others
🔬 Mount Options Deep-Dive — Performance and Security

Mount options control HOW a filesystem behaves. Key options beyond defaults (which = rw,suid,dev,exec,auto,nouser,async): Performance: noatime (don't update access time — huge I/O savings, especially for databases and web servers. relatime is the modern default — updates atime only if older than mtime). data=ordered (ext4 default — journal metadata, data written first). Security: nosuid (ignore setuid bits — critical for /tmp and web-accessible mounts). noexec (prevent binary execution — use on /tmp and upload directories). nodev (ignore device files — prevents creating /dev/sda inside a mount). Anihpj example: /var/www/anihpj/media should be mounted with defaults,nosuid,noexec,nodev to prevent uploaded files from being executed or creating device nodes. /tmp should have: defaults,nosuid,noexec,nodev,noatime.

🔧 Before Rebooting: Always test fstab!

sudo mount -a mounts all entries in /etc/fstab. If it succeeds silently, your fstab is correct. If it errors, fix before rebooting — a broken fstab can prevent the system from booting!

23.5 Checking and Repairing Filesystems

CommandWhat It DoesWhen to Use
fsck /dev/sdb1Check and repair filesystemSystem won't mount; after power failure
fsck -f /dev/sdb1Force check even if marked cleanSuspected corruption but fsck skips
e2fsck -p /dev/sda1Auto-repair ext4 (no prompts)Quick fix for known ext4 issues
xfs_repair /dev/sdb1Repair xfs filesystemXFS corruption (must be unmounted!)
xfs_check /dev/sdb1Check xfs without repairDiagnostic before attempting repair
⚠️ ALWAYS unmount before running fsck!

Running fsck on a mounted filesystem can cause severe corruption. Boot into recovery mode or use a live USB if you need to check the root filesystem. umount /mnt/data first, then fsck /dev/sdb1.

📊 Chapter 23 Visual Summary

📁

📌

🩺
fsckCheck/repair unmounted FS. e2fsck for ext4. xfs_repair for xfs. ⚠️ unmount first!

Q5
What is the difference between ext4 and xfs? When would you choose xfs for anihpj?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

ext4 is the general-purpose Linux filesystem — stable, well-tested, good for OS and small-to-medium files. xfs is optimized for large files and high concurrency — better for databases, large media files, and backup storage. For anihpj: use xfs for /var/lib/postgresql (database) and /backups (large sequential writes). Use ext4 for general directories.

Q6

✅ Answer

# Output: /dev/sdb1: SGI XFS filesystem data ...
# OR: /dev/sdb1: Linux rev 1.0 ext4 filesystem data ...
Q7
A filesystem won't unmount because it's "busy." How do you find what's using it and force unmount?

✅ Answer

sudo lsof /mnt/data
# OR:
sudo fuser -v /mnt/data

# Force unmount:
sudo umount -l /mnt/data   # lazy unmount
# OR:
sudo umount -f /mnt/data   # force (NFS only)
Q8
What does the "pass" field in /etc/fstab control? What values are valid?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

The 6th field controls fsck order at boot. Values: 0 = don't check. 1 = check first (root filesystem only!). 2 = check after root (all other filesystems). Multiple filesystems can have pass=2 — they're checked in parallel.

Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo mount -a

This mounts ALL entries from /etc/fstab. If it completes silently, fstab is correct. If there's an error — fix the entry before rebooting! A broken fstab can make the system unbootable.

Chapter 24: LVM — Logical Volume Management

LVM is the storage administrator's superpower. It abstracts physical disks into flexible pools of storage that can be resized, combined, and snapshotted — all without unmounting or reformatting. This chapter covers the complete LVM workflow: creating Physical Volumes, pooling them into Volume Groups, carving out Logical Volumes, and extending them live when your anihpj uploads directory runs out of space.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🗄️ Storage

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the LVM layer cake: PV → VG → LV → Filesystem → Mount
  • ✅ Create complete LVM storage from scratch
  • ✅ Extend logical volumes online without downtime
  • ✅ Create and use LVM snapshots for safe migrations
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Storage Management 20%
🎯 LVM on the LFCS Exam

LVM is heavily tested. You'll be given raw disks and asked to create a complete LVM setup: PV → VG → LV → format → mount → fstab. Know the difference between extending the LV (lvextend) and growing the filesystem (resize2fs for ext4, xfs_growfs for xfs). They are TWO separate steps!

24.1 Why LVM? — The Flexibility Problem

The LVM Layer Cake — PV → VG → LV
  LOGICAL VOLUMES (what you actually use — /dev/vgname/lvname)
  ┌───────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐
  │  lv-root (50GB)   │  │  lv-data (20GB)│  │  lv-var (10GB)│
  └───────────────────┘  └────────────────┘  └───────────────┘
                         │
  VOLUME GROUP (the pool — ubuntu-vg)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Total: 80GB = /dev/sda3 (50GB) + /dev/sdb1 (30GB)    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                         │
  PHYSICAL VOLUMES (raw storage — pvcreate'd)
  ┌─────────────────────┐      ┌─────────────────────────┐
  │  /dev/sda3 (50GB)   │      │  /dev/sdb1 (30GB)       │
  └─────────────────────┘      └─────────────────────────┘
❌ Without LVM

Fixed partitions. Resizing requires: unmount → backup → delete partition → create larger → restore. DOWNTIME. Risk of data loss.

✅ With LVM

Flexible volumes. Resizing: add disk to VG → lvextend → resize2fs. ONLINE. No downtime. Minutes, not hours.

24.2 Creating LVM from Scratch

StepCommandWhat It Does
1. Initializepvcreate /dev/sdb1Mark partition as LVM Physical Volume
2. Create Poolvgcreate anihpj-vg /dev/sdb1Create Volume Group (storage pool)
3. Create Volumelvcreate -L 100G -n data anihpj-vgCreate 100GB Logical Volume named "data"
4. Formatmkfs.ext4 /dev/anihpj-vg/dataFormat the new LV
5. Mountmount /dev/anihpj-vg/data /dataMake it usable
6. Make PermanentAdd to /etc/fstabMount automatically at boot
🔧 Useful LVM Inspection Commands

pvs / pvdisplay — list Physical Volumes. vgs / vgdisplay — list Volume Groups. lvs / lvdisplay — list Logical Volumes. The short forms (pvs, vgs, lvs) are scriptable; the long forms show detailed info.

24.3 Extending and Resizing LVM Volumes

Scenario: Anihpj Uploads Disk at 90% — Extend Without Downtime
  # 1. Add new physical disk and create a partition
  sudo parted /dev/sdc mklabel gpt
  sudo parted /dev/sdc mkpart primary 0% 100%

  # 2. Initialize as PV and add to the volume group
  sudo pvcreate /dev/sdc1
  sudo vgextend anihpj-vg /dev/sdc1

  # 3. Extend the logical volume by 200GB
  sudo lvextend -L +200G /dev/anihpj-vg/data

  # 4. Grow the filesystem (depends on type):
  sudo resize2fs /dev/anihpj-vg/data        # ext4 — unmounted or mounted
  # OR:
  sudo xfs_growfs /var/www/anihpj/media     # xfs — MUST be mounted!

  RESULT: /media grew from 90% full to 30% full — zero downtime!

24.4 LVM Snapshots — Point-in-Time Copies

CommandWhat It Does
lvcreate -L 2G -s -n data-snap /dev/anihpj-vg/dataCreate a 2GB snapshot of the data LV
mount /dev/anihpj-vg/data-snap /mnt/snapshotMount the snapshot (read-only preferred)
lvremove /dev/anihpj-vg/data-snapDelete the snapshot when done
🔬 LVM Striping & Mirroring — Performance and Redundancy

Beyond basic linear LVs, LVM supports RAID-like configurations. Striping (RAID 0 — performance): lvcreate --type striped -i 2 -L 50G -n data-fast anihpj-vg /dev/sdb1 /dev/sdc1 — stripes data across 2 PVs. Reads and writes are parallelized → near-double throughput. Best for: temporary/scratch data where speed matters more than safety. ⚠️ If ANY PV fails, ALL data is lost (no redundancy). Mirroring (RAID 1 — redundancy): lvcreate --type raid1 -m1 -L 50G -n data-safe anihpj-vg /dev/sdb1 /dev/sdc1 — mirrors data to 2 PVs. ⚠️ Uses more space (50GB LV needs 100GB raw). Can survive a single disk failure. Verify with lvs -a -o name,attr,size — mirrored LVs show 'r' attribute. Striping + Mirroring (RAID 10): lvcreate --type raid10 -i 2 -m1 -L 50G -n data-best anihpj-vg /dev/sd{b,c,d,e}1 — both fast AND redundant, needs 4+ PVs. Anihpj: Use linear LVs (default) for most data. Use RAID1 for critical DB volumes if you have spare disks. Use striped for /tmp (speed, data is disposable).

🔧 Use Case: Safe Django Database Migrations

Before running python manage.py migrate: create an LVM snapshot of the PostgreSQL data LV. If the migration corrupts data, unmount the production LV and copy from the snapshot. Once verified: lvremove the snapshot. The snapshot only needs enough space to hold CHANGES during its lifetime — not a full copy of the original.

🔬 Thin Provisioning — Overcommit Storage, Allocate on Demand

Thin pools let you create LVs larger than the physical storage you have — storage is allocated as data is written, not upfront. Create: lvcreate --type thin-pool -L 100G vg-data/thinpool, then lvcreate --type thin -V 500G --thinpool vg-data/thinpool -n thin-lv. The LV shows 500GB but only uses what's written. Monitor: lvs -a shows Data% and Meta%. ⚠️ If the thin pool fills up, ALL thin LVs in it become read-only. Always monitor and extend before 80%.

⚡ LVM Cache — SSD-Accelerated HDD Storage

Combine an SSD (fast) with an HDD (large) using LVM cache: frequently accessed data lives on SSD, bulk data on HDD. lvcreate --type cache --cachemode writethrough -L 50G -n cache-lv vg-data/data /dev/nvme0n1p1. This gives near-SSD performance for your HDD-hosted LVs. Perfect for: PostgreSQL data on large HDD with NVMe cache, or media storage with hot-files accelerated.

📊 Chapter 24 Visual Summary

🧱
PV → VG → LVpvcreate→vgcreate→lvcreate. Physical→Pool→Logical. Abstract hardware from usage.
📈
Extend Onlinevgextend→lvextend→resize2fs/xfs_growfs. Grow storage without unmounting.
📸
SnapshotsPoint-in-time copy. Safe migrations. Delete with lvremove when done.

Chapter 24 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Create an LVM setup: partition /dev/sdb1 as PV, create VG "vg-data", create 50GB LV "media", format as ext4.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo pvcreate /dev/sdb1
sudo vgcreate vg-data /dev/sdb1
sudo lvcreate -L 50G -n media vg-data
sudo mkfs.ext4 /dev/vg-data/media
Q2
Add a new disk /dev/sdc1 to the existing VG and extend the LV by 100GB. Write all commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo pvcreate /dev/sdc1
sudo vgextend vg-data /dev/sdc1
sudo lvextend -L +100G /dev/vg-data/media
sudo resize2fs /dev/vg-data/media
Q3
What's the difference between lvextend and resize2fs? Why are both needed?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

lvextend grows the block device (the LV) — it makes the container bigger. resize2fs grows the filesystem INSIDE the container to fill the new space. Both are needed because the container and its contents are separate layers. For xfs: xfs_growfs replaces resize2fs and requires the filesystem be mounted.

Q4
Create an LVM snapshot named "pre-migration" of the data LV before running a database migration.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo lvcreate -L 2G -s -n pre-migration /dev/vg-data/data
Q5
How do you view all Physical Volumes, Volume Groups, and Logical Volumes on the system?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo pvs && sudo vgs && sudo lvs
# OR for detailed info:
sudo pvdisplay && sudo vgdisplay && sudo lvdisplay
Q6
You need to reduce an ext4 LV from 100GB to 50GB. What are the steps and why is this risky?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# 1. UNMOUNT first (required for shrink)
sudo umount /data
# 2. Check filesystem
sudo e2fsck -f /dev/vg-data/media
# 3. Shrink filesystem FIRST (before LV!)
sudo resize2fs /dev/vg-data/media 50G
# 4. Then shrink the LV
sudo lvreduce -L 50G /dev/vg-data/media
# 5. Remount
sudo mount /data

Risky: If you shrink the LV BEFORE the filesystem, you truncate data. ALWAYS shrink filesystem first. Backup before any shrink operation. xfs CANNOT be shrunk — only ext4 supports online/offline shrinking.

Q7
Remove a physical volume /dev/sdc1 from the volume group. What must you do first?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# 1. Move data off the PV first:
sudo pvmove /dev/sdc1
# 2. Then remove from VG:
sudo vgreduce vg-data /dev/sdc1
# 3. Remove PV label:
sudo pvremove /dev/sdc1

pvmove relocates all data to other PVs in the VG. If there's not enough free space, the command fails — you must add more storage first.

Q8
What happens when an LVM snapshot runs out of space? How do you prevent this?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

When a snapshot fills up, it becomes invalid (I/O errors on access) — the snapshot is automatically disabled. Prevention: (1) Allocate enough space for expected changes during the snapshot lifetime. (2) Monitor with lvs — the Data% column shows snapshot usage. (3) Auto-extend with /etc/lvm/lvm.conf: snapshot_autoextend_threshold = 70 and snapshot_autoextend_percent = 20.

Q9
Display detailed information about the volume group "anihpj-vg" including free space.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo vgdisplay anihpj-vg
# Key fields: VG Size, Free PE / Size (available for new LVs)
Q10
You extended an LV but forgot to grow the filesystem. User reports "disk full" despite df showing space. What happened?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

lvextend grows the block device (visible with lvs), but the filesystem inside it still uses the old size (visible with df -h). Fix: sudo resize2fs /dev/vg-name/lv-name (ext4) or sudo xfs_growfs /mountpoint (xfs). This is the #1 LVM mistake — always remember it's a TWO-STEP process.

🚀

LVM is powerful. In the cloud, storage evolves further:
