Chapter 25: Swap, NFS & Remote Storage

Not all storage is local. This chapter covers swap space (virtual memory on disk), NFS (network file sharing between Linux servers), and iSCSI (remote block storage). You'll learn when to use each, how to configure them, and how they fit into the anihpj storage strategy — including the critical 3-2-1 backup rule.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1 hour💾 Storage

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Create and manage swap space (partitions and files)
  • ✅ Set up NFS server and client for shared file access
  • ✅ Understand iSCSI for remote block storage
  • ✅ Apply the 3-2-1 backup strategy to anihpj
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Storage Management 20%
🎯 Swap & NFS on the LFCS Exam

You may be asked to create and enable swap, or configure an NFS client mount. Know both swap partition (mkswap) and swap file (dd + mkswap) approaches. For NFS: know the /etc/exports format, exportfs -a, and the client mount syntax.

25.1 SWAP — Virtual Memory Extension

MethodCommandBest For
Swap Partitionmkswap /dev/sdb2 && swapon /dev/sdb2Dedicated disk, predictable size
Swap Filedd if=/dev/zero of=/swapfile bs=1G count=8 && chmod 600 /swapfile && mkswap /swapfile && swapon /swapfileCloud VMs, no free partitions
View active swapswapon -s or free -hCheck what's currently used
Disable swapswapoff /dev/sdb2Remove swap before repartitioning
Persist in fstab/dev/sdb2 swap swap defaults 0 0Automatically enabled at boot

When to use swap: Always have some swap (1-2x RAM). It prevents OOM kills during memory spikes. Swapiness (/proc/sys/vm/swappiness, default 60): lower values prefer RAM, higher values prefer swap. For database servers: set swappiness=10 to keep PostgreSQL in RAM.

🔧 Real-World: "The Uploads Disk Is Full!" — Storage Emergency on Anihpj

Situation: Users can't upload images. df -h shows /media at 100%. You have a new 500GB disk (/dev/sdc) ready.

  1. Diagnose: df -h /media → 100% full. du -sh /media/* | sort -rh | head → images consuming 190GB.
  2. Add disk to LVM: pvcreate /dev/sdc1 && vgextend anihpj-vg /dev/sdc1
  3. Grow the volume ONLINE: lvextend -L +400G /dev/anihpj-vg/media && resize2fs /dev/anihpj-vg/media
  4. Verify: df -h /media → 450GB total, 42% used. Uploads work again!

Time elapsed: 3 minutes. Downtime: ZERO. This is why LVM exists.

25.2 NFS — Network File System (File Sharing)

NFS Server + Client Setup for Anihpj Media Uploads
  # === SERVER SIDE (nfs-server) ===
  sudo apt install nfs-kernel-server
  sudo mkdir -p /var/www/anihpj/media

  # /etc/exports — define what to share:
  /var/www/anihpj/media  192.168.1.0/24(rw,sync,no_subtree_check)

  sudo exportfs -a    # apply exports
  sudo exportfs -v    # verify

  # === CLIENT SIDE (app-server-1, app-server-2) ===
  sudo apt install nfs-common
  sudo mount -t nfs nfs-server:/var/www/anihpj/media /mnt/nfs-media

  # /etc/fstab (client — persistent):
  nfs-server:/var/www/anihpj/media  /mnt/nfs-media  nfs  defaults,_netdev  0  0
OptionMeaning
rwRead-write access
syncWrite immediately (safer, slower)
no_subtree_checkSkip verification (faster, default)
_netdev (client)Wait for network before mounting

25.3 iSCSI — Remote Block Storage

iSCSI presents a remote disk as a local block device (/dev/sdb). Unlike NFS (file-level), iSCSI is block-level — the client formats it with its own filesystem. Used for databases that need raw disk performance over the network.

📁 NFS — File-Level

Server manages the filesystem. Multiple clients can read/write the SAME files simultaneously. Simple to set up. Best for: shared media directories, home directories, config files.

💾 iSCSI — Block-Level

Client sees a raw disk and formats it with its OWN filesystem. Only ONE client can mount at a time (without cluster FS). Performs like a local disk. Best for: database storage, VM disk images.

CommandWhat It Does
iscsiadm -m discovery -t st -p 192.168.1.100Discover available iSCSI targets
iscsiadm -m node --loginConnect to discovered target
iscsiadm -m sessionShow active iSCSI sessions
NFS vs iSCSI vs Local Storage — Remote Storage Options
TechnologyTypeUse CaseLFCS Tested
NFSFile-level (NAS)Shared /media across web servers. Simple, mature.✅ mount, fstab, exports
iSCSIBlock-level (SAN)Remote disk for DB. Appears as /dev/sdb.✅ iscsiadm discovery
Local FSDirect-attach (DAS)PostgreSQL data, OS. Fastest, simplest.✅ mkfs, mount
🔬 NFS v3 vs v4 — Which Version Should You Use?

NFSv3: Older, simpler. Uses separate mount/lock/status protocols (portmapper/rpcbind required). Stateless-ish. UID/GID-based auth (no Kerberos). Good for simple LAN setups. NFSv4: Modern. Single protocol on port 2049 (no portmapper needed). Stateful (better caching, delegations). Supports Kerberos authentication (krb5p = encrypt ALL traffic). Pseudo-filesystem (exports appear under a single root). File locking is built-in (no separate lockd). For anihpj: Use NFSv4 with krb5p for production NFS. Commands: rpcinfo -p server shows RPC services. showmount -e server lists NFS exports (v3 only — v4 uses the pseudo-filesystem). The kernel negotiates the highest version automatically — check with nfsstat -m or mount | grep nfs.

🔬 autofs — Mount on Demand, Unmount When Idle

autofs mounts NFS/Samba shares automatically when accessed and unmounts them after a period of inactivity. Install: apt install autofs. Configure /etc/auto.master: /mnt/nfs /etc/auto.nfs --timeout=60. Then /etc/auto.nfs: backups -rw nfs-server:/backups/anihpj. Now ls /mnt/nfs/backups triggers the mount automatically. systemctl restart autofs. Perfect for: backup mounts that are only used during nightly jobs.

25.4 Disk Usage Analysis — Finding What Eats Your Space

CommandWhat It DoesExample
df -hTDisk space per filesystem with typedf -hT /var → check specific mount
du -sh /var/*Summary size of each subdirectorydu -sh /var/* | sort -rh | head -10
du -ah /var/logShow ALL files (not just dirs)du -ah /var/log | grep "\.log$"
du --max-depth=1 -h /One level deep, human-readableGreat for finding what fills root
ncdu /Interactive ncurses browserncdu /var → arrow keys, d=delete
find / -type f -size +100MFiles larger than 100MBfind /var -type f -size +1G -exec ls -lh {} \;
find / -type f -mtime +30 -size +50MOld large files (candidates for cleanup)Files >50MB, unmodified in 30+ days

Common disk hogs in production: (1) Log files — /var/log/syslog, journald (journalctl --disk-usage). (2) Package cache — apt clean or dnf clean all. (3) Docker images — docker system prune -a. (4) Old kernels — dpkg --list | grep linux-image. (5) Core dumps — /var/lib/systemd/coredump/. (6) Database WAL files — pg_wal/ in PostgreSQL.

🔧 Pro Tip: Find Today's Largest New Files

find / -type f -mtime -1 -size +10M -exec ls -lh {} \; 2>/dev/null | sort -k5 -rh | head -20 — find files created/modified in the last 24 hours over 10MB, sorted by size. This catches runaway log files, unexpected core dumps, or a forgotten mysqldump filling /tmp.

💡 Anihpj Storage Strategy: PostgreSQL data → LOCAL ext4 on LVM (fast!). User uploads → LOCAL ext4 on LVM (growable). Backups → NFS to NAS (off-server safety). Static files → LOCAL (Nginx cache). 3-2-1 Backup Rule: 3 copies of data, 2 different media types, 1 copy off-site. Anihpj example: Copy 1 = production LVM, Copy 2 = local /backups disk (nightly pg_dump), Copy 3 = NFS mount to NAS (rsync'd after backup).

📊 Chapter 25 Visual Summary

🔄
Swapmkswap + swapon. Partition or file. swappiness controls RAM vs swap balance.
📡
NFSServer: /etc/exports + exportfs -a. Client: mount -t nfs. _netdev in fstab.
💾
iSCSIBlock-level remote disk. iscsiadm discovery + login. Appears as /dev/sdX.
🛡️
3-2-1 Backup3 copies, 2 media types, 1 off-site. Production + local backup + NFS/cloud.

Chapter 25 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Create a 4GB swap file at /swapfile, enable it, and make it permanent in /etc/fstab.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo dd if=/dev/zero of=/swapfile bs=1G count=4
sudo chmod 600 /swapfile
sudo mkswap /swapfile
sudo swapon /swapfile
# Add to /etc/fstab:
/swapfile  swap  swap  defaults  0  0
Q2
Configure an NFS export for /data to be accessible read-write from the 10.0.0.0/24 network.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Add to /etc/exports:
/data  10.0.0.0/24(rw,sync,no_subtree_check)
# Apply:
sudo exportfs -a
Q3
Mount an NFS share from server 192.168.1.50:/shared to /mnt/nfs. Write the command and fstab entry.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo mount -t nfs 192.168.1.50:/shared /mnt/nfs
# fstab entry:
192.168.1.50:/shared  /mnt/nfs  nfs  defaults,_netdev  0  0
Q4
What's the difference between NFS and iSCSI? When would you use each for anihpj?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

NFS is file-level (NAS) — the server manages the filesystem, clients read/write files. Use for: sharing /media uploads across multiple web servers. iSCSI is block-level (SAN) — the client sees a raw disk and formats it with its own filesystem. Use for: giving a database server a remote disk that performs like a local one.

Q5
Check how much swap is currently in use and which devices are being used for swap.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
swapon -s
# OR:
free -h
Q6
What is swappiness? How do you check the current value and change it to 10 for a database server?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
cat /proc/sys/vm/swappiness   # check current (default 60)
sudo sysctl vm.swappiness=10   # change now (temporary)
# Permanent:
echo "vm.swappiness=10" | sudo tee -a /etc/sysctl.conf

Lower values prefer RAM over swap. For database servers, 10 keeps PostgreSQL in RAM and only swaps under extreme pressure.

Q7
What NFS export option ensures data is written to disk before acknowledging the client?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

sync — the server acknowledges writes only after data is committed to stable storage. Without it, async is the default, which is faster but risks data loss if the server crashes. Always use sync for production data.

Q8
Discover iSCSI targets on 192.168.1.100 and connect to them. Write the commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo iscsiadm -m discovery -t st -p 192.168.1.100
sudo iscsiadm -m node --login
# Verify:
sudo iscsiadm -m session
lsblk  # new disk should appear
Q9
Explain the 3-2-1 backup rule. How would you implement it for anihpj's PostgreSQL database?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

3 copies of data: Copy 1 = production LVM (live DB). Copy 2 = local backup disk (nightly pg_dump). Copy 3 = NFS mount to NAS (rsync'd after backup). 2 different media types: local SSD + network NAS (different failure modes). 1 copy off-site: NAS is in a different building/availability zone.

Q10
How do you disable all swap on the system? When would you need to do this?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo swapoff -a   # disable all swap
# When: before repartitioning a swap disk, or to force
# the kernel to free RAM by killing processes (testing OOM).
# To re-enable: sudo swapon -a (reads fstab)

🗓️ Day in the Life: How Chapters 22-25 Work Together

From provisioning a new disk to ensuring backups are safe — here's how storage management forms a complete workflow:

💿 9:00
Provision Disk
Ch 22: fdisk/parted

Cloud provider attached 500GB volume. lsblk confirms /dev/sdc. parted /dev/sdc mklabel gpt && mkpart primary 0% 100%

🗄️ 9:10
Add to LVM
Ch 24: pvcreate + vgextend

pvcreate /dev/sdc1 && vgextend anihpj-vg /dev/sdc1 — storage pool grows to 800GB.

📈 9:15
Extend Volume
Ch 24: lvextend

lvextend -L +200G /dev/anihpj-vg/media && xfs_growfs /media — uploads now have room.

📁 9:20
Format & Mount
Ch 23: mkfs + fstab

mkfs.xfs /dev/anihpj-vg/backups → add to fstab → mount -a. Done.

📡 9:30
Setup NFS Share
Ch 25: exports

/etc/exports: /backups 10.0.0.0/24(rw,sync) → exportfs -a. Backup server mounts it.

✅ 9:45
Verify Everything
Ch 22-25: All Tools

df -hT, vgs, lvs, swapon -s, showmount -e — all green. Storage day complete.

Chapter 26: SSH — Secure Remote Access

SSH is the sysadmin's lifeline to every Linux server. It encrypts ALL traffic between you and your anihpj server — login credentials, commands, file transfers, even database queries tunneled through it. This chapter covers the SSH protocol, server configuration (sshd_config), key-based authentication, port forwarding, client config files, and production hardening.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔐 Security

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand the SSH protocol — encryption, authentication, integrity
  • ✅ Install and configure sshd with secure production settings
  • ✅ Set up SSH key-based authentication (Ed25519)
  • ✅ Use SSH port forwarding for secure tunnels
  • ✅ Harden SSH: disable root login, disable passwords, change port
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Service Configuration 20%
🎯 SSH on the LFCS Exam

You'll need to configure sshd, set up key-based auth, and troubleshoot connection issues. Key files: /etc/ssh/sshd_config (server), ~/.ssh/config (client), ~/.ssh/authorized_keys (allowed keys). Always test config with sshd -t before restarting! Wrong permissions on ~/.ssh (must be 700) or authorized_keys (must be 600) are the #1 cause of key auth failures.

26.1 SSH Server (sshd_config)

How SSH Works — The Encrypted Handshake
  CLIENT (your laptop)                    SERVER (anihpj production)
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ① TCP CONNECT to port 22
     ─────────────────────────────────────▶  sshd listening on :22

  ② VERSION EXCHANGE
     "SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_9.3"  ───────────▶  "SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_9.3"
     (agree on protocol version)

  ③ KEY EXCHANGE (Diffie-Hellman)
     ◀══════════════════════════════════▶  Both sides compute a
         (mathematical agreement)           SHARED SESSION KEY
         🔑 Session key is NEVER             without ever sending
            transmitted over network!        it across the network

  ④ SERVER AUTHENTICATION
     ◀───────────────────────────────────  Server presents its
         "Here's my HOST KEY"              HOST KEY fingerprint.
         (you verify on first connect)     Stored in ~/.ssh/known_hosts

  ⑤ USER AUTHENTICATION
     Method A: Password
       "password: ******"  ──────────────▶  Check against /etc/shadow
     Method B: PUBLIC KEY (recommended!)
       "I'll prove I have the             ◀  "Here's a random challenge,
        private key for this                encrypt it with user's public key"
        public key in authorized_keys"

  ⑥ 🔒 ENCRYPTED SESSION ESTABLISHED
     All subsequent data encrypted with AES-256 / ChaCha20
     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  🔐 EVERYTHING ENCRYPTED: commands, output, files, TCP │
     │     tunnels. Even if someone snoops, they see garbage.  │
     └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
File / CommandWhat It IsKey Directives
/etc/ssh/sshd_configSSH daemon (server) configPort, PermitRootLogin, PasswordAuthentication
systemctl status sshdCheck if SSH server is runningActive: active (running)
sshd -tTest config syntax before reloadingNo output = OK. ALWAYS run this first!
systemctl reload sshdApply config changes without dropping connectionsExisting sessions survive reload
Complete sshd_config for Anihpj Production Server
  # /etc/ssh/sshd_config — HARDENED configuration for anihpj
  Port 22                          # Default port (consider changing to reduce noise)
  Protocol 2                       # SSH protocol version 2 ONLY (v1 is insecure)
  HostKey /etc/ssh/ssh_host_ed25519_key   # Modern, fast, secure key type

  # AUTHENTICATION — KEY-ONLY IN PRODUCTION
  PermitRootLogin no               # NEVER allow direct root SSH login!
  PasswordAuthentication no         # Disable password login (key-only)
  PubkeyAuthentication yes          # Enable public key authentication
  ChallengeResponseAuthentication no # Disable keyboard-interactive

  # SESSION LIMITS
  MaxAuthTries 3                    # Disconnect after 3 failed attempts
  MaxSessions 10                    # Max simultaneous sessions per connection
  ClientAliveInterval 300          # Send keepalive every 5 min (detect dead clients)
  ClientAliveCountMax 3            # Disconnect after 3 missed keepalives (15 min)

  # ACCESS CONTROL
  AllowUsers anihpj-deploy admin   # WHITELIST: only these users can SSH in
  # DenyUsers  baduser             # BLACKLIST alternative (AllowUsers is stronger)

  # SECURITY HARDENING
  X11Forwarding no                 # Disable GUI forwarding (not needed on server)
  PermitTunnel no                  # Disable tunneling unless needed
  AllowTcpForwarding no            # Disable port forwarding unless needed
  PermitUserEnvironment no         # Prevent users from setting env vars via SSH

  # LOGGING
  SyslogFacility AUTH              # Log to /var/log/auth.log
  LogLevel VERBOSE                 # Log connection attempts (helps fail2ban)

  # After editing: sudo sshd -t && sudo systemctl reload sshd

26.2 SSH Key Management

CommandWhat It DoesWhen to Use
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "anihpj-deploy"Generate Ed25519 key pair (recommended!)Creating new keys — faster & more secure than RSA
ssh-keygen -t rsa -b 4096 -C "legacy"Generate RSA 4096-bit key pairCompatibility with old systems
ssh-copy-id user@serverCopy public key to server's authorized_keysFirst-time setup — easiest method
cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pubView your public key (safe to share)Manual key sharing or pasting into cloud console
ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519Add private key to SSH agent (cache passphrase)Avoid typing passphrase repeatedly
⚠️ SSH Key Permissions are CRITICAL!

Wrong permissions = SSH silently ignores your keys. Must be: chmod 700 ~/.ssh (directory — owner only). chmod 600 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 (private key — owner read/write only). chmod 644 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub (public key — world-readable is fine). chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys (on server — owner read/write only). If SSH key auth fails: tail -f /var/log/auth.log on the server — it will tell you EXACTLY which file has wrong permissions.

🔬 Ed25519 vs RSA — Why Ed25519 Wins

Ed25519 keys are: (1) Faster — 256-bit keys with same security as 3072-bit RSA. (2) Smaller — public key is 68 chars vs 700+ for RSA. (3) More secure — resistant to side-channel attacks that affect RSA. (4) Modern — supported by OpenSSH 6.5+ (2014). For new deployments: ALWAYS use Ed25519. Use RSA only for legacy systems that don't support it. The LFCS exam accepts both — show you know the difference!

🔬 How Diffie-Hellman Key Exchange Actually Works (Simplified)

The magic: two parties agree on a shared secret that was NEVER transmitted. Simplified with colors: (1) Alice and Bob agree on a public color (yellow). (2) Each picks a SECRET color (Alice=red, Bob=blue). (3) Each mixes their secret with the public color (Alice=orange, Bob=green) and sends the mixture. (4) Each adds their own secret to the received mixture. Alice: green+red = brown. Bob: orange+blue = brown. Both now have brown — the shared secret! An eavesdropper sees yellow, orange, and green — but can't figure out brown without knowing red or blue. Real DH uses modular exponentiation instead of colors: g^a mod p where only g and p are public. Computing a from g^a mod p is the discrete logarithm problem — mathematically infeasible for large numbers. The shared secret becomes the session key for AES-256 encryption. This is why SSH is secure even on untrusted networks.

⚠️ SSH Agent Forwarding — The Hidden Danger

SSH agent forwarding (ssh -A or ForwardAgent yes in config) lets you use your local SSH keys on a remote server. The danger: if the remote server is compromised, an attacker can use your forwarded agent to authenticate to ANY server your key has access to. They don't steal your private key — they use the agent socket while you're connected. Never use agent forwarding on untrusted servers. Safer alternative: ProxyJump (ssh -J bastion target) which tunnels the connection through an intermediate host without exposing your keys. Another alternative: use separate keys with limited scope per server.

26.3 SSH Troubleshooting

SSH Troubleshooting Decision Tree
  SSH FAILS TO CONNECT?
      │
      ├─ "Connection refused"
      │   ├─ sshd running? → systemctl status sshd
      │   ├─ Correct port?  → ss -tlnp | grep 22
      │   └─ Firewall?      → ufw status, iptables -L
      │
      ├─ "Connection timed out"
      │   ├─ Can ping?      → ping server-ip
      │   ├─ Port open?     → nc -zv server 22
      │   └─ Cloud SG?      → Check AWS/Azure security group
      │
      ├─ "Permission denied (publickey)"
      │   ├─ Key in authorized_keys? → cat ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
      │   ├─ Permissions?    → chmod 700 ~/.ssh; chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
      │   ├─ Right key?      → ssh -i /path/to/key user@host
      │   └─ Check server log → tail -f /var/log/auth.log
      │
      ├─ "Host key verification failed"
      │   ├─ Expected change? → ssh-keygen -R hostname
      │   └─ UNEXPECTED?      → ⚠️ POSSIBLE MITM ATTACK! Verify with admin.
      │
      └─ "Too many authentication failures"
          ├─ Too many keys in agent? → ssh -o IdentitiesOnly=yes
          └─ Try specific key: ssh -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 -o IdentitiesOnly=yes user@host
🔬 How known_hosts Prevents MITM Attacks

On your FIRST SSH connection to a server, you see: "The authenticity of host 'anihpj.com (203.0.113.50)' can't be established. ED25519 key fingerprint is SHA256:abc123... Are you sure you want to continue?" This is the most important security prompt in SSH. If you type "yes", the server's public host key is saved in ~/.ssh/known_hosts. On every FUTURE connection, SSH verifies the server's presented key MATCHES the saved one. If they don't match, SSH screams "HOST KEY VERIFICATION FAILED" and refuses to connect. This protects against MITM (Man-in-the-Middle) attacks: if someone intercepts your connection and presents a fake server, the key won't match and SSH warns you. When to trust the warning: Server was reinstalled, IP reassigned, or you're connecting to a DIFFERENT server at the same IP. When to PANIC: You didn't expect the key to change — someone might be intercepting your traffic. Fix: ssh-keygen -R hostname removes the old key so you can accept the new one. Never blindly type "yes" to a changed host key — verify with the server admin first.

SymptomLikely CauseFix
"Connection refused"sshd not running or wrong portsystemctl status sshd, check ss -tlnp | grep 22
"Permission denied (publickey)"Key not in authorized_keys OR wrong permissionsCheck ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, run chmod 600 ~/.ssh/*
"Host key verification failed"Server's host key changed (reinstall/IP change)ssh-keygen -R hostname to remove old key
"Connection timed out"Firewall blocking port 22Check UFW: ufw status, cloud security group rules
SSH hangs after "Connecting..."DNS reverse lookup slow/failingAdd UseDNS no to sshd_config on server
"Too many authentication failures"SSH agent offering too many keysssh -o IdentitiesOnly=yes -o PubkeyAuthentication=no user@host
🔧 Debug SSH Like a Pro

ssh -vvv user@server — triple verbose mode shows the ENTIRE handshake: key exchange, offered keys, accepted auth methods, and the exact reason for failure. On the server side: tail -f /var/log/auth.log shows sshd's perspective. These two together solve 99% of SSH problems.

🔬 SSH Port Forwarding Deep-Dive — Three Types of Tunnels

SSH can tunnel TCP connections through its encrypted channel — turning SSH into a poor-man's VPN. Type 1 — Local Forward (most common): ssh -L 5433:localhost:5432 user@server. Traffic to YOUR port 5433 → encrypted over SSH → server's localhost:5432. Use: access remote PostgreSQL without exposing it to the internet. Type 2 — Remote Forward (reverse tunnel): ssh -R 8080:localhost:80 user@public-server. Traffic to public-server:8080 → encrypted back to YOUR localhost:80. Use: expose your dev server behind NAT to a public server. Type 3 — Dynamic SOCKS Proxy: ssh -D 1080 user@server. Creates a SOCKS5 proxy on your local port 1080. Configure browser to use localhost:1080 as proxy → ALL browser traffic tunnels through the server. Use: browse internet as if you're at the server's location, bypass geo-restrictions.

26.4 SSH Hardening

⚠️ The Single Biggest SSH Mistake

Leaving PasswordAuthentication yes on a public-facing server. Within MINUTES of going online, bots will start guessing passwords: root/admin/ubuntu/123456 — thousands of attempts per day. Even a "strong" 12-character password can be brute-forced given enough time. SSH keys (Ed25519) are mathematically impossible to brute-force. PasswordAuthentication no is the single most impactful security change you can make. Every other hardening measure is secondary.

🔴 Password Authentication

Bots guess passwords 24/7. Even strong passwords can be keylogged or phished. Server logs fill with "Failed password for root from 103.x.x.x". A single weak user password = full server compromise.

🟢 SSH Key Authentication

Ed25519 key = 256 bits of cryptographic proof. Cannot be brute-forced. Cannot be keylogged (private key never leaves your machine). Pair with a passphrase for 2FA-like protection.

The SSH Hardening Ladder — From Default to Fortress
  LEVEL 1: DEFAULT (INSECURE)
  ├─ Port 22, root login allowed, password auth enabled
  └─ 🔴 Bots WILL find you and try passwords within MINUTES

    ▼

  LEVEL 2: BASIC HARDENING
  ├─ PermitRootLogin no
  ├─ PasswordAuthentication no (key-only)
  └─ 🔶 Stops ALL password-guessing attacks

    ▼

  LEVEL 3: INTERMEDIATE
  ├─ Change port to 2222 (reduces automated scans by 99%)
  ├─ AllowUsers whitelist (only specific users can SSH)
  ├─ MaxAuthTries 3
  └─ 🔶 Dramatically reduces attack surface

    ▼

  LEVEL 4: ADVANCED
  ├─ fail2ban: auto-ban IPs after 5 failed attempts
  ├─ Port knocking: SSH port hidden until secret sequence
  ├─ 2FA: Google Authenticator + SSH key
  └─ 🟢 Production-grade security

    ▼

  LEVEL 5: ENTERPRISE
  ├─ SSH Certificate Authority (signed keys with expiry)
  ├─ Bastion host (jump server, single SSH entry point)
  ├─ Session recording (audit all SSH commands)
  └─ 🟢 Zero-trust architecture
DirectiveRecommended ValueWhy
PermitRootLoginnoForce sudo via personal account. Audit trail.
PasswordAuthenticationnoKeys are un-bruteforceable. Passwords are not.
Port2222 (non-default)Eliminates 99% of automated SSH scan noise.
AllowUsersWhitelist specific usersEven if someone steals a key, they need the username too.
MaxAuthTries3Disconnect after 3 failures. Works with fail2ban.

26.5 SSH Config File (~/.ssh/config)

Client-Side ~/.ssh/config — Your SSH Shortcut File
  # ~/.ssh/config — save typing with aliases and defaults
  # chmod 600 ~/.ssh/config (must be owner-only!)

  # === ANIHPJ PRODUCTION ===
  Host anihpj-prod
      HostName 203.0.113.50
      User anihpj-deploy
      Port 2222
      IdentityFile ~/.ssh/anihpj_ed25519
      IdentitiesOnly yes          # Only try THIS key
      ServerAliveInterval 60      # Keep connection alive

  # === ANIHPJ STAGING ===
  Host anihpj-staging
      HostName staging.anihpj.com
      User anihpj-deploy
      IdentityFile ~/.ssh/anihpj_ed25519

  # === DATABASE TUNNEL (access PostgreSQL via SSH) ===
  Host anihpj-db-tunnel
      HostName 203.0.113.50
      User anihpj-deploy
      IdentityFile ~/.ssh/anihpj_ed25519
      LocalForward 5432 localhost:5432   # /dbs/port-forwarding/idioms

  # === GLOBAL DEFAULTS ===
  Host *
      ServerAliveInterval 120     # Send keepalive every 2 min
      ForwardAgent no             # Don't forward SSH agent (security!)
      StrictHostKeyChecking ask   # Prompt on new host keys

  # NOW: Instead of:
  #   ssh -i ~/.ssh/anihpj_ed25519 -p 2222 anihpj-deploy@203.0.113.50
  # Just type:
  #   ssh anihpj-prod
DirectiveExampleWhat It Does
HostHost anihpj-prodAlias name — what you type after ssh
HostNameHostName 203.0.113.50Real server IP or FQDN
UserUser deployDefault SSH username (skip user@ in commands)
PortPort 2222Non-default SSH port
IdentityFileIdentityFile ~/.ssh/anihpj_ed25519Which private key to use
IdentitiesOnlyIdentitiesOnly yesOnly try THIS key (ignore ssh-agent keys)
LocalForwardLocalForward 5433 localhost:5432Tunnel local port to remote host:port
ProxyJumpProxyJump bastion.anihpj.comJump through bastion host to reach target
ServerAliveIntervalServerAliveInterval 60Send keepalive every 60s (prevents timeout)
ForwardAgentForwardAgent noNEVER forward SSH agent (security risk!)
StrictHostKeyCheckingStrictHostKeyChecking askPrompt on new host keys (security default)
❌ WITHOUT ~/.ssh/config

ssh -i ~/.ssh/anihpj_ed25519 -p 2222 anihpj-deploy@203.0.113.50
Every. Single. Time. You must remember the port, user, key path, and IP.

✅ WITH ~/.ssh/config

ssh anihpj-prod
That's it. Tab-completes. All settings applied automatically from the config file.

🔬 ProxyJump — The Safe Way to Reach Internal Servers

Many organizations have a bastion/jump host — the ONLY server exposed to the internet. Internal servers (databases, staging, monitoring) sit behind it with no public IP. Instead of SSH-ing into the bastion THEN SSH-ing again, use ProxyJump: ssh -J bastion.anihpj.com internal-db.anihpj.local. The connection is tunneled THROUGH the bastion — your keys stay on your laptop. In ~/.ssh/config: Host internal-db
HostName 10.0.1.50
User deploy
ProxyJump bastion.anihpj.com. This is why ProxyJump replaces the dangerous ssh -A (agent forwarding) — same result, zero key exposure.

Configure sshd to: disable root login, disable password authentication, and limit SSH to users "admin" and "deploy" only.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

PermitRootLogin no
PasswordAuthentication no
AllowUsers admin deploy
# Test and reload:
sudo sshd -t && sudo systemctl reload sshd
Q3
SSH key authentication fails. What three permission checks should you perform on the server?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Check these on the SERVER side:
ls -la ~/.ssh/              # directory must be 700 (drwx------)
ls -la ~/.ssh/authorized_keys # file must be 600 (-rw-------)
ls -la ~                     # home directory must NOT be group/world writable

# Debug: tail -f /var/log/auth.log while attempting SSH connection
Q4
Create ~/.ssh/config entry so "ssh anihpj" connects to 203.0.113.50 as user "deploy" on port 2222 with a specific key.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# ~/.ssh/config (chmod 600):
Host anihpj
    HostName 203.0.113.50
    User deploy
    Port 2222
    IdentityFile ~/.ssh/anihpj_ed25519
# Now: ssh anihpj connects with all settings applied!
Q5
What does "Host key verification failed" mean? How do you fix it safely?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

The server's host key doesn't match the one stored in ~/.ssh/known_hosts. This happens when the server is reinstalled or its IP is reassigned. Fix: ssh-keygen -R hostname removes the old key. Then reconnect — you'll be prompted to accept the new key. ⚠️ Only do this if you EXPECT the key to have changed (reinstall). If it changed unexpectedly, it could be a MITM attack!

Q6
Set up an SSH tunnel to access PostgreSQL on the remote server (localhost:5432) via your local port 5433.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ssh -L 5433:localhost:5432 deploy@anihpj-server
# Now localhost:5433 on YOUR machine → server's localhost:5432
# Test: psql -h localhost -p 5433 -U anihpj_user anihpj_db
Q7
How do you test sshd_config for syntax errors before applying changes? Why is this critical?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo sshd -t
# No output = config is valid. Exit code 0.
# If there are errors, sshd prints the line number and description.
# CRITICAL because: if you reload with a broken config,
# sshd may fail to start, and you're LOCKED OUT of the server!
Q8
SSH connection hangs at "Connecting to server..." for 30+ seconds before succeeding. What's the likely cause and fix?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Likely: DNS reverse lookup timeout
# Server tries to resolve client's IP → slow/failing DNS

# Fix on server (/etc/ssh/sshd_config):
UseDNS no
# Then: sudo systemctl reload sshd

# Alternative: check /etc/nsswitch.conf hosts order
Q9
How do you check which SSH sessions are currently active on the server? Show both commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
who           # shows logged-in users and their IPs
ss -tnp | grep :22   # shows active TCP connections to SSH port
w             # shows users + what command they're running
last | head   # recent login history
# For sshd-specific: sudo ss -tnp state established '( sport = :22 )'
Q10
You changed sshd_config and ran "systemctl restart sshd". Now you can't SSH in. What should you have done differently?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# ALWAYS do this sequence:
sudo sshd -t              # 1. TEST config first!
sudo systemctl reload sshd  # 2. RELOAD (not restart!) — existing session stays alive

# NEVER "systemctl restart sshd" because:
# - Kills your current SSH session
# - If config is broken, sshd won't start → LOCKED OUT

# Recovery (if you have console access):
# Boot into recovery mode or use cloud console to fix the config
# Or: schedule a "sanity reboot" with "shutdown -r +5" before risky changes
#     → if you break SSH, the server reboots in 5 min with old config

Chapter 27: Web Servers — Nginx & Apache

Every web request to anihpj.com hits a web server first. This chapter covers the two dominant Linux web servers: Nginx (the modern event-driven reverse proxy) and Apache (the process-based workhorse). You'll learn their architectural differences, how to configure Nginx as a reverse proxy for Gunicorn/Django, set up SSL/TLS with Let's Encrypt, and handle Apache basics for the LFCS exam.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~2 hours🌐 Web

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Compare Nginx (event-driven) vs Apache (process-based) architectures
  • ✅ Install and configure Nginx with virtual hosts (server blocks)
  • ✅ Build a complete Nginx reverse proxy config for anihpj + Gunicorn
  • ✅ Enable HTTPS with Let's Encrypt (Certbot) — zero downtime
  • ✅ Understand Apache2 virtual hosts and module management
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Service Configuration 20%
🎯 Web Servers on the LFCS Exam

You may need to install and configure a basic web server. Know how to: create virtual hosts (server blocks in Nginx, VirtualHost in Apache), enable/disable sites, test configs (nginx -t, apache2ctl -t), and reload without downtime (systemctl reload). The exam tests basic setup, not advanced performance tuning.

27.1 Nginx vs Apache — Architecture Differences

FeatureNginxApache
ArchitectureEvent-driven, asynchronous, single-threaded worker processesProcess/thread per connection (prefork/worker/event MPMs)
ConcurrencyHandles 10,000+ connections with few workers (C10K)Each connection consumes a process/thread — more RAM
Static FilesExcellent — serves directly from disk with minimal overheadGood, but more overhead per request
Dynamic ContentReverse proxy to backend (Gunicorn, uWSGI, PHP-FPM)Modules run code in-process (mod_php, mod_python)
Config StyleClean, block-based. server { } and location { } blocksXML-like. Directory-based .htaccess overrides.
.htaccessNOT supported (by design — faster without per-dir checks)Supported — allows per-directory config overrides
Best ForReverse proxy, load balancing, static files, high concurrencyShared hosting, legacy apps needing .htaccess
🟢 Nginx (Event-Driven)

1 worker process handles thousands of connections simultaneously using epoll/kqueue. Like a restaurant with one super-efficient waiter who never stops moving. Anihpj choice: Nginx as reverse proxy.

🔵 Apache (Process-Based)

Each connection gets its own process/thread. Like a restaurant with one waiter per table. More resource usage but simpler mental model. Anihpj choice: Not used; Nginx is primary.

🔬 How epoll Makes Nginx So Fast — The Event Loop Explained

Traditional servers (Apache prefork) create one process PER connection. 1,000 concurrent users = 1,000 processes, each consuming ~2-10MB RAM. That's 2-10GB just for idle connections! Nginx uses epoll (Linux) / kqueue (BSD): the kernel maintains a list of file descriptors (sockets) and wakes up Nginx ONLY when data is ready. One worker process with epoll can handle 10,000+ idle connections using almost no CPU. The event loop: (1) Register all sockets with epoll, (2) Go to sleep — kernel does the waiting, (3) Kernel wakes Nginx: "data ready on socket #42", (4) Nginx handles that ONE socket, (5) Go back to sleep. No polling, no per-connection processes, minimal context switching. Analogy: Apache = 1,000 waiters each watching one table. Nginx = 1 waiter with a pager that buzzes when ANY table needs attention.

🔬 UNIX Socket vs TCP Socket — Why Gunicorn Uses a File

Nginx talks to Gunicorn via /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock — a UNIX domain socket. Why not TCP on localhost:8000? Performance: UNIX sockets bypass the TCP/IP stack entirely — no packetization, no TCP handshake, no congestion control. Data is copied directly between processes in the kernel. Security: UNIX sockets respect filesystem permissions (chmod 660, chown anihpj:www-data) — only specific users/groups can connect. TCP on localhost:8000 can be connected to by ANY local user. No port conflicts: You can have unlimited UNIX sockets, but only one service per TCP port. The downside: UNIX sockets only work on the same machine (no remote connections). For anihpj: Nginx and Gunicorn are on the SAME server, so the UNIX socket is perfect.

27.2 Installing & Configuring Nginx

Command / PathWhat It Is
apt install nginxInstall Nginx (starts automatically after install)
/etc/nginx/nginx.confMain config file (worker_processes, events, http block, includes)
/etc/nginx/sites-available/Virtual host configs (one file per site)
/etc/nginx/sites-enabled/Symlinks to active sites (what Nginx actually reads)
nginx -tTest configuration syntax (ALWAYS run before reload!)
systemctl reload nginxApply config changes without dropping connections (graceful)
ln -s /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/Enable a site
rm /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/defaultDisable default site
Nginx Configuration Structure
  /etc/nginx/
  ├── nginx.conf                    ← Main config (worker_processes, events, http)
  ├── sites-available/
  │   ├── default                   ← Default welcome page
  │   └── anihpj                    ← Our virtual host config
  ├── sites-enabled/
  │   └── anihpj → ../sites-available/anihpj  ← SYMLINK (enabled)
  ├── conf.d/                       ← Additional config snippets
  ├── snippets/                     ← Reusable config fragments
  │   ├── ssl-params.conf           ← SSL security parameters
  │   └── anihpj-proxy.conf         ← Proxy headers
  └── modules-enabled/              ← Enabled modules

  WORKFLOW:
  1. Create/edit config in sites-available/
  2. Enable: ln -s sites-available/NAME sites-enabled/NAME
  3. Test:   sudo nginx -t
  4. Apply:  sudo systemctl reload nginx

27.3 Virtual Host for anihpj

🔧 Real-World: "Nginx 502 Bad Gateway" — What Happened to Anihpj?

Situation: Users report anihpj.com showing "502 Bad Gateway." You SSH in to investigate.

  1. Check Nginx error log: tail -50 /var/log/nginx/anihpj-error.log → "connect() to unix:/run/anihpj/anihpj.sock failed (111: Connection refused)"
  2. Check Gunicorn: systemctl status anihpj → inactive (dead). Gunicorn crashed!
  3. Check journal: journalctl -u anihpj --since "10 min ago" → "ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'django'" — someone updated the virtualenv without reinstalling deps.
  4. Fix: source /var/www/anihpj/venv/bin/activate && pip install -r requirements.txt && systemctl restart anihpj
  5. Verify: curl -I https://anihpj.com → HTTP/2 200. Back online!

Root cause: Gunicorn crashed because Django wasn't importable. Nginx correctly returned 502 (upstream unreachable). Lesson: Always check BOTH nginx error log AND the upstream service when debugging 502 errors.

Complete Nginx Config for Anihpj — Reverse Proxy to Gunicorn
  # /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj
  # After editing: sudo nginx -t && sudo systemctl reload nginx

  # === HTTP (Port 80) — Redirect ALL traffic to HTTPS ===
  server {
      listen 80;
      server_name anihpj.com www.anihpj.com;

      # Redirect to HTTPS (301 permanent)
      return 301 https://$server_name$request_uri;
  }

  # === HTTPS (Port 443) — The REAL server block ===
  server {
      listen 443 ssl http2;              # HTTP/2 for faster page loads
      server_name anihpj.com www.anihpj.com;

      # SSL CERTIFICATES (from Let's Encrypt / Certbot)
      ssl_certificate     /etc/letsencrypt/live/anihpj.com/fullchain.pem;
      ssl_certificate_key /etc/letsencrypt/live/anihpj.com/privkey.pem;

      # SECURITY HEADERS
      add_header Strict-Transport-Security "max-age=63072000" always;
      add_header X-Frame-Options DENY;
      add_header X-Content-Type-Options nosniff;

      # LOGS
      access_log /var/log/nginx/anihpj-access.log;
      error_log  /var/log/nginx/anihpj-error.log;

      # CLIENT MAX BODY SIZE (for jobpost file uploads)
      client_max_body_size 10M;

      # === STATIC FILES — Served DIRECTLY by Nginx (fast!) ===
      location /static/ {
          alias /var/www/anihpj/staticfiles/;
          expires 30d;                    # Cache for 30 days
          add_header Cache-Control "public, immutable";
      }

      # === MEDIA FILES — User uploads (jobpost images) ===
      location /media/ {
          alias /var/www/anihpj/media/;
      }

      # === EVERYTHING ELSE → Gunicorn (Django app) ===
      location / {
          proxy_pass http://unix:/run/anihpj/anihpj.sock;

          # Pass original request info to Django
          proxy_set_header Host $http_host;
          proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
          proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
          proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;

          # Timeout settings
          proxy_connect_timeout 60s;
          proxy_read_timeout 60s;
      }
  }

  KEY DIRECTIVES EXPLAINED:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ listen 443 ssl http2     → Listen on HTTPS with HTTP/2          │
  │ server_name anihpj.com   → This block handles requests for      │
  │                            anihpj.com ONLY (virtual hosting)    │
  │ location /static/         → URL path matching: /static/* files   │
  │ alias /var/www/...        → Map URL path to filesystem path      │
  │ proxy_pass http://unix:   → Forward to Gunicorn via UNIX socket  │
  │ proxy_set_header          → Inject headers so Django knows the   │
  │                            original client IP and protocol       │
  │ client_max_body_size 10M  → Allow file uploads up to 10MB       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

27.4 SSL/TLS with Let's Encrypt (Certbot)

The Certificate Chain — How Your Browser Trusts Anihpj.com
  ROOT CA (pre-installed in browsers/OS)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ISRG Root X1                            │
  │  (Internet Security Research Group)      │
  │  🔑 Self-signed, valid until 2035        │
  │  Pre-installed in ALL modern systems     │
  └──────────────────┬──────────────────────┘
                     │ Signed by ISRG Root X1
                     ▼
  INTERMEDIATE CA
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Let's Encrypt R3 / R4                   │
  │  🔑 Signed by ISRG Root X1              │
  │  Issues end-user certificates            │
  └──────────────────┬──────────────────────┘
                     │ Signed by Let's Encrypt R3
                     ▼
  END-ENTITY (YOUR SERVER)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj.com                               │
  │  🔑 Signed by Let's Encrypt R3          │
  │  Valid for 90 days (auto-renew!)         │
  │  SAN: anihpj.com, www.anihpj.com         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  VERIFICATION PATH (browser does this automatically):
  1. Browser sees anihpj.com cert → signed by Let's Encrypt R3
  2. Browser checks Let's Encrypt R3 → signed by ISRG Root X1
  3. ISRG Root X1 is in browser's TRUST STORE → CHAIN TRUSTED ✅

  🎯 LFCS: You create the BOTTOM cert via certbot.
     The chain (fullchain.pem) includes YOUR cert + intermediate.
     The browser already has the root. Trust flows DOWN the chain.
How HTTPS/TLS Works — The Handshake (Simplified for LFCS)
  BROWSER (Client)                    NGINX (Server — anihpj.com)
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ① CLIENT HELLO
     "I support TLS 1.3, these cipher
      suites: AES-256-GCM, ChaCha20..."  ──────▶

  ②                                ◀─────────── SERVER HELLO
                                    "Let's use TLS 1.3 + AES-256-GCM"

  ③                                ◀─────────── SERVER CERTIFICATE
                                    "Here's my cert, signed by Let's
                                     Encrypt. My public key is inside."

  ④ CERTIFICATE VERIFICATION
     ✓ Signed by trusted CA?          ✅ Let's Encrypt IS trusted
     ✓ For anihpj.com?                ✅ CN/SAN match
     ✓ Not expired?                   ✅ Valid for 90 days

  ⑤ KEY EXCHANGE (Diffie-Hellman)
     Both sides compute a SHARED
     SECRET without sending it.
     (Even if someone snoops, they
      can't figure out the key.)

  ⑥ 🔒 SYMMETRIC ENCRYPTION BEGINS
     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  ALL DATA ENCRYPTED WITH AES-256-GCM                     │
     │  GET /jobs/  →  [ENCRYPTED]  →  Nginx → Gunicorn → DB  │
     │  200 OK      ←  [ENCRYPTED]  ←  HTML response           │
     └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  🎯 LFCS: You configure the SERVER side (steps 2-3).
     certbot handles the complexity — you just need to:
     1. Install certbot: apt install certbot python3-certbot-nginx
     2. Run certbot: sudo certbot --nginx -d anihpj.com
     3. Verify auto-renewal: sudo certbot renew --dry-run
CommandWhat It Does
apt install certbot python3-certbot-nginxInstall Certbot with Nginx plugin
certbot --nginx -d anihpj.com -d www.anihpj.comGet certificate and auto-configure Nginx
certbot renew --dry-runTest auto-renewal (no actual renewal)
certbot certificatesList all managed certificates and expiry dates
/etc/letsencrypt/live/anihpj.com/Certificate files: fullchain.pem, privkey.pem
🔬 Why HTTPS Matters for Anihpj

Without HTTPS: (1) Passwords sent in plaintext — anyone on the WiFi can see them. (2) Session cookies stolen — attacker hijacks user accounts. (3) Browsers show "Not Secure" warning — users leave. (4) Google ranks HTTP sites lower. With HTTPS + HSTS: browsers refuse to connect over HTTP. Certificates are FREE with Let's Encrypt — there's no excuse not to use HTTPS in production.

Chapter 27 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Install Nginx and create a virtual host for anihpj.com that listens on port 80 and serves files from /var/www/anihpj.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo apt install nginx
sudo nano /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj
# Add: server { listen 80; server_name anihpj.com; root /var/www/anihpj; }
sudo ln -s /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/
sudo nginx -t && sudo systemctl reload nginx
Q2
What's the difference between sites-available and sites-enabled in Nginx?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

sites-available/ stores ALL virtual host config files — active or not. sites-enabled/ contains ONLY symlinks to active sites. Nginx only reads from sites-enabled/. This lets you disable a site by removing the symlink without deleting the config file. Pattern: write in available, enable with symlink, test, reload.

Q3
Configure Nginx to redirect all HTTP (port 80) traffic to HTTPS (port 443) for anihpj.com.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
server {
    listen 80;
    server_name anihpj.com www.anihpj.com;
    return 301 https://$server_name$request_uri;
}
Q4
Configure Nginx as a reverse proxy to forward all requests to a Gunicorn UNIX socket at /run/anihpj/anihpj.sock.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
location / {
    proxy_pass http://unix:/run/anihpj/anihpj.sock;
    proxy_set_header Host $http_host;
    proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
    proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
    proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
}
Q5
Get an SSL certificate for anihpj.com using Certbot and verify auto-renewal works.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo apt install certbot python3-certbot-nginx
sudo certbot --nginx -d anihpj.com -d www.anihpj.com
sudo certbot renew --dry-run   # test auto-renewal
Q6
What command tests Nginx configuration for errors? What happens if you reload with a broken config?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo nginx -t
# No output + exit 0 = config is valid
# If you reload with a broken config:
# - nginx will REFUSE to reload (the old config stays active)
# - systemctl reload nginx will fail with an error message
# - Existing connections are NOT affected
Q7
Why does Nginx NOT support .htaccess files? What's the advantage of this design?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Nginx intentionally does NOT support per-directory config files (.htaccess). Reason: Apache checks .htaccess in EVERY directory in the path for EVERY request — this is slow. Nginx's approach: all config is in the central server blocks — read once at startup, no per-request filesystem checks. Result: Nginx is faster for static files and uses less CPU. The trade-off: you can't let users override config in their directories (which shared hosting needs).

Q8
Enable an Apache site called "anihpj" and disable the default site. Write the commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo a2dissite 000-default    # disable default
sudo a2ensite anihpj           # enable our site
sudo apache2ctl -t             # test config
sudo systemctl reload apache2  # apply
Q9
You changed Nginx config and ran "systemctl restart nginx". Why is reload usually better?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

systemctl reload nginx tells the master process to re-read config and gracefully restart workers — existing connections finish, new connections use new config. ZERO downtime. systemctl restart nginx KILLS all workers immediately, dropping active connections. Use reload for config changes, restart only when nginx itself is broken or after package upgrades.

Q10
Configure Nginx to serve static files from /var/www/anihpj/staticfiles/ with a 30-day browser cache.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
location /static/ {
    alias /var/www/anihpj/staticfiles/;
    expires 30d;
    add_header Cache-Control "public, immutable";
}
🚀

Chapter 28: PostgreSQL — Database Server Configuration

PostgreSQL stores every job posting, user profile, and application on anihpj. This chapter covers installing PostgreSQL, understanding its two critical config files (postgresql.conf and pg_hba.conf), creating the anihpj database and user with proper privileges, backup/restore strategies, and performance tuning basics for production.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🗄️ Database

  • ✅ Configure postgresql.conf (server settings) and pg_hba.conf (authentication)
  • ✅ Create the anihpj database, user, and grant proper privileges
  • ✅ Backup and restore databases with pg_dump and pg_restore
  • ✅ Tune PostgreSQL memory settings for production performance
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Service Configuration 20%
🎯 PostgreSQL on the LFCS Exam

You may need to install PostgreSQL, create a database and user, and configure basic authentication. Know: how to switch to the postgres user (sudo -u postgres psql), the difference between postgresql.conf (server settings) and pg_hba.conf (who can connect), and basic backup with pg_dump.

28.1 Installing PostgreSQL

PostgreSQL Directory Structure — What Goes Where
  /etc/postgresql/15/main/          ← CONFIGURATION (what you edit)
  ├── postgresql.conf                ← Server settings: port, memory, SSL
  ├── pg_hba.conf                    ← Authentication: who can connect
  ├── pg_ident.conf                  ← User name mapping (rarely used)
  └── environment                    ← Environment variables for PostgreSQL

  /var/lib/postgresql/15/main/       ← DATA DIRECTORY (where your data lives)
  ├── base/                          ← Actual database files (one subdir per DB)
  │   ├── 1/                         ← Template database
  │   ├── 13765/                     ← anihpj_db (OID = 13765)
  │   │   ├── 12345                  ← jobpost_jobpost table file
  │   │   └── 12345_fsm              ← Free space map for that table
  │   └── ...
  ├── pg_wal/                        ← Write-Ahead Log (crash recovery)
  ├── pg_stat/                       ← Statistics (query planner uses this)
  ├── pg_logical/                    ← Logical replication data
  └── PG_VERSION                     ← File containing "15" (version marker)

  /usr/lib/postgresql/15/bin/        ← EXECUTABLES
  ├── postgres                       ← The database engine itself
  ├── pg_dump / pg_restore           ← Backup/restore tools
  ├── psql                           ← Interactive SQL client
  └── pg_ctl                         ← Start/stop/reload control

  /var/log/postgresql/               ← LOG FILES
  └── postgresql-15-main.log         ← Main log (errors, slow queries, connections)

  KEY INSIGHT: Config is in /etc/, data is in /var/lib/, binaries in /usr/lib/,
  logs in /var/log/.  Back up /var/lib/postgresql/15/main/ — that's your data.
  Never lose pg_wal/ — it's your crash recovery safety net.
Command / PathWhat It Is
apt install postgresql postgresql-contribInstall PostgreSQL + extra modules (pgcrypto, etc.)
/etc/postgresql/15/main/Config directory (15 = version number)
/var/lib/postgresql/15/main/Data directory (actual database files on disk)
systemctl status postgresqlCheck if PostgreSQL is running
sudo -u postgres psqlConnect as the postgres superuser
pg_lsclustersList all PostgreSQL clusters, versions, and status
🔬 The "postgres" User — Linux User vs Database Role

PostgreSQL creates a Linux system user called postgres during installation. This user has NO password (locked account) and NO login shell — you can only switch to it via sudo -u postgres. Inside PostgreSQL, there's also a database ROLE called postgres which is the superuser. When you run sudo -u postgres psql, you connect as the Linux postgres user, and PostgreSQL's "peer" authentication (in pg_hba.conf) automatically logs you in as the postgres database role. No password needed — this is the DEFAULT secure setup.

28.2 Configuration Files

The Two PostgreSQL Config Files — What Each Controls
  /etc/postgresql/15/main/
  ├── postgresql.conf      ← SERVER SETTINGS (how PostgreSQL runs)
  │   • listen_addresses = 'localhost'    # Only local connections (secure!)
  │   • port = 5432                       # Default PostgreSQL port
  │   • max_connections = 100             # Max simultaneous connections
  │   • shared_buffers = 128MB            # Memory for caching data
  │   • effective_cache_size = 4GB        # Hint for query planner
  │   • work_mem = 4MB                    # Memory per sort/hash operation
  │   • log_destination = 'stderr'        # Where to write logs
  │   • logging_collector = on            # Enable log file collection
  │   • log_directory = 'log'             # Log file location
  │
  └── pg_hba.conf          ← AUTHENTICATION (WHO can connect, and HOW)
      # TYPE  DATABASE    USER        ADDRESS          METHOD
      local   all         postgres                    peer
      local   all         all                         peer
      host    all         all         127.0.0.1/32    md5
      host    all         all         ::1/128         md5

      FORMAT:  type  database  user  address  auth_method

      type:     local (Unix socket) / host (TCP/IP) / hostssl (TLS only)
      database: all / specific_database_name
      user:     all / specific_user_name
      address:  CIDR for TCP connections (e.g., 192.168.1.0/24)
      method:   peer (OS user match) / md5 (password hash) / scram-sha-256
                trust (NO password — NEVER in production!)
SettingWhat It ControlsAnihpj Recommendation
listen_addressesWhich IPs PostgreSQL listens on'localhost' — only app server can connect
max_connectionsHow many simultaneous DB connections20-50 (Gunicorn workers × connection pool)
shared_buffersMemory for caching data pages25% of system RAM (e.g., 1GB on 4GB server)
sslEnable TLS for client connectionson if connecting over network

28.3 Creating the anihpj Database & User

🔧 Real-World: "Django Can't Connect to PostgreSQL" — Debugging Connection Refused

Situation: python manage.py migrate fails with "could not connect to server: Connection refused. Is the server running on host 'localhost' (127.0.0.1)?"

  1. Check PostgreSQL is running: systemctl status postgresql → active ✅
  2. Check listening interfaces: ss -tlnp | grep 5432 → NOTHING! PostgreSQL isn't listening!
  3. Check postgresql.conf: grep listen_addresses /etc/postgresql/15/main/postgresql.conf → listen_addresses = '' (EMPTY means no TCP, only Unix sockets!)
  4. Fix: sudo sed -i "s/listen_addresses = ''/listen_addresses = 'localhost'/" /etc/postgresql/15/main/postgresql.conf && sudo systemctl restart postgresql
  5. Check pg_hba.conf: Ensure there's a line: host anihpj_db anihpj_user 127.0.0.1/32 md5
  6. Test: PGPASSWORD='pass' psql -h localhost -U anihpj_user -d anihpj_db -c "SELECT 1" → 1. Fixed!

Key insight: Empty/blank listen_addresses = Unix sockets only (peer auth). 'localhost' = TCP on 127.0.0.1 + Unix sockets. Django needs TCP to connect via localhost.

Complete SQL Setup for Anihpj Database
  # Connect as PostgreSQL superuser
  sudo -u postgres psql

  -- 1. Create the database
  CREATE DATABASE anihpj_db;

  -- 2. Create the application user (what Django uses to connect)
  CREATE USER anihpj_user WITH PASSWORD 'generate-a-strong-random-password-here';

  -- 3. Grant all privileges on the database to our user
  GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON DATABASE anihpj_db TO anihpj_user;

  -- 4. Make our user the database owner (can create/drop tables)
  ALTER DATABASE anihpj_db OWNER TO anihpj_user;

  -- 5. Connect to the database and grant schema permissions
  \c anihpj_db
  GRANT ALL ON SCHEMA public TO anihpj_user;
  GRANT CREATE ON SCHEMA public TO anihpj_user;

  -- 6. Verify
  \l anihpj_db    -- list database details (owner should be anihpj_user)
  \du anihpj_user -- list user details

  -- 7. Test connection (from shell, not psql):
  PGPASSWORD='the-password' psql -h localhost -U anihpj_user -d anihpj_db -c "SELECT 1;"
  # Should return:  ?column?  |  1

  # IMPORTANT: Store the password in Django's .env file:
  # DATABASE_URL=postgres://anihpj_user:password@localhost:5432/anihpj_db
💡 The Golden Rule of Database Security

PostgreSQL should NEVER listen on a public IP address. Always listen_addresses = 'localhost'. If Django and PostgreSQL are on the same server (which they should be for most deployments), there's ZERO reason for PostgreSQL to accept network connections from anywhere but 127.0.0.1. If you need remote access: use an SSH tunnel (ssh -L 5432:localhost:5432 user@server), NEVER open port 5432 to the internet. Thousands of PostgreSQL instances are compromised daily because of this one misconfiguration.

⚠️ NEVER expose PostgreSQL to the internet!

listen_addresses should be 'localhost' for production. If Django and PostgreSQL are on the same server (which they should be for small-to-medium deployments), PostgreSQL should only listen on 127.0.0.1. External access opens you to brute-force attacks. If you need remote access, use an SSH tunnel: ssh -L 5432:localhost:5432 user@server.

28.4 Backup & Restore

CommandWhat It DoesOutput Format
pg_dump anihpj_db > backup.sqlFull database backup as SQL textPlain SQL (editable, human-readable)
pg_dump -Fc anihpj_db > backup.dumpCustom format (compressed, flexible)Binary (smaller, supports selective restore)
pg_dumpall > all_databases.sqlBackup ALL databases + rolesPlain SQL (global objects included)
psql anihpj_db < backup.sqlRestore from SQL dumpMust specify target database
pg_restore -d anihpj_db backup.dumpRestore from custom formatCan restore selective tables
pg_restore -l backup.dumpList contents of custom dumpSee what's inside before restoring
Automated Nightly Backup Script for Anihpj
  #!/bin/bash
  # /usr/local/bin/anihpj-backup.sh — Nightly PostgreSQL backup
  # Cron: 0 2 * * * /usr/local/bin/anihpj-backup.sh

  BACKUP_DIR="/backups/anihpj"
  DB_NAME="anihpj_db"
  RETENTION_DAYS=14   # Keep 2 weeks of daily backups
  TIMESTAMP=$(date +%Y%m%d_%H%M%S)

  mkdir -p "$BACKUP_DIR"

  # Create backup
  sudo -u postgres pg_dump -Fc "$DB_NAME" > "$BACKUP_DIR/${DB_NAME}_${TIMESTAMP}.dump"

  # Compress (custom format is already compressed, but SQL dumps benefit)
  # gzip "$BACKUP_DIR/${DB_NAME}_${TIMESTAMP}.sql"

  # Delete backups older than retention period
  find "$BACKUP_DIR" -name "*.dump" -mtime +$RETENTION_DAYS -delete

  # Optional: rsync to offsite location
  # rsync -av "$BACKUP_DIR/" backup-server:/backups/anihpj/

  echo "[$(date)] Backup complete: ${DB_NAME}_${TIMESTAMP}.dump"

28.5 Performance Basics

PostgreSQL Memory Architecture — How RAM Is Used
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    TOTAL SYSTEM RAM (4GB)                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐                   │
  │  │  shared_buffers = 1GB (25%)          │ ← PG's own cache │
  │  │  ┌────┬────┬────┬────┬────┬────┐    │   Data pages     │
  │  │  │ 📄 │ 📄 │ 📄 │ 📄 │ 📄 │ 📄 │... │   read from disk  │
  │  │  └────┴────┴────┴────┴────┴────┘    │   cached here     │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐                   │
  │  │  OS Page Cache ~2.5GB (the rest)     │ ← Linux caches   │
  │  │  (effective_cache_size = 3GB hint)   │   files in RAM.  │
  │  │  PostgreSQL reads through this —     │   "Double buffering"│
  │  │  data cached TWICE if not tuned!    │   is wasteful.    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                 │
  │  │work_mem  │  │work_mem  │  │work_mem  │  ← Per-operation │
  │  │ 16MB     │  │ 16MB     │  │ 16MB     │     NOT per-     │
  │  │ Sort Q1  │  │ Sort Q2  │  │ Hash Q3  │     connection!  │
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘                 │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚠️ 10 connections × 4 sorts each × 16MB = 640MB possible!  │
  │     total work_mem usage can spike dramatically.            │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐                                   │
  │  │ maintenance_work_mem │  ← Used by VACUUM, CREATE INDEX  │
  │  │ 256MB                │     Only ONE operation at a time │
  │  └──────────────────────┘                                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TUNING RULE OF THUMB:
  shared_buffers = 25% RAM (start here, max 8GB)
  effective_cache_size = 75% RAM (just a hint, not an allocation)
  work_mem = (RAM - shared_buffers) / (max_connections × 4)
  maintenance_work_mem = 10% RAM (but max 2GB)
ParameterWhat It DoesRecommended (4GB Server)
shared_buffersPostgreSQL's internal cache — the single most important setting1GB (25% of RAM)
effective_cache_sizeHint: how much OS cache is available (doesn't allocate, just estimates)3GB (75% of RAM)
work_memMemory per sort/hash operation PER QUERY (not per connection!)16MB (start conservative)
maintenance_work_memMemory for VACUUM, CREATE INDEX, ALTER TABLE256MB
random_page_costCost of random disk I/O (lower for SSDs!)1.1 (SSD) / 4.0 (HDD default)
autovacuumAutomatically reclaim dead rows and update statisticson (DEFAULT — never turn off!)
🔬 Why VACUUM Matters — PostgreSQL's Garbage Collector

PostgreSQL uses MVCC (Multi-Version Concurrency Control): when you UPDATE or DELETE a row, the OLD version stays on disk until VACUUM reclaims it. Without autovacuum: (1) tables bloat (dead rows accumulate), (2) queries slow down scanning dead rows, (3) transaction ID wraparound can SHUT DOWN the database. Check: SELECT relname, n_dead_tup FROM pg_stat_user_tables;. Run manual VACUUM: VACUUM ANALYZE anihpj_jobpost;. Never turn autovacuum off — just tune it if needed.

🔬 How MVCC Works — Why Readers Never Block Writers

PostgreSQL's secret sauce: every row has hidden columns xmin (transaction ID that CREATED this version) and xmax (transaction ID that DELETED/UPDATED this version). When you UPDATE a row: (1) PostgreSQL doesn't modify the existing row — it marks it with xmax = current transaction ID, (2) It inserts a NEW version of the row with xmin = current transaction ID, (3) Other transactions still see the OLD version (their snapshot only shows rows where xmin < their transaction start). Result: READERS never block WRITERS and WRITERS never block READERS. No read locks needed! The cost: old row versions accumulate. That's what VACUUM cleans up. This is fundamentally different from MySQL/InnoDB's undo-log approach. Concrete example: If you run a 1-hour analytics query on the jobs table, and simultaneously 50 users are posting new jobs or editing existing ones — both operations work concurrently without blocking each other. The analytics query sees a consistent snapshot from when it started. The new posts create new row versions invisible to the analytics query. All without a single lock.

🔬 WAL — Write-Ahead Log: PostgreSQL's Safety Net

Before PostgreSQL modifies ANY data file, it FIRST writes the change to the WAL (Write-Ahead Log) in pg_wal/. This is a sequential, append-only log of every database change. Why: If the server crashes mid-operation, on restart PostgreSQL replays the WAL — redoing any changes that were logged but not yet written to data files. This guarantees NO data loss from crashes (Durability in ACID). WAL also enables: (1) Point-in-Time Recovery (PITR) — replay WAL to any moment in time, (2) Streaming Replication — standby servers continuously apply the primary's WAL, (3) Logical Replication — decode WAL changes into SQL for selective replication. Monitoring: SELECT pg_current_wal_lsn(); shows current WAL position. pg_waldump reads WAL files for debugging. The WAL is why PostgreSQL can survive a power outage without data corruption — it's the ultimate safety net.

Two Configspostgresql.conf = server settings. pg_hba.conf = who can connect and how.
👤
DB & UserCREATE DATABASE + CREATE USER + GRANT ALL. Peer auth for postgres, md5 for app.
💾
Backuppg_dump -Fc for custom format. pg_restore to restore. Automate with cron @ 2AM.

Chapter 28 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Install PostgreSQL and check that it's running. Write both commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo apt install postgresql postgresql-contrib
systemctl status postgresql
Q2
Connect to PostgreSQL as the superuser and list all databases.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo -u postgres psql
# Then in psql:
\l
Q3
Create a database "anihpj_db" and a user "anihpj_user" with password "secure123". Grant all privileges.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo -u postgres psql
CREATE DATABASE anihpj_db;
CREATE USER anihpj_user WITH PASSWORD 'secure123';
GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON DATABASE anihpj_db TO anihpj_user;
ALTER DATABASE anihpj_db OWNER TO anihpj_user;
Q4
What's the difference between postgresql.conf and pg_hba.conf?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

postgresql.conf controls HOW PostgreSQL runs: port, memory (shared_buffers, work_mem), max connections, logging, SSL. pg_hba.conf controls WHO can connect and HOW they authenticate: which users, from which IPs, using which auth method (peer, md5, scram-sha-256). Think of postgresql.conf as engine settings and pg_hba.conf as the security guard at the door.

Q5
Backup the anihpj_db database in custom format and restore it to a new database called "anihpj_test".
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Backup:
sudo -u postgres pg_dump -Fc anihpj_db > /backups/anihpj.dump
# Create new DB and restore:
sudo -u postgres psql -c "CREATE DATABASE anihpj_test;"
sudo -u postgres pg_restore -d anihpj_test /backups/anihpj.dump
Q6
What authentication method does PostgreSQL use when you run "sudo -u postgres psql"? Why no password?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

It uses peer authentication (the default for local connections in pg_hba.conf). Peer auth checks: "Is the Linux username the SAME as the PostgreSQL role name?" Since you're running as Linux user postgres (via sudo), and there's a PostgreSQL role called postgres, the names match → access granted with NO password. This is secure because only root (via sudo) can become the postgres Linux user.

Q7
Configure PostgreSQL to only listen on localhost. Why is this important for anihpj?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# In /etc/postgresql/15/main/postgresql.conf:
listen_addresses = 'localhost'
# Then: sudo systemctl reload postgresql

# Why: Django and PostgreSQL are on the SAME server.
# PostgreSQL should NEVER be accessible from the internet.
# Localhost-only prevents ALL remote attacks on the database.
Q8
How do you check which queries are currently running on PostgreSQL? What about slow queries?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
-- Active queries:
SELECT pid, now() - query_start AS duration, query, state
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state != 'idle'
ORDER BY duration DESC;

-- Kill a runaway query:
SELECT pg_terminate_backend(pid);

-- Enable slow query logging (postgresql.conf):
log_min_duration_statement = 1000   -- log queries > 1 second
Q9
What is autovacuum and why must it never be disabled in production?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

autovacuum is PostgreSQL's automatic garbage collector. It: (1) Reclaims dead rows from UPDATE/DELETE (prevents table bloat), (2) Updates query planner statistics (so queries use optimal plans), (3) Prevents transaction ID wraparound (which would FORCE a database shutdown). Disabling autovacuum leads to: slow queries, bloated tables, and eventually a database that refuses to start. Check it's running: SELECT name, setting FROM pg_settings WHERE name LIKE '%vacuum%';

Q10
Create a cron job that backs up PostgreSQL daily at 2 AM and keeps 14 days of backups.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Create backup script at /usr/local/bin/pg-backup.sh:
#!/bin/bash
sudo -u postgres pg_dump -Fc anihpj_db > "/backups/anihpj_$(date +%Y%m%d).dump"
find /backups/ -name "anihpj_*.dump" -mtime +14 -delete

# Add to root's crontab (sudo crontab -e):
0 2 * * * /usr/local/bin/pg-backup.sh
🚀

Single-server PostgreSQL is the LFCS scope. Production adds:

→
