Chapter 29: Email, Time Services & FTP

Beyond the core web stack, every anihpj server needs accurate time (for SSL certs, log correlation, and cron jobs), email delivery (for password resets and notifications), and optionally file transfer. This chapter covers Chrony (NTP time sync), Postfix (mail transfer agent basics), and vsftpd (FTP server — though SFTP is preferred).

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1 hour📧 Services

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Synchronize system time with Chrony/NTP
  • ✅ Configure Postfix for sending email notifications
  • ✅ Set up vsftpd (and understand why SFTP is better)
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Service Configuration 20%
🎯 Time & Mail on the LFCS Exam

LFCS may test basic time sync (timedatectl) and simple mail configuration. Know how to check timezone, enable NTP, and set up a basic Postfix relay. FTP is less tested but may appear — focus on vsftpd config and understanding why SFTP (SSH-based) is the modern secure alternative.

29.1 Chrony & NTP — Time Synchronization

CommandWhat It DoesExample Output
timedatectlView timezone, NTP status, system clockTime zone: Africa/Lagos (WAT, +0100)
timedatectl list-timezonesList all available timezonesgrep for your city: | grep Lagos
timedatectl set-timezone Africa/LagosSet system timezone permanentlyAffects all users and services
timedatectl set-ntp trueEnable automatic time sync via NTPUses systemd-timesyncd or chrony
chronyc trackingDetailed Chrony sync statusShows offset, drift, last update
chronyc sources -vList NTP servers and their status^* = currently synced source
🔬 Why Accurate Time is CRITICAL for Anihpj

(1) SSL/TLS certificates: Browsers reject certs if system clock is wrong — users see scary warnings. (2) Log correlation: When debugging, you need logs from Nginx, Gunicorn, and PostgreSQL to agree on timestamps. (3) Cron jobs: Nightly backup at 2 AM must actually run at 2 AM. (4) Database replication: PostgreSQL streaming replication requires synchronized clocks. (5) Authentication: TOTP 2FA and Kerberos require time within ~30 seconds. Install chrony: apt install chrony. Config: /etc/chrony/chrony.conf — pool entries like pool 2.ubuntu.pool.ntp.org iburst.

🔬 NTP Stratum Levels — The Hierarchy of Time

NTP organizes time sources into a hierarchy: Stratum 0: Atomic clocks, GPS receivers — the ABSOLUTE reference (you can't connect to these directly). Stratum 1: Servers directly connected to stratum 0 hardware (few hundred exist globally). Stratum 2: Servers synced to stratum 1 (these are what most organizations use — like pool.ntp.org). Stratum 3+: Servers synced to stratum 2, and so on. Each level adds microseconds of drift. Your anihpj server is probably stratum 3 or 4. Why it matters: chrony selects the BEST source among configured servers — lower stratum, lower jitter, lower delay. chronyc sources -v shows stratum for each source. The ^* marker indicates the currently selected best source. Best practice: Use at least 4 NTP servers from the pool — chrony will detect and reject outliers ("falsetickers").

29.2 Postfix — Basic Mail Transfer Agent

Command / PathWhat It Does
apt install postfix mailutilsInstall Postfix + mail command
/etc/postfix/main.cfMain Postfix configuration
postconf -e "myhostname = anihpj.com"Set config value from command line
postfix reloadApply config changes
echo "Test body" | mail -s "Test Subject" admin@anihpj.comSend a test email
mailqView mail queue (undelivered messages)
Postfix Config for Anihpj — Send-Only (Relay via External SMTP)
  # /etc/postfix/main.cf — Send-only configuration for anihpj
  # Purpose: Send password reset emails, notifications, error alerts

  myhostname = anihpj.com
  myorigin = /etc/mailname          # Domain emails appear to come from
  inet_interfaces = loopback-only   # Only listen on localhost (not a public mail server!)

  # Relay all outbound mail through your email provider
  relayhost = [smtp.gmail.com]:587

  # SASL authentication for the relay
  smtp_sasl_auth_enable = yes
  smtp_sasl_password_maps = hash:/etc/postfix/sasl_passwd
  smtp_sasl_security_options = noanonymous
  smtp_tls_security_level = encrypt
  smtp_use_tls = yes

  # /etc/postfix/sasl_passwd (chmod 600!):
  # [smtp.gmail.com]:587    anihpj@gmail.com:app-specific-password
  # Then: sudo postmap /etc/postfix/sasl_passwd

  # Test:
  echo "Anihpj backup completed successfully" | mail -s "Daily Backup Report" admin@anihpj.com
Mail Flow Diagram — How Anihpj Sends a Password Reset Email
  ┌────────────────────┐
  │  Django (anihpj)    │
  │  User clicks        │
  │  "Forgot Password"  │
  └────────┬───────────┘
           │ ① Django calls send_mail()
           ▼
  ┌────────────────────┐
  │  Postfix (localhost)│  ← MTA (Mail Transfer Agent)
  │  Accepts email      │
  │  Queues for delivery│
  └────────┬───────────┘
           │ ② Postfix authenticates
           │    with SASL (smtp_sasl)
           ▼
  ┌────────────────────┐
  │  Gmail SMTP Relay   │  ← Upstream MTA
  │  smtp.gmail.com:587│
  │  (App Password auth)│
  └────────┬───────────┘
           │ ③ DNS MX lookup:
           │    recipient@company.com
           │    → MX: mail.company.com
           ▼
  ┌────────────────────┐
  │  Recipient's MTA    │
  │  mail.company.com   │
  │  (e.g., Office 365, │
  │   Gmail, Exchange)  │
  └────────┬───────────┘
           │ ④ Deliver to mailbox
           ▼
  ┌────────────────────┐
  │  Recipient's Inbox  │
  │  "Anihpj Password   │
  │   Reset Link"       │
  └────────────────────┘

  IMPORTANT CONCEPTS:
  MUA (Mail User Agent)  = Thunderbird, Outlook, Gmail web — what user sees
  MTA (Mail Transfer)    = Postfix, Sendmail — routes mail between servers
  MDA (Mail Delivery)    = Dovecot, procmail — puts mail in user's mailbox
  SMTP (Simple Mail)     = The protocol MTAs speak to each other (port 25/587)
🔬 How Email Actually Travels — SMTP Protocol Flow

When your Django app sends a password reset email: (1) Django connects to Postfix on localhost:25, (2) Postfix accepts the email and queues it, (3) Postfix connects to the relay (smtp.gmail.com:587), authenticates, and forwards, (4) Gmail's servers look up the MX record of the recipient's domain, (5) Gmail delivers to the recipient's mail server. Key terms: MUA (Mail User Agent — Thunderbird, Gmail web), MTA (Mail Transfer Agent — Postfix, Sendmail, Gmail's servers), MDA (Mail Delivery Agent — dovecot, places mail in user's mailbox). Postfix can be: (a) a full mail server (receives AND sends), (b) a relay (sends only, like anihpj), (c) a smarthost (forwards everything to one upstream). Anihpj uses relay mode — sends notifications but doesn't receive email. Check logs: tail -f /var/log/mail.log shows the full SMTP conversation including delivery status. SPF/DKIM/DMARC: If your emails go to spam, you need these DNS records to prove your server is authorized to send from @anihpj.com.

29.3 vsftpd — FTP Server Basics

🔴 FTP (vsftpd) — Plaintext Legacy

Username and password sent in CLEAR TEXT. Anyone on the network (WiFi, ISP, VPN) can sniff credentials. Separate port range for data (passive mode nightmare with firewalls). No encryption whatsoever.

🟢 SFTP (SSH-based) — Encrypted Modern

Runs over SSH port 22. Same encryption as your SSH connection. Same keys, same authentication. No additional daemon. Firewall-friendly (single port). Supported by every FTP client since 2005.

DirectiveRecommendedWhy
anonymous_enableNONever allow anonymous uploads
local_enableYESAllow local user accounts to login
write_enableYESAllow file uploads (if needed)
chroot_local_userYESJail users to their home directory
Active vs Passive FTP — The Firewall Nightmare Explained
  ACTIVE FTP (the OLD way — firewall unfriendly)
  ┌──────────┐                    ┌──────────┐
  │ CLIENT   │                    │ SERVER   │
  │ Port 21──┼───────────────────▶│ Port 21  │ ← Control (commands)
  │           │                    │          │
  │ Port 20◀─┼────────────────────│ Port 20  │ ← DATA (server connects BACK!)
  └──────────┘                    └──────────┘
  ⚠️  PROBLEM: Server initiates connection TO client.
      Client firewalls BLOCK this! NAT breaks it!

  PASSIVE FTP (the NEW way — firewall friendly)
  ┌──────────┐                    ┌──────────┐
  │ CLIENT   │                    │ SERVER   │
  │ Port 21──┼───────────────────▶│ Port 21  │ ← Control (commands)
  │           │  "Enter Passive"    │          │
  │ Port ? ──┼───────────────────▶│ Port 30xx│ ← DATA (client connects out)
  └──────────┘                    └──────────┘
  ✅ Client initiates BOTH connections → firewalls allow outgoing traffic

  🎯 SFTP (SSH-based, port 22) eliminates this ENTIRE problem:
      ONE port, ONE connection, FULL encryption. No active/passive drama.
⚠️ Use SFTP, Not FTP!

FTP sends usernames and passwords in plaintext over the network. Anyone on the same network can sniff credentials. SFTP (SSH File Transfer Protocol) encrypts everything and uses the same SSH key infrastructure you already have. It runs over port 22 — no additional daemon needed. Modern tools (FileZilla, WinSCP, scp, rsync) all support SFTP. sftp user@anihpj-server is the secure alternative. vsftpd is included on the LFCS exam, but in real production, always use SFTP.

🔬 /etc/aliases — Redirect System Mail to Real Addresses

Linux system daemons (cron, at, sudo) send mail to the local root user for errors and reports. Since you (hopefully) never log in as root, these messages pile up unseen. /etc/aliases lets you redirect them: root: admin@anihpj.com. After editing, run sudo newaliases to update the alias database. Other useful aliases: postmaster: root (RFC requirement), www-data: /dev/null (discard web server mail). Test: echo "test" | mail -s "Alias Test" root → should arrive at admin@anihpj.com.

📊 Chapter 29 Visual Summary

⏰
Chrony/NTPtimedatectl set-timezone & set-ntp. chronyc tracking. Accurate time = valid SSL + correct logs.
📧
PostfixSend-only relay. loopback-only. main.cf + sasl_passwd. mailq to check queue.
📁
vsftpdanonymous_enable=NO, chroot_local_user=YES. LFCS tested. Use SFTP in production!

Q5
Why is accurate system time critical for a production web server? Give three reasons.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

1. SSL/TLS certificates — browsers reject certs if clock is wrong (invalid date errors). 2. Log correlation — debugging requires matching timestamps across Nginx, Gunicorn, PostgreSQL logs. 3. Cron jobs — nightly backups at 2 AM must run at the correct time. Also: database replication, 2FA (TOTP requires ±30 sec accuracy), and Kerberos authentication.

Q6
Configure vsftpd to disallow anonymous access and jail local users to their home directories.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# /etc/vsftpd.conf:
anonymous_enable=NO
local_enable=YES
chroot_local_user=YES
# Apply: sudo systemctl reload vsftpd
Q7
Why is SFTP preferred over FTP for production file transfers?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

FTP sends credentials (username + password) in PLAINTEXT — anyone on the network can sniff them. SFTP runs over SSH, which encrypts ALL traffic including authentication. SFTP uses the same SSH keys you already manage. No additional daemon, no additional port, no additional attack surface. FTP should only be used for legacy systems that don't support SFTP.

Q8
Check if any emails are stuck in Postfix's mail queue. How do you flush the queue?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
mailq              # view queue
sudo postqueue -f   # flush (retry delivery now)
sudo postsuper -d ALL  # delete ALL queued mail (use with caution)
# Queue files are in: /var/spool/postfix/
Q9
What's the difference between chronyd and systemd-timesyncd? Which should anihpj use?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

systemd-timesyncd is a minimal SNTP client — it only queries time, doesn't adjust clock gradually (can cause jumps). Good for desktop/laptop. chronyd is a full NTP implementation — it slews the clock gradually (no jumps), supports multiple servers with error detection, and handles intermittent networks. Anihpj should use chrony for production. Install: apt install chrony. It automatically disables systemd-timesyncd.

Q10
List all available timezones containing "Africa" and set the system to use one.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
timedatectl list-timezones | grep Africa
sudo timedatectl set-timezone Africa/Lagos
# Verify: timedatectl

30.1 The Full Anihpj Production Stack

Anihpj Production Architecture — Every Component Connected
  Internet
      │ HTTPS :443

  USER'S BROWSER
      │ ① DNS lookup: anihpj.com → 203.0.113.50
      │ ② TCP connect to 203.0.113.50:443
      ▼

      │
      ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NGINX :443                                              │
  │  ③ TLS HANDSHAKE → encrypt with AES-256-GCM             │
  │  ④ Parse HTTP request: GET /jobs/ HTTP/1.1              │
  │  ⑤ /jobs/ matches location / → proxy to Gunicorn socket │
  └──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
                             │ ⑥ HTTP via UNIX socket
                             ▼

  │  GUNICORN (anihpj.service)                               │

  │  ⑨ SELECT * FROM jobpost_jobpost WHERE status='active'  │
  │  ⑩ Return 1,247 rows                                    │
  └──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DJANGO → GUNICORN → NGINX → BROWSER                    │
  │  ⑪ Django renders HTML template with 1,247 jobs         │
  │  ⑫ Gunicorn returns HTTP 200 with HTML                  │
  │  ⑬ Nginx sends encrypted response to browser            │
  │  ⑭ Browser decrypts + renders anihpj job board          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TOTAL TIME: ~50-200ms (depending on query complexity)
  SECURITY: Every step from ③-⑬ is encrypted or local-only

30.2 Step-by-Step Production Deployment Checklist

13 Steps to a Production Anihpj Server
  ☐ 1. OS INSTALL
     Ubuntu 22.04 LTS. Set hostname, timezone (Africa/Lagos),
     create admin user with sudo. apt update && apt upgrade -y.

  ☐ 2. SECURITY BASICS
     ufw allow 22/tcp && ufw allow 80/tcp && ufw allow 443/tcp && ufw enable
     sshd_config: PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no
     Install fail2ban: apt install fail2ban

  ☐ 3. CREATE ANIHPJ SERVICE USER
     sudo useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /var/www/anihpj anihpj
     # -r = system account, -s = no login shell, -d = home directory

  ☐ 4. INSTALL PYTHON & VENV
     apt install python3 python3-venv python3-pip
     python3 -m venv /var/www/anihpj/venv
     source /var/www/anihpj/venv/bin/activate
     pip install django gunicorn psycopg2-binary

  ☐ 5. CONFIGURE POSTGRESQL
     apt install postgresql postgresql-contrib
     sudo -u postgres psql
       CREATE DATABASE anihpj_db;
       CREATE USER anihpj_user WITH PASSWORD 'STRONG_RANDOM_PASSWORD';
       GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES ON DATABASE anihpj_db TO anihpj_user;
       ALTER DATABASE anihpj_db OWNER TO anihpj_user;

  ☐ 6. SET ENVIRONMENT VARIABLES
     nano /var/www/anihpj/.env  (chmod 600!)
       DEBUG=False
       SECRET_KEY=django-insecure-GENERATE_RANDOM_50_CHARS
       DATABASE_URL=postgres://anihpj_user:PASS@localhost:5432/anihpj_db
       ALLOWED_HOSTS=anihpj.com,www.anihpj.com

  ☐ 7. DEPLOY DJANGO CODE
     cd /var/www/anihpj/
     source venv/bin/activate
     python manage.py migrate
     python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
     python manage.py createsuperuser

  ☐ 8. CREATE GUNICORN SYSTEMD SERVICE
     nano /etc/systemd/system/anihpj.service
       [Unit] Description=Anihpj Gunicorn / After=network.target postgresql.service
       [Service] User=anihpj / Group=www-data
       WorkingDirectory=/var/www/anihpj
       ExecStart=/var/www/anihpj/venv/bin/gunicorn anihpj.wsgi:application \
         --workers 3 --bind unix:/run/anihpj/anihpj.sock
       Restart=on-failure / RestartSec=5s
       [Install] WantedBy=multi-user.target
     systemctl daemon-reload && systemctl enable --now anihpj

  ☐ 9. INSTALL & CONFIGURE NGINX
     apt install nginx
     Create /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj (see Ch 27.3)
     ln -s /etc/nginx/sites-available/anihpj /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/
     rm /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/default
     nginx -t && systemctl reload nginx

  ☐ 10. GET SSL CERTIFICATE
     apt install certbot python3-certbot-nginx
     certbot --nginx -d anihpj.com -d www.anihpj.com

  ☐ 11. SETUP NIGHTLY BACKUPS
     Create /usr/local/bin/anihpj-backup.sh (see Ch 28.4)
     sudo crontab -e: 0 2 * * * /usr/local/bin/anihpj-backup.sh

  ☐ 12. VERIFY EVERYTHING
     systemctl status nginx postgresql anihpj
     curl -I https://anihpj.com  → HTTP/2 200
     sudo -u postgres psql -c "SELECT 1"  → 1
     journalctl -u anihpj --no-pager | tail -20

  ☐ 13. ENABLE ALL SERVICES ON BOOT
     systemctl enable nginx postgresql anihpj fail2ban chrony
     reboot (test that everything comes back automatically)
🔧 Pro Tip: Build a "Golden Image"

After completing this checklist ONCE, take a snapshot/image of your VM. For your NEXT project (another Django app, a staging server, a client deployment), clone the image and skip steps 1-5. Docker takes this concept even further — the Dockerfile encodes ALL these steps as code (see Appendix H for Docker fundamentals).

🔬 Production Monitoring — Is Anihpj Actually Working?

Deploying is step 1. Knowing it STAYS working is the real challenge. What to monitor: (1) Uptime — curl -o /dev/null -s -w '%{http_code}' https://anihpj.com should return 200. Cron this every minute. (2) Disk space — df -h / /var/lib/postgresql /var/www/anihpj/media. Alert at 80%. (3) PostgreSQL — SELECT count(*), now() - MAX(created_at) AS latest_post FROM jobpost_jobpost;. If latest post is >1 hour old during business hours, something's wrong. (4) SSL expiry — certbot certificates or echo | openssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -enddate. (5) System load — uptime / top -bn1 | head. Tools evolution: cron + mail (LFCS level) → Monit/Nagios → Prometheus + Grafana → Datadog/New Relic. Start with cron alerts — they're free and you already know how to set them up.

🔬 Rate Limiting with Nginx — Protect Anihpj from Abuse

A single malicious user can overwhelm your job board with thousands of requests. Nginx rate limiting protects you: limit_req_zone $binary_remote_addr zone=anihpj:10m rate=10r/s; in the http block creates a shared memory zone tracking requests per IP. Then limit_req zone=anihpj burst=20 nodelay; in the location block allows 10 req/sec with a burst of 20. Beyond that: HTTP 429 "Too Many Requests." Why it matters: Without rate limiting, a simple script can scrape all your job listings, overload the database with fake applications, or brute-force login forms. Rate limiting is your first line of defense — before fail2ban, before WAF, before anything else. It's configured in Nginx with 3 lines.

🚀 Complete Bash Deployment Script — One Command to Production
  #!/bin/bash
  # deploy-anihpj.sh — Complete production deployment
  # Usage: sudo bash deploy-anihpj.sh
  set -e  # Exit on any error

  echo "=== Step 1: System Update ==="
  apt update && apt upgrade -y
  timedatectl set-timezone Africa/Lagos
  hostnamectl set-hostname anihpj-prod

  echo "=== Step 2: Create Service User ==="
  useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /var/www/anihpj anihpj

  echo "=== Step 3: Install Packages ==="
  apt install -y python3 python3-venv python3-pip nginx postgresql postgresql-contrib certbot python3-certbot-nginx fail2ban chrony

  echo "=== Step 4: Setup PostgreSQL ==="
  systemctl enable --now postgresql
  sudo -u postgres psql < /etc/systemd/system/anihpj.service <

Chapter 30 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Create a system user for the anihpj application with no login shell and home at /var/www/anihpj.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin -d /var/www/anihpj anihpj
Q2
What are the three services that must be enabled at boot for anihpj to function? Write the systemctl commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo systemctl enable nginx postgresql anihpj
# anihpj.service depends on postgresql (After=postgresql.service in unit file)
# NGINX should also be enabled but can start before Gunicorn — it just won't proxy until the socket exists
Q3
Create a systemd service unit for Gunicorn that restarts on failure after 5 seconds and runs as user "anihpj".
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
[Unit]
Description=Anihpj Gunicorn
After=network.target postgresql.service

[Service]
User=anihpj
Group=www-data
WorkingDirectory=/var/www/anihpj
ExecStart=/var/www/anihpj/venv/bin/gunicorn anihpj.wsgi:application --workers 3 --bind unix:/run/anihpj/anihpj.sock
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5s

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
Q4
Why does the anihpj system user have /usr/sbin/nologin as its shell? What's the security benefit?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Setting the shell to /usr/sbin/nologin prevents ANYONE from logging in as the anihpj user via SSH or console. Even if someone steals the anihpj user's password (which it doesn't have — it's locked), they can't get a shell. This implements least privilege: the service account can run Gunicorn (via systemd) but can never be used for interactive login. Service accounts should NEVER have real shells.

Q5
What's the purpose of "collectstatic" in Django deployment? Where do the files end up?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
# Collectstatic gathers ALL static files (CSS, JS, images) from
# all Django apps and STATICFILES_DIRS into STATIC_ROOT
# (e.g., /var/www/anihpj/staticfiles/).
# Nginx then serves these files DIRECTLY (not through Django/Gunicorn),
# which is MUCH faster — no Python overhead for static assets.
Q6
How do you verify all services start correctly after a reboot? Write the verification commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
systemctl is-active nginx postgresql anihpj  # should all return "active"
curl -I https://anihpj.com                    # should return HTTP/2 200
sudo -u postgres psql -c "SELECT 1"           # should return 1
journalctl -u anihpj --no-pager | tail -20     # check for errors
Q7
Why is the .env file permission set to 600? What's inside it that needs protection?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

chmod 600 .env ensures ONLY the owner (anihpj) can read the file. It contains SECRETS: Django SECRET_KEY (used for cryptographic signing — if leaked, attackers can forge sessions), DATABASE_URL with the PostgreSQL password, API keys, and email credentials. Anyone who can read .env can steal the database password. 600 = owner read/write only — no group, no others.

Q8
What's the minimum UFW firewall rules needed for anihpj production? Write the commands.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo ufw default deny incoming
sudo ufw default allow outgoing
sudo ufw allow 22/tcp    # SSH
sudo ufw allow 80/tcp    # HTTP (redirects to HTTPS)
sudo ufw allow 443/tcp   # HTTPS
sudo ufw enable
sudo ufw status verbose   # verify
Q9
What does the Nginx "proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For" do? Why does Django need it?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

When Nginx proxies a request to Gunicorn, Gunicorn sees the connection coming from localhost (127.0.0.1) — it doesn't know the REAL client IP. proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for passes the original client IP in a header. Django reads this header to: log real IPs in access logs, apply IP-based rate limiting, and show accurate IPs in the admin panel. Without it, all requests appear to come from 127.0.0.1.

Q10
Write a single command to check if PostgreSQL is listening on the correct interface and port.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo ss -tlnp | grep postgres
# Should show: LISTEN  127.0.0.1:5432  (localhost ONLY)
# If it shows 0.0.0.0:5432 → PostgreSQL is exposed to the internet! FIX IMMEDIATELY!

🧩 Part 8 Consolidation — The Anihpj Service Stack: SSH (Ch26) is your encrypted entry point — configure it once, harden it, and it protects every future connection. Nginx (Ch27) is the public face — it terminates SSL, serves static files at wire speed, and proxies dynamic requests to Gunicorn. PostgreSQL (Ch28) is the silent workhorse — running on localhost, serving data only to trusted local services, backed up nightly. Chrony (Ch29) keeps the clocks synchronized so SSL certs validate and logs correlate. Together, these four services form the minimum viable production stack for ANY web application — not just anihpj. Master these four, and you can deploy any Linux-based web app.

  │ BIOS password, secure boot enabled                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  🎯 KEY INSIGHT: If ONE layer fails, the NEXT still protects you.
     Defense in Depth = no single point of failure.
🔐
Layered Security

No single tool protects everything. SELinux + fail2ban + auditd + firewall = overlapping defenses that compensate for each other's gaps.

🔍
Auditability

Every access to sensitive files is logged. When a breach happens, you know exactly what was accessed and when — not guessing days later.

🚫

fail2ban doesn't wait for you — it bans attackers automatically. AIDE alerts you to changed files immediately. Security that works while you sleep.

Chapter 31: SELinux & AppArmor — Mandatory Access Control

Standard Linux permissions (DAC) let the file owner decide who accesses it. But what if a hacker compromises your anihpj web app and runs code as the www-data user? DAC can't stop them from reading everything www-data can read. Mandatory Access Control (MAC) adds a second, system-enforced security layer that even root can't bypass. This chapter covers both MAC systems tested on the LFCS: SELinux (Rocky/RHEL) and AppArmor (Ubuntu).

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~2 hours🛡️ Security

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand DAC vs MAC — why Linux permissions alone aren't enough
  • ✅ Manage SELinux modes, contexts, and troubleshooting
  • ✅ Manage AppArmor profiles, modes, and troubleshooting
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Security 10%
🎯 MAC on the LFCS Exam

The LFCS exam gives you BOTH an Ubuntu VM and a Rocky VM. On Ubuntu: use AppArmor commands. On Rocky: use SELinux commands. The #1 exam scenario: "Service X can't access files" — check the MAC system FIRST before debugging permissions. Know how to temporarily disable (for testing) and re-enable.

31.1 DAC vs MAC — Why Linux Permissions Aren't Enough

🔴 DAC — Discretionary Access Control

Owner decides who accesses. rwx bits + owner/group. The PROBLEM: if a process runs as user "anihpj", it has ALL of anihpj's permissions. A compromised Django app can read .env, SSH keys, everything anihpj owns.

🟢 MAC — Mandatory Access Control

System enforces policy BEYOND rwx. Even if you're root, the MAC policy can block you. A compromised nginx process can only access files labeled httpd_sys_content_t — your .env and SSH keys are off-limits.

🔬 Analogy: DAC vs MAC

DAC = Apartment key. If you have the key (correct rwx permissions), you can enter any room. MAC = Security guard with a clipboard. Even if you have the key, the guard checks your ID against the list. If you're not on the list for that room, you don't enter — even if you're the building owner (root). SELinux/AppArmor is that security guard. Every process and file gets a label, and the kernel enforces which labels can interact.

1
Service Fails with "Permission Denied"

Your web app or daemon can't access a file even though ls -l shows correct rwx permissions. First clue that MAC is blocking.

2
Check the MAC System

Ubuntu: sudo dmesg | grep apparmor. Rocky: sudo ausearch -m avc -ts recent. Look for "DENIED" entries with the process name.

3
Fix the Policy (Not Disable It)

Ubuntu: Edit AppArmor profile or aa-complain for testing. Rocky: restorecon -Rv /path/ or semanage fcontext. NEVER permanently disable MAC!

31.2 SELinux — Security-Enhanced Linux (Rocky/RHEL)

CommandWhat It Does
getenforceShow current mode: Enforcing / Permissive / Disabled
setenforce 0Temporarily switch to Permissive (log but don't block)
/etc/selinux/configPersistent SELinux mode setting
ls -Z /var/www/anihpj/Show SELinux context (user:role:type:level)
ps -ZShow SELinux context of running processes
restorecon -Rv /var/www/anihpj/Restore default file contexts (fixes most SELinux issues!)
semanage fcontext -a -t httpd_sys_content_t "/var/www/anihpj(/.*)?"Set persistent file context for a directory
ausearch -m avc -ts recentSearch audit log for recent SELinux denials
ausearch -c nginxSearch denials for a specific command (quick filter)
sealert -l \*Show human-readable explanations for all recent denials
audit2allow -a -M anihpj_policyGenerate SELinux policy from denial logs
🔬 SELinux Booleans — Toggle Features Without Rewriting Policy

SELinux booleans are on/off switches that modify policy behavior WITHOUT creating custom policies. getsebool -a lists all booleans. getsebool httpd_can_network_connect checks one. setsebool -P httpd_can_network_connect on enables it permanently (-P = persistent across reboots). Common booleans for web servers: httpd_can_network_connect (allow nginx/apache to make outbound connections — needed for reverse proxy), httpd_can_sendmail (allow web app to send email), httpd_enable_cgi (allow CGI scripts). Anihpj use case: If Django needs to connect to an external API (e.g., payment gateway), and SELinux blocks it: sudo setsebool -P httpd_can_network_connect on. This is safer than disabling SELinux entirely!

🔧 Real-World: "SELinux Is Blocking Nginx from Serving Anihpj"

Situation: You deployed anihpj code to /var/www/anihpj. Nginx returns 403 Forbidden for all requests.

  1. Check SELinux: getenforce → Enforcing. ausearch -m avc -ts recent | grep nginx → "denied { read } for pid=1234 comm="nginx" name="index.html" dev="sda1" ino=45678 scontext=system_u:system_r:httpd_t:s0 tcontext=unconfined_u:object_r:default_t:s0"
  2. Diagnosis: Files have context default_t (wrong!). Nginx needs httpd_sys_content_t.
  3. Fix: sudo semanage fcontext -a -t httpd_sys_content_t "/var/www/anihpj(/.*)?" && sudo restorecon -Rv /var/www/anihpj/
  4. Verify: ls -Z /var/www/anihpj/ → httpd_sys_content_t. Nginx now serves files!

Key lesson: 90% of SELinux problems are wrong file contexts. restorecon fixes most of them.

31.3 AppArmor — Application Armor (Ubuntu)

CommandWhat It Does
aa-statusShow AppArmor status: loaded profiles, modes, processes in complain
/etc/apparmor.d/Profile directory — one profile per application
aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginxEnforce a profile (block violations)
aa-complain /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginxComplain mode (log but don't block — for debugging)
aa-disable /etc/apparmor.d/usr.sbin.nginxDisable a profile entirely
dmesg | grep apparmorView AppArmor denial messages in kernel log
apparmor_parser -r /etc/apparmor.d/profileReload a profile after editing
🔧 Real-World: "AppArmor Blocks Gunicorn from Writing Logs"

Situation: Gunicorn can't write to /var/log/anihpj/. dmesg | grep apparmor shows: apparmor="DENIED" operation="mknod" profile="/usr/bin/gunicorn" name="/var/log/anihpj/gunicorn-error.log".

  1. Diagnose: The gunicorn AppArmor profile doesn't allow writing to /var/log/anihpj/.
  2. Fix (temporary): sudo aa-complain /etc/apparmor.d/usr.bin.gunicorn — puts profile in complain mode.
  3. Fix (permanent): Edit /etc/apparmor.d/local/usr.bin.gunicorn and add: /var/log/anihpj/* rw,. Reload: sudo apparmor_parser -r /etc/apparmor.d/usr.bin.gunicorn. Set back to enforce: sudo aa-enforce /etc/apparmor.d/usr.bin.gunicorn.
🔬 Inside an AppArmor Profile — What the Rules Actually Look Like

AppArmor profiles live in /etc/apparmor.d/ and are named after the executable path (slashes become dots: usr.sbin.nginx). Each profile declares what the program CAN do — everything else is denied. Key sections: #include <tunables/global> (variables like @{HOME}), capability net_bind_service, (Linux capabilities), /var/www/anihpj/staticfiles/** r, (file access: r=read, w=write, m=memory-map, k=lock), network inet tcp, (allow TCP networking). Local overrides: /etc/apparmor.d/local/usr.sbin.nginx — add custom rules here (not in main profile) so package updates don't overwrite them. Syntax: glob patterns use * (one directory level) and ** (recursive). deny /etc/shadow r, explicitly blocks access even if a broader rule would allow it. Debugging: aa-logprof scans logs for denials and interactively suggests profile updates — the easiest way to fix a broken profile!

SELinux vs AppArmor — LFCS Comparison (Both Are Tested!)
FeatureSELinuxAppArmor
Used byRHEL, Rocky, FedoraUbuntu, Debian
LFCS distroRocky Linux 9 VMUbuntu 22.04 VM
ApproachLabel-based — every FILE & PROCESS has a context (user:role:type:level)Path-based — profiles attached to executable paths
Check statusgetenforceaa-status
ModesEnforcing / Permissive / DisabledEnforce / Complain / Disabled
Temp disablesetenforce 0aa-complain /path/to/profile
Troubleshootausearch -m avcdmesg | grep apparmor

Q9
What's the fundamental difference between SELinux and AppArmor? Which distro uses which?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

SELinux: Label-based — every file and process has a security context (user:role:type:level). Policies define which contexts can interact. Used by RHEL/Rocky/Fedora. AppArmor: Path-based — profiles attached to executable file paths. Simpler syntax. Used by Ubuntu/Debian. On the LFCS exam: Ubuntu VM = AppArmor, Rocky VM = SELinux.

Q10
A service works when SELinux is in permissive mode but fails in enforcing mode. What's the proper fix (not just disabling SELinux)?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# 1. Check denials:
sudo ausearch -m avc -ts recent
# 2. Set correct file context:
sudo semanage fcontext -a -t CORRECT_TYPE "/path(/.*)?"
sudo restorecon -Rv /path/
# 3. OR generate policy from denials:
sudo audit2allow -a -M myapp_policy
sudo semodule -i myapp_policy.pp
# NEVER: setenforce 0 permanently or SELINUX=disabled in config.
# Always fix the policy — disabling SELinux removes a critical security layer.

Chapter 32: System Hardening — Defense in Depth

Security isn't one tool — it's layers. This chapter covers the practical hardening techniques every anihpj server needs: least privilege (giving users/services only what they need), auditd (tracking who accessed what), fail2ban (automatic IP banning), AIDE (detecting changed files), and a comprehensive hardening checklist you can apply to any production server.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔒 Security

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Apply the principle of least privilege to users, services, and networks
  • ✅ Set up auditd to track access to sensitive files
  • ✅ Configure fail2ban to block brute-force attacks
  • ✅ Use AIDE to detect unauthorized file changes
  • ✅ Follow a 30-point hardening checklist for production
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Security 10%
🎯 Hardening on the LFCS Exam

The exam may ask you to configure fail2ban, set up audit rules, or implement least privilege. Key commands: auditctl -w (watch files), fail2ban-client (manage jails), find / -perm -4000 (find SUID binaries). Know the philosophy: every open port, every running service, every user with a shell is an attack surface.

32.1 The Principle of Least Privilege

Least Privilege Applied to Anihpj
  PRINCIPLE: Every entity should have the MINIMUM permissions
  needed to do its job — and NO MORE.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ANIHPJ USER (anihpj)                                       │
  │  ✅ Can: run Gunicorn, read .env, write to /var/log/anihpj │
  │  ❌ Cannot: sudo, SSH in (nologin shell), read other homes  │
  │  Implementation: useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DATABASE USER (anihpj_user)                                │
  │  ✅ Can: SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on anihpj_db           │
  │  ❌ Cannot: CREATE DATABASE, DROP DATABASE, see other DBs   │
  │  Implementation: GRANT ALL ON DATABASE anihpj_db (only!)    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NGINX WORKERS (www-data)                                   │
  │  ✅ Can: read /var/www/anihpj/static/, connect to socket    │
  │  ❌ Cannot: read .env, write to code, execute binaries      │
  │  Implementation: chown anihpj:www-data; chmod 750 static/   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NETWORK                                                   │
  │  ✅ Open: 22 (SSH), 80 (HTTP), 443 (HTTPS)                 │
  │  ❌ Closed: 5432 (PostgreSQL), 8000 (Gunicorn), 3306 (MySQL)│
  │  Implementation: ufw default deny; ufw allow 22,80,443     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
1
Service Accounts

Create with useradd -r -s /usr/sbin/nologin. No interactive login possible. Each service gets its own user — never share accounts.

2
File Permissions

.env = 600. Static files = 644. Application code = 640 for owner:group. find / -perm -o+w -type f to audit world-writable files.

3
sudo Control

Grant specific commands: deploy ALL=(root) /usr/bin/systemctl reload nginx — NOT ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: ALL. Audit sudo usage: journalctl -xe | grep sudo.

4
Network Restriction

UFW default deny incoming. Open only needed ports. PostgreSQL on localhost. Internal services on private IPs only. Audit: ss -tlnp weekly.

32.2 auditd — Auditing File Access

CommandWhat It Does
apt install auditdInstall the audit daemon
auditctl -w /etc/passwd -p rwxa -k passwd_changesWatch /etc/passwd for read/write/execute/attr changes
auditctl -w /etc/shadow -p rwa -k shadow_accessWatch /etc/shadow for any access
auditctl -lList all active audit rules
ausearch -f /etc/shadowSearch audit log for /etc/shadow access
ausearch -k passwd_changes -iSearch by key name, interpret timestamps/UIDs
aureport --summarySummary report of all audit events
augenrules --loadLoad rules from /etc/audit/rules.d/ (persistent)
🔬 Make Audit Rules Persistent

Rules added with auditctl -w are LOST on reboot. For persistent rules, add them to /etc/audit/rules.d/audit.rules: -w /etc/passwd -p rwxa -k passwd_changes. Then sudo augenrules --load && sudo systemctl restart auditd. Audit logs are at /var/log/audit/audit.log — search with ausearch, not grep (the log is binary-optimized).

🔬 auditd Rule Syntax Deep-Dive

Audit rules use flags to specify what to monitor. File watches (-w): -p rwxa = watch for (r)ead, (w)rite, e(x)ecute, (a)ttribute changes. Syscall rules (-a): -a always,exit -S unlink -S rename -k file_deletion logs every file deletion and rename. Key (-k): Tags all events from a rule with a searchable key — ausearch -k passwd_changes finds them all. Performance: Too many rules can slow the system. Be selective: watch /etc/, /var/www/anihpj/, ~/.ssh/authorized_keys — not /var/log/ (excessive noise). View loaded rules: auditctl -l. Delete a rule: auditctl -W /etc/passwd -p rwxa (same syntax but uppercase -W). Anihpj example: -w /var/www/anihpj/ -p wa -k anihpj_code_changes — alerts on any code modification.

32.3 fail2ban — Automatic IP Banning

How fail2ban Protects Anihpj
  ATTACKER                          ANIHPJ SERVER
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ① SSH attempt (wrong password)
     ─────────────────────────────────▶  /var/log/auth.log:
                                          "Failed password for root"

  ② SSH attempt (wrong password)
     ─────────────────────────────────▶  Failed again (2nd attempt)

  ③ SSH attempt (wrong password)
     ─────────────────────────────────▶  Failed again (3rd attempt)

  ④ SSH attempt (wrong password)
     ─────────────────────────────────▶  Failed again (4th attempt)

  ⑤ SSH attempt (wrong password)
     ─────────────────────────────────▶  Failed again (5th attempt!)
                                          🔴 fail2ban TRIGGERS:
                                          iptables -A INPUT -s IP -j DROP

  ⑥ SSH attempt (any password)
     ──────── ✗ BLOCKED ✗ ─────────────  iptables DROPS connection
                                          (Banned for 1 hour)

  CONFIG (/etc/fail2ban/jail.local):
  [sshd]
  enabled = true
  port = 22
  maxretry = 5
  bantime = 3600        # 1 hour
  findtime = 600        # 10 minute window for counting failures

  CHECK STATUS:
  sudo fail2ban-client status sshd     → list banned IPs
  sudo fail2ban-client set sshd unbanip 1.2.3.4  → manual unban
🔬 fail2ban for Nginx — Protect Your Web App Too

SSH isn't the only attack vector. fail2ban can protect Nginx against: (1) 4xx floods: Ban IPs generating excessive 404/403 errors (vulnerability scanners). (2) Login brute-force: Django admin or WordPress wp-login.php attacks. (3) Rate-limit abuse: IPs hitting your API too fast. Setup: Create /etc/fail2ban/filter.d/nginx-4xx.conf with regex matching 4xx errors in access.log. Create /etc/fail2ban/jail.local entry: [nginx-4xx]
enabled = true
port = http,https
filter = nginx-4xx
logpath = /var/log/nginx/anihpj-access.log
maxretry = 100
findtime = 300
bantime = 3600. This bans IPs generating 100+ 4xx errors in 5 minutes. Check: sudo fail2ban-client status nginx-4xx.

32.4 AIDE — File Integrity Monitoring

CommandWhat It Does
apt install aideInstall AIDE
aideinitInitialize AIDE database (baseline snapshot of system files)
aide --checkCompare current state against baseline — report changes
aide --updateUpdate baseline after legitimate changes (apt upgrade, deploy)
/etc/aide/aide.confConfig: which directories to monitor, what attributes to check
🔬 What AIDE Detects

AIDE creates a cryptographic hash of every monitored file. On each check, it recomputes hashes and compares. It detects: modified files (hacker changed your code), new files (hacker uploaded a backdoor), deleted files (hacker removed logs), changed permissions (hacker made a file SUID root). Anihpj use case: Monitor /var/www/anihpj/, /etc/nginx/, /etc/postgresql/, /etc/ssh/sshd_config, /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow. Run aide --check daily via cron and email the diff. If source code changes unexpectedly — investigate immediately.

32.5 Hardening Checklist

🔐
SSH Hardening

PermitRootLogin no, PasswordAuthentication no, AllowUsers whitelist, non-default port. sshd -t before reload.

🛡️
Firewall

UFW default deny incoming. Only 22, 80, 443 open. PostgreSQL on localhost only. Verify weekly with ufw status verbose.

👤
User Security

Service accounts: nologin shell. .env: chmod 600. sudo: specific commands only. umask 027 for services.

📦
Updates & Patches

unattended-upgrades for security patches. Weekly apt update. Remove unused services. Kernel hardening via sysctl.

📋
Auditing

auditd watching /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow. AIDE daily integrity checks. fail2ban active on SSH and Nginx.

💾
Backups & Recovery

Nightly pg_dump. GPG encrypted offsite copy. Tested restore procedure. 3-2-1 backup rule enforced.

Complete 30-Point Production Hardening Checklist
  ☐  1. SSH: PermitRootLogin no
  ☐  2. SSH: PasswordAuthentication no (key-only)
  ☐  3. SSH: AllowUsers whitelist
  ☐  4. SSH: non-default port (>1024 reduces scan noise)
  ☐  5. UFW: default deny incoming, allow 22/80/443
  ☐  6. PostgreSQL: listen_addresses = 'localhost'
  ☐  7. PostgreSQL: strong password for anihpj_user
  ☐  8. anihpj user: nologin shell, no sudo
  ☐  9. .env file: chmod 600, NOT in Git
  ☐ 10. Django: DEBUG=False in production
  ☐ 11. Django: ALLOWED_HOSTS restricted
  ☐ 12. Django: SECRET_KEY not in settings.py (use env var)
  ☐ 13. unattended-upgrades: auto-install security patches
  ☐ 14. fail2ban: active on SSH (maxretry=5, bantime=1h)
  ☐ 15. fail2ban: active on Nginx (rate-limit abuse)
  ☐ 16. AppArmor: enforce profiles on nginx, gunicorn
  ☐ 17. auditd: watch /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow, /etc/sudoers
  ☐ 18. AIDE: monitor /var/www/anihpj/, key configs
  ☐ 19. File permissions: find / -perm -4000 (audit SUID)
  ☐ 20. No default passwords: change all vendor defaults
  ☐ 21. Chrony: time synced (SSL certs need correct time)
  ☐ 22. logrotate: logs rotated (prevent disk full)
  ☐ 23. /tmp mounted with noexec,nosuid,nodev
  ☐ 24. Remove unused services: telnet, rsh, ftp, nfs if not needed
  ☐ 25. Kernel: sysctl net.ipv4.tcp_syncookies=1 (SYN flood protection)
  ☐ 26. Kernel: sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward=0 (unless router)
  ☐ 27. SSL: certbot auto-renewal verified
  ☐ 28. Backups: nightly, tested restore, offsite copy
  ☐ 29. Monitoring: disk space, CPU, memory alerts
  ☐ 30. Documentation: server build log, config backups in Git

Q7
Find all SUID binaries on the system. Why are these a security concern?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
sudo find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null
# SUID binaries run with the OWNER's permissions (often root).
# If a SUID binary has a vulnerability, attackers can exploit
# it to escalate to root. Audit this list regularly — remove
# SUID from any binary that doesn't absolutely need it.
Q8
What's the principle of least privilege? Give anihpj-specific examples.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Every entity gets the MINIMUM permissions needed. Anihpj examples: (1) anihpj Linux user has nologin shell — can run Gunicorn via systemd but can never SSH in. (2) anihpj_user PostgreSQL role only has privileges on anihpj_db — can't see other databases. (3) UFW only opens ports 22, 80, 443 — PostgreSQL (5432) stays on localhost. (4) www-data can read static files but can't read .env or source code.

Q9
How do you make auditd rules persistent across reboots?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Add rules to /etc/audit/rules.d/audit.rules:
-w /etc/passwd -p wa -k passwd_changes
-w /etc/shadow -p rwa -k shadow_access
# Then load them:
sudo augenrules --load
sudo systemctl restart auditd
Q10
List 5 unused services that should be removed/disabled on a production web server. Why?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Remove/disable if not needed:
sudo systemctl disable --now telnet    # plaintext remote access
sudo systemctl disable --now rsh       # legacy remote shell
sudo systemctl disable --now ftp       # plaintext file transfer
sudo systemctl disable --now nfs-server # network file system
sudo systemctl disable --now cups      # printing (on a web server?!)

# Why: Every running service is an attack surface.
# Each has open ports, potential vulnerabilities, and
# consumes resources. If you don't need it, disable it.

Chapter 33: Certificates, Keys & Encryption

Encryption is the math that keeps anihpj user data safe. This chapter dives into the two types of encryption (symmetric vs asymmetric), practical OpenSSL commands for working with certificates, GPG for encrypting backups before sending them offsite, and an SSH keys deep-dive covering certificate-based authentication for teams.

🔴 Advanced⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔐 Crypto

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand symmetric vs asymmetric encryption — when to use each
  • ✅ Generate, inspect, and verify certificates with OpenSSL
  • ✅ Encrypt files and sign code with GPG
  • ✅ Master SSH key types, agents, and certificate-based auth
🎓 LFCS Relevance
Security 10%
🎯 Crypto on the LFCS Exam

LFCS may ask you to generate a self-signed certificate with OpenSSL, check cert expiry, or use GPG to encrypt a file. Key commands: openssl req -x509, openssl x509 -enddate, gpg -e, gpg -d. Know the difference between .pem (text), .der (binary), .p12 (PKCS#12 bundle with private key).

33.1 Symmetric vs Asymmetric Encryption

🔑 Symmetric (AES)

ONE key for both encrypt AND decrypt. Like a physical key that locks AND unlocks. Fast — good for bulk data. Problem: how do you share the key securely? Used in HTTPS for the actual data transfer (AES-256-GCM).

🔐 Asymmetric (RSA/Ed25519)

TWO keys: public (encrypt) and private (decrypt). Like a mailbox — anyone can drop mail in (public key), only you can read it (private key). Slower but solves key distribution. Used in HTTPS for the initial key exchange.

Hybrid Encryption — How HTTPS Actually Works
  BROWSER                                          SERVER
  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ① Browser gets server's PUBLIC KEY (from certificate)

  ② Browser generates random SESSION KEY (symmetric)
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  🔑 Session Key: a8f3b2c1... (256bit)│
     └──────────────────────────────────────┘

  ③ Browser ENCRYPTS session key with server's PUBLIC KEY
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  🔒 Encrypted Session Key            │
     │  (can only be decrypted with          │
     │   server's PRIVATE key)              │
     └──────────────────────────────────────┘
     ────────────────────────────────────▶  Sent to server

  ④ Server decrypts session key with PRIVATE KEY
     Now BOTH sides have the same session key!

  ⑤ 🔒 ALL SUBSEQUENT DATA encrypted with AES-256 + session key
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  GET /jobs/  →  AES-256 encrypt  →   │
     │  [encrypted bytes]  ──────────────▶  │
     │              ←  AES-256 decrypt  ←   │
     │                 [encrypted bytes]    │
     └──────────────────────────────────────┘

  💡 Asymmetric (slow) used ONCE for key exchange.
     Symmetric (fast) used for EVERYTHING after.
     Best of both worlds: security + performance.

33.2 OpenSSL — Working with Certificates

CommandWhat It Does
openssl genrsa -out anihpj.key 4096Generate 4096-bit RSA private key
openssl req -x509 -new -key anihpj.key -days 365 -out anihpj.crtGenerate self-signed cert (dev/testing only!)
openssl x509 -in anihpj.crt -text -nooutView all certificate details
openssl x509 -in anihpj.crt -enddate -nooutCheck certificate expiry date only
openssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443 -servername anihpj.comTest TLS connection (shows full chain and cipher)
openssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -datesCheck remote cert expiry (one-liner)
openssl req -new -key anihpj.key -out anihpj.csrGenerate CSR (Certificate Signing Request)
🔧 Real-World: Self-Signed Cert for Anihpj Dev Server

Situation: Internal staging server at staging.anihpj.local needs HTTPS but can't get Let's Encrypt (no public DNS).

  1. Generate key + cert: openssl req -x509 -newkey rsa:4096 -keyout staging.key -out staging.crt -days 365 -nodes -subj "/CN=staging.anihpj.local"
  2. Configure Nginx: point ssl_certificate and ssl_certificate_key to the files.
  3. Trust on dev machines: Add staging.crt to browser/system trust store. Or just click through the browser warning — it's only dev.
  4. Verify: openssl s_client -connect staging.anihpj.local:443 → shows your self-signed cert.

33.3 GPG — Encrypting Files and Emails

CommandWhat It Does
gpg --gen-keyGenerate GPG key pair (interactive)
gpg --list-keysList your public keys
gpg -e -r admin@anihpj.com backup.sqlEncrypt file for specific recipient (creates backup.sql.gpg)
gpg -d backup.sql.gpg > backup.sqlDecrypt file (prompts for your private key passphrase)
gpg --sign anihpj-v1.0.tar.gzSign a file (proves it came from YOU, not tampered with)
gpg --verify anihpj-v1.0.tar.gz.sigVerify a signature
🔬 Use Case: Encrypt Anihpj Backups Before Offsite Transfer

You're sending nightly database dumps to an offsite NAS or S3 bucket. If that storage is compromised, your raw SQL dumps expose ALL user data. Encrypt before transfer: pg_dump anihpj_db | gzip | gpg -e -r backup@anihpj.com > /backups/anihpj-$(date +%F).sql.gz.gpg. Now even if someone steals the backup file, they can't read it without the backup@anihpj.com private key and passphrase. The private key lives ONLY on a secure admin machine — never on the server itself.

33.4 SSH Keys — Deep Dive

Key TypeSizeSecurityRecommendation
Ed25519256-bitExcellent (128-bit equivalent)⭐ USE THIS — modern, fast, compact
RSA2048-4096 bitGood (3072+ recommended)Use only for legacy compatibility
ECDSA256/384/521 bitGood but NIST curvesUse Ed25519 instead
DSA1024 bitDEPRECATEDNEVER use — not supported since OpenSSH 7.0
SSH Key Workflow — From Generation to Authentication
  YOUR LAPTOP                              ANIHPJ SERVER
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ① ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "anihpj-deploy"
     Creates: ~/.ssh/id_ed25519       (PRIVATE — never share!)
              ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub   (PUBLIC — safe to share)

  ② ssh-copy-id deploy@anihpj-server
     Copies id_ed25519.pub → server's ~/.ssh/authorized_keys

  ③ ssh deploy@anihpj-server
     ─────────────────────────────────────▶
     "I want to authenticate as 'deploy'"

     ◀───────────────────────────────────
     "Prove it. Here's a random challenge."

     ─────────────────────────────────────▶
     "I signed the challenge with my
      private key. Here's the signature."

     ◀───────────────────────────────────
     Server checks: signature + public key
     in authorized_keys → MATCH! ✅
     "Welcome, deploy!"

  CERTS (for teams): Instead of copying individual keys to every
  server, sign user keys with a CA: ssh-keygen -s ca_key -I user_id
  -n deploy -V +52w user_key.pub. Servers trust the CA — any key
  signed by the CA can log in. Expiry is enforced. Revoke by
  listing in revoked_keys file. No more authorized_keys management!
🔬 SSH Agent — Store Passphrases So You Don't Type Them 100 Times

If your SSH private key has a passphrase (and it should!), you'd normally type it on every connection. ssh-agent solves this: it runs in the background, holds your decrypted keys in memory, and provides them to SSH clients on demand. Workflow: (1) Start agent: eval $(ssh-agent) — sets environment variables so SSH knows where the agent socket is. (2) Add key: ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 — you type the passphrase ONCE. (3) Now ssh anihpj-prod works without prompting. Commands: ssh-add -l lists loaded keys. ssh-add -D removes all keys (logout cleanup). Security: ForwardAgent no in ~/.ssh/config prevents remote servers from using your agent (if the remote server is compromised, attacker can authenticate as you to other servers). Modern alternative: macOS Keychain and GNOME Keyring integrate with ssh-agent automatically. On Linux: ssh-add -c requires confirmation for each use.

1
Generate Private Key

openssl genrsa -out anihpj.key 4096 — Creates a 4096-bit RSA private key. This is the MOST sensitive file — protect it with chmod 600.

2
Create CSR (Certificate Signing Request)

openssl req -new -key anihpj.key -out anihpj.csr — CSR contains your public key + identity info. Send this to a CA (or certbot).

3
Get Certificate Signed

Production: certbot sends CSR to Let's Encrypt, returns signed cert. Dev: openssl req -x509 self-signs. Cert proves your identity to browsers.

4
Install & Verify

Install cert in Nginx. Test: openssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443. Monitor expiry: openssl x509 -enddate. Auto-renew with certbot.

📊 Chapter 33 Visual Summary

🔑
SymmetricAES — one key, fast, bulk data. Used for HTTPS data transfer.
🔐
AsymmetricRSA/Ed25519 — two keys, slow, key exchange. Used for HTTPS handshake.
📜
OpenSSLgenrsa, req -x509, s_client, x509 -enddate. The Swiss Army knife of crypto.
🔏
GPGgpg -e to encrypt, gpg -d to decrypt. Encrypt backups before offsite transfer.

Chapter 33 — LFCS Practice Questions

Q1
Generate a 4096-bit RSA private key and a self-signed certificate valid for 365 days for anihpj.com.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
openssl genrsa -out anihpj.key 4096
openssl req -x509 -new -key anihpj.key -days 365 -out anihpj.crt -subj "/CN=anihpj.com"
Q2
Check when the SSL certificate for anihpj.com expires. Write the command.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
echo | openssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443 -servername anihpj.com 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -enddate
Q3
What's the difference between symmetric and asymmetric encryption? When is each used in HTTPS?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Symmetric (AES): ONE key encrypts and decrypts. FAST. Used for the actual data transfer in HTTPS — the session key encrypts all HTTP traffic. Asymmetric (RSA/Ed25519): TWO keys — public encrypts, private decrypts. SLOW but solves key distribution. Used ONCE in HTTPS: the browser encrypts the random session key with the server's public key, and the server decrypts it with its private key. The session key is then used for symmetric encryption of all subsequent data.

Q4
Encrypt the file backup.sql with GPG for recipient admin@anihpj.com.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
gpg -e -r admin@anihpj.com backup.sql
# Creates backup.sql.gpg
Q5
Decrypt backup.sql.gpg and save the output to backup.sql.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
gpg -d backup.sql.gpg > backup.sql
Q6
Generate an Ed25519 SSH key specifically for deploying anihpj code.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "anihpj-deploy-key"
Q7
View all details of a certificate file anihpj.crt using OpenSSL.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
openssl x509 -in anihpj.crt -text -noout
Q8
Which SSH key types are recommended and which are deprecated? Why?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Recommended: Ed25519 (fast, compact, secure, modern). Acceptable: RSA 3072+ bit (compatible but slower, larger keys). Deprecated: DSA (removed in OpenSSH 7.0), RSA <2048 bit (brute-forceable). Avoid: ECDSA (uses NIST curves with potential backdoor concerns — Ed25519 is strictly better). Always generate: ssh-keygen -t ed25519.

Q9
How does SSH certificate-based authentication differ from regular key authentication?
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer

Regular keys: Each user's public key must be copied to EVERY server's authorized_keys file. When someone leaves, you must remove their key from ALL servers. Certificate-based: A Certificate Authority (CA) signs user keys. Servers trust the CA, not individual keys. Benefits: (1) Users can have expiring certs (no cleanup needed), (2) Revoke by listing in revoked_keys — one place, (3) No more copying keys — sign once, access all servers. Generate CA: ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -f ca_key. Sign user key: ssh-keygen -s ca_key -I user@anihpj -n deploy -V +52w user_key.pub.

Q10
Create a one-liner to check if anihpj.com's SSL certificate expires within 30 days.
Click to reveal answer
✅ Answer
# Check expiry date:
EXPIRY=$(echo | openssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443 -servername anihpj.com 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -enddate | cut -d= -f2)
# Convert to epoch and compare (30 days = 2592000 seconds):
EXPIRY_EPOCH=$(date -d "$EXPIRY" +%s)
NOW_EPOCH=$(date +%s)
if [ $((EXPIRY_EPOCH - NOW_EPOCH)) -lt 2592000 ]; then
    echo "⚠️  Certificate expires in less than 30 days!"
fi

Morning Audit

🚫 8:15
Check Bans
Ch 32: fail2ban

fail2ban-client status sshd → 3 IPs banned overnight. Normal bot activity. No action needed.

🏷️ 9:00
Deploy New Code
Ch 31: SELinux Contexts

New static files deployed. restorecon -Rv /var/www/anihpj/staticfiles/ ensures correct SELinux contexts.

📜 10:00
Check SSL Certs
Ch 33: OpenSSL

openssl s_client -connect anihpj.com:443 | openssl x509 -noout -enddate → 45 days left. No renewal needed.

🔏 13:00
Offsite Backup
Ch 33: GPG

pg_dump | gpg -e -r backup@anihpj.com > backup.sql.gpg — encrypted before leaving the server.

✅ 16:00
Verify Everything
Ch 31-33: All Tools

getenforce, aa-status, fail2ban-client status, aide --check — all green. Security day complete.

🧩 Part 9 Consolidation — Defense in Depth: SELinux/AppArmor (Ch31) is your last line of defense — even if an attacker compromises the application, MAC policies limit the damage. auditd and AIDE (Ch32) are your surveillance cameras — they record who accessed what and alert you when files change. fail2ban (Ch32) is your automated bouncer — too many failed attempts and you're banned. OpenSSL and GPG (Ch33) secure your data in transit and at rest — certs for HTTPS, encryption for backups. Security isn't a product — it's a process. These tools are how you implement that process.
